TAB KEY
1. Is it a building or a designed built whole (a garden, a complex), placed by its style? → K Architecture. Ornament carved, painted or tiled on a building and studied where it stands is asked next (2); loose architectural fragments in a museum go by their material (D, E, I).
2. Is it made on or into a fixed surface and studied where it stands: rock art, wall painting, in-situ mosaic and tile revetment, a geoglyph, or art on the living body? → L Art in Place.
3. Is it a photograph or made with a camera? → J Photography. Is it time-based, installed or performed (video, installation, performance, digital)? → M New Media, Installation & Performance.
4. Is the written or decorated book the work: calligraphy, illumination (ornament), binding, paper decoration, or a manuscript tradition studied as the book? → C Book Arts & Calligraphy. Pictures in a manuscript belong to a painting tradition and go on to 6 (precedence P1).
5. Is it a drawing made as an end in itself or as a design, or a print pulled from a matrix? → B Drawings & Prints.
6. Is it a picture made with paint or ink on a movable flat support (paper, vellum, panel, canvas, cloth, silk)? → A Painting.
7. Is it carved, modelled or cast as an image or as carving for its own sake: statues, reliefs, carved stucco, ivory, bone, jade, rock crystal and hardstone, masks and puppets? → D Sculpture & Carving. A vessel carved from rock crystal or jade stays here; a cast metal vessel is G.
8. Otherwise place it by its material: fired clay → E Ceramics; glass → F Glass; metal (vessels, instruments, arms, jewellery) → G Metalwork, Arms & Jewellery; fibre (carpets, woven, embroidered, dyed and felted textiles, dress) → H Textiles, Carpets & Dress; wood, lacquer and papier-mâché, furniture → I Woodwork, Lacquer & Furniture.
Inside a family, run the family's own key (on each family card), then choose the leaf whose definition fits; a leaf's kinds are its named sub-schools, centres, workshops, sub-types and makers.

## A Painting — test: a picture made by laying paint or ink on a movable flat support — paper, vellum, panel, canvas, cloth or silk. Wall painting fixed to a building is L.
  key: 1. Small-format opaque watercolour or ink on paper or vellum, made for a book or an album? → A1.
  key: 2. Ink and colour with the brush on silk or paper in the East Asian formats (hanging scroll, handscroll, screen, album)? → A2.
  key: 3. A devotional image on a wooden panel in the icon tradition? → A3. A painting on cloth made to be hung or carried (thangka, pata, phad)? → A4.
  key: 4. Oil, tempera or acrylic on canvas or panel in the easel tradition and its modern and contemporary lineages? → A5.
  key: 5. A folk or popular painting named as its own tradition (reverse glass, coffeehouse painting, bark painting)? → A6.
### A1 Manuscript & Album Painting — Axis: the painting tradition (a court and school lineage). Small-format pictures in opaque watercolour or ink on paper or vellum, made to illustrate a book or to be mounted in an album (miniature painting).
A1-1 Arab Painting: manuscript painting of the Arab lands from the 12th to the 15th century: illustrated scientific texts, the Maqamat of al-Hariri and Kalila wa Dimna, made in Baghdad, Mosul, Syria and Mamluk Egypt (al-Wasiti's 1237 Maqamat). [aka Arabic miniature; Baghdad school; Mesopotamian school]
A1-2 Persian Miniature: Persian manuscript and album painting from the Mongol conquest to the 19th century, taught in court workshops whose schools are named after their centres (Herat, Tabriz, Qazvin, Isfahan, Shiraz, Bukhara); kinds are those schools and their masters (Bihzad, Sultan Muhammad, Riza-yi 'Abbasi). [aka Persian painting; Iranian miniature; negārgari]
A1-3 Ottoman Miniature: painting of the Ottoman court workshop (nakkaşhane) for histories, festival books and albums, from Mehmed II to the 18th century (Matrakçı Nasuh's topographies, Nakkaş Osman's Hünername, Levni's Surname-i Vehbi). [aka Turkish miniature; nakış]
A1-4 Mughal Painting: painting of the Mughal court atelier and its offshoots in South Asia, 16th–19th century: the Hamzanama and the illustrated histories of Akbar, Jahangir's portraits and natural history, and the sub-imperial and provincial schools (Lucknow, Murshidabad). [aka Mughal miniature; Indo-Persian painting]
A1-5 Deccan Painting: painting of the Deccan sultanates (Ahmadnagar, Bijapur, Golconda) and of Hyderabad after them, 16th–19th century, with its lyrical colour and Persian, Turkman and Vijayanagara sources. [aka Deccani painting; Deccani miniature]
A1-6 Company Painting: paintings made by Indian artists for officials of the East India Company and other Europeans, 18th–19th century, in a hybrid of Mughal technique and European natural history and topography (Delhi, Lucknow, Patna, Murshidabad, Tanjore). [aka Company school; Company style]
A1-7 Rajput Painting: Court painting of the Rajput kingdoms of Rajasthan and Central India, 16th–19th century, in schools named for their courts: Mewar Ragamala sets, Bundi and Kota hunting scenes, Kishangarh portraits of Radha, Jodhpur durbar paintings. [aka Rajasthani painting;Rajasthani miniature]
A1-8 Pahari Painting: Painting of the Hindu courts of the Punjab Hills, 17th–19th century, from the bold Basohli Rasamanjari to the lyrical Guler and Kangra Gita Govinda and Bhagavata Purana series of the family of Pandit Seu. [aka Pahari miniature;Punjab Hills painting]
A1-9 Jain Manuscript Painting: The Western Indian style of manuscript painting on palm leaf and then paper (11th–16th century), chiefly Jain scriptures with angular figures and projecting far eyes: Kalpasutra, Kalakacharyakatha, painted wooden covers (patli). [aka Western Indian style;Jain painting;Western Indian painting]
A1-10 Sikh Painting: Painting in the Punjab under Sikh patronage, late 18th–19th century, in Pahari and Company-influenced idioms: portraits of Ranjit Singh and his court, Janamsakhi illustrations, Gurus series. [aka Sikh school]
A1-11 Assamese Manuscript Painting: Manuscript painting of Assam on sanchipat (agarwood-bark) folios, 16th–19th century, in sattriya and Ahom court styles: Hastividyarnava, illustrated Bhagavata. [aka Sanchipat painting;Painting of Assam]
A1-12 Burmese Parabaik Painting: Painting in watercolour on white parabaik (folding books of mulberry paper) by the royal and monastic painters of Konbaung Burma, 18th–19th century: court ceremonies and processions, Life of the Buddha, Jataka cycles, cosmologies. [aka Burmese court painting;white parabaik]
A1-13 Thai Manuscript Painting: Paintings in gouache and gold on khoi-paper folding books of Siam, late 17th–19th century, chiefly funerary chanting books: Phra Malai manuscripts, Traiphum cosmologies, Ten Great Jatakas. [aka samut khoi painting;Phra Malai manuscript painting]
A1-14 Nara-ehon: Illustrated books and scrolls of tales painted in bright mineral colours by largely anonymous town painters of Kyoto, late 16th–18th century: Tales of Ise, Bunshō zōshi, otogi-zōshi booklets. [aka Nara ehon;Nara picture books]
A1-15 Ethiopian Manuscript Painting: Painting in Ethiopian Christian manuscripts on parchment, from the Aksumite-era gospel books to the Gondarine Marian miracle books: Garima Gospels evangelist portraits, Miracles of Mary (Täammərä Maryam) cycles, illustrated Psalters. [aka Ethiopian illuminated manuscripts]
A1-16 Byzantine Illuminated Manuscripts: Painted miniatures in Greek manuscripts of the Byzantine empire, 4th–15th century, from late antique codices to the classicising Macedonian Renaissance and Palaiologan books: Vienna Dioscurides, Rossano Gospels, Paris Psalter, Menologion of Basil II. [aka Byzantine manuscript painting;Byzantine illumination]
A1-17 Borgia Group: The pre-Hispanic ritual and divinatory screenfold codices painted on deerskin in the Mixteca-Puebla style of central Mexico, 14th–early 16th century: Codex Borgia, Codex Laud, Codex Fejérváry-Mayer, Codex Cospi. [aka Borgia codices;Mixteca-Puebla codices]
A1-18 Mixtec Codices: Genealogical and historical screenfold codices painted on deerskin by Mixtec (Ñudzavui) tlacuiloque of Oaxaca, 14th–16th century: Codex Zouche-Nuttall, Codex Vindobonensis Mexicanus I, Codex Bodley, Codex Colombino. [aka Ñudzavui codices]
A1-19 Aztec Codices: Pictorial manuscripts of the Nahua of central Mexico, from the few pre-Hispanic and early colonial ritual books to the colonial histories, tribute rolls and encyclopedias painted by indigenous tlacuiloque: Codex Borbonicus, Codex Mendoza, Florentine Codex, Codex Azcatitlan. [aka Nahua codices;Aztec codex]
A1-20 Armenian Manuscript Illumination: Painting and ornament of Armenian Gospel books and service books from the 10th to the 18th century, taught in monastic schools named after their regions: Etchmiadzin Gospels, Mugni Gospels, Toros Roslin's Gospels, Glajor Gospels. [aka Armenian miniature painting;Armenian illuminated manuscripts]
### A2 East Asian Ink & Colour Painting — Axis: school and lineage. Built at scale (Chinese, Japanese and Korean painting).
A2-1 Gongbi: The meticulous, finely outlined and coloured manner of Chinese court and academy painting from the Tang and Song: Emperor Huizong's Auspicious Cranes, Zhang Zeduan's Along the River during the Qingming Festival. [aka Court painting;academy painting]
A2-2 Northern Landscape Style: The monumental landscape painting of the Five Dynasties and Northern Song: Fan Kuan's Travelers among Mountains and Streams, Guan Tong's Waiting for the Ferry. [aka Northern Song landscape;shanshui (monumental)]
A2-3 Literati Painting: Scholar-amateur painting of China (wenrenhua) and its Korean and Japanese reflexes, valuing brushwork, poetry and calligraphy over likeness: Ni Zan's Rongxi Studio, Huang Gongwang's Dwelling in the Fuchun Mountains, Dong Qichang's landscapes. [aka Southern School;wenrenhua;scholar painting;Chinese landscape painting;shanshui;Chinese scroll painting]
A2-4 Zhe School: The professional Ming school of landscape continuing Southern Song Ma–Xia painting (15th–16th century): Dai Jin's Returning Home Late from a Spring Outing, Wu Wei's figures. [aka Zhe school (painting)]
A2-5 Wu School: The Ming literati school of Suzhou (15th–16th century) around Shen Zhou and Wen Zhengming: Shen Zhou's Lofty Mount Lu, Wen Zhengming's Old Trees by a Cold Waterfall.
A2-6 Shanghai School: The commercial-literati painting of late Qing Shanghai, bold in colour and subject: Ren Bonian's portraits, Wu Changshuo's flowers. [aka Haipai]
A2-7 Lingnan School: The Cantonese school (early 20th century) of the Gao brothers and Chen Shuren that joined Chinese brushwork with Japanese and Western realism: Gao Jianfu's Flying in the Rain, Zhao Shao'ang's birds.
A2-8 Yamato-e: The classical Japanese narrative and court painting style from the Heian period, continued by the Tosa school: the Tale of Genji scrolls, the Shigisan engi emaki, Tosa Mitsuoki's Quail and Millet. [aka Tosa school]
A2-9 Kanō School: The hereditary school of Japanese official painting (15th–19th century) joining Chinese ink and Yamato-e colour: Kanō Eitoku's Cypress Trees, Kanō Tan'yū's Nijō Castle screens.
A2-10 Rinpa School: The decorative Japanese school of Sōtatsu, Kōrin and Hōitsu (17th–19th century): Sōtatsu's Wind God and Thunder God, Kōrin's Irises. [aka Rimpa]
A2-11 Nanga: Japanese literati painting (bunjinga) of the later Edo period and its Meiji continuation: Ike no Taiga and Yosa Buson's Ten Conveniences and Ten Pleasures, Uragami Gyokudō's Frozen Clouds. [aka Bunjinga;Southern painting]
A2-12 Maruyama–Shijō School: The naturalistic Kyoto painting of Maruyama Ōkyo and Matsumura Goshun (from the 1770s) and its modern heirs: Ōkyo's Pine Trees in Snow, Goshun's Woodcutters and Fishermen. [aka Shijō school;Maruyama school]
A2-13 Nihonga: Modern Japanese-style painting (from the 1880s) in mineral pigments and ink on silk and paper: Hishida Shunsō's Fallen Leaves, Yokoyama Taikan's works, Uemura Shōen's Flame. [aka Japanese-style painting]
A2-14 Ink Wash Painting: Monochrome ink painting of the Zen monasteries of Muromachi Japan (suibokuga), after Song and Yuan models: Mokuan Reien's Four Sleepers, Josetsu's Catching a Catfish with a Gourd, Sesshū's Landscape of the Four Seasons. [aka suibokuga;sumi-e;Muromachi ink painting]
### A3 Icon Painting — Axis: school. Built at scale (Byzantine, Russian, Ethiopian, Cretan icons).
A3-1 Ethiopian Icon Painting: Panel painting of the Ethiopian Orthodox Church from the 15th century, in tempera on gessoed wood (later over glued cloth), as diptychs, triptychs and processional icons, in the styles named First and Second Gondarine: Virgin and Child with archangels, St George on horseback, Kwer'ata Re'esu. [aka Ethiopian icons;Gondarine painting]
A3-2 Byzantine Icons: Devotional images painted in encaustic or egg tempera on wooden panels in the Byzantine empire and its orbit, 6th–15th century: Sinai Christ Pantokrator, Theotokos of Vladimir, Hodegetria and Deesis icons. [aka Byzantine icon painting]
A3-3 Cretan School: Post-Byzantine icon painting of Venetian Crete, 15th–17th century, joining the Palaiologan manner with Italian elements: icons of Andreas Ritzos and Michael Damaskinos, Theophanes the Cretan's Athonite murals. [aka Post-Byzantine painting;Cretan icons]
A3-4 Georgian Icon Painting: Painted panel icons of medieval and early modern Georgia, from the 10th century, often set in chased silver revetments: icons of Svaneti, Gelati iconostasis, Georgian icons at Sinai. [aka Georgian icons]
A3-5 Palaeologan Renaissance: Late Byzantine painting (1261–1453), classicising and intensely expressive in icons and frescoes: the Chora Church frescoes, Theophanes the Greek's icons. [aka Palaeologan art]
A3-6 Russian Icons: Icon painting of Rus' and Muscovy and its schools (Novgorod, Moscow, Vologda, Stroganov): Rublev's Trinity, Dionisius's Ferapontov icons. [aka Russian icon painting]
### A4 Cloth & Scroll Painting — Axis: tradition. Built at scale (thangka, pata, phad, kalighat).
A4-1 Pattachitra: Cloth-based painting of Odisha by the chitrakar community of Puri and Raghurajpur, in mineral colour on gum-and-chalk-primed cotton: Jagannath images, Gita Govinda panels, palm-leaf pothis. [aka Patachitra;Odisha pata]
A4-2 Bengal Patachitra: The narrative scroll painting of the patuas (chitrakars) of West Bengal and Bangladesh, unrolled frame by frame while the painter sings (pater gaan): Manasamangal scrolls, Jadupatua scrolls of the Santal. [aka Patua scroll painting;Bengal pat;pata]
A4-3 Kalighat Painting: Watercolours on mill paper sold by patua painters at the Kalighat temple in Calcutta, 19th–early 20th century, with bold outlines and satire of urban life: Durga and Kali icons, Tarakeswar scandal, babu and courtesan pictures. [aka Kalighat pat]
A4-4 Pichwai: Large painted cloth hangings made at Nathdwara to hang behind the image of Shrinathji: Sharad Purnima, Annakut, cows and lotus ponds. [aka pichhwai;pichhavai]
A4-5 Mata ni Pachedi: Ritual cloth shrines of the Vaghri community of Ahmedabad, hand-painted and block-printed in red and black around the Mother Goddess: Mata on her vahana, temple cloth panels. [aka Mata no Chandarvo]
A4-6 Cheriyal Scroll Painting: Narrative scrolls of Cheriyal in Telangana painted for the bardic storytellers of the Kaki Padagollu: Markandeya Purana scrolls, Gauda Purana. [aka Cheriyal patam]
A4-7 Thangka: Buddhist painting on cotton or silk mounted in a textile frame to be hung, rolled and carried, the principal portable painting of Tibet, Bhutan, Ladakh and Mongolia: deity and lineage portraits, mandalas, wheel-of-life paintings, appliqué festival thangkas. [aka tangka;thanka;Tibetan scroll painting]
A4-8 Beri: The Newar-derived style of Tibetan painting (13th–15th century) and its later continuations, with dense red and blue grounds and Nepalese figure canons: Sakya lineage thangkas, Ngor mandala sets. [aka Beri style;Balri;Nepalese style (in Tibet)]
A4-9 Menri: The central Tibetan painting tradition founded by Menla Döndrub (15th century), with Chinese-inspired landscape and a canon of peaceful deities, renewed as New Menri by Choying Gyatso at Tashi Lhunpo (17th century): arhat sets, lineage paintings. [aka Menri style;sMan ris;Menluk]
A4-10 Khyenri: The central Tibetan painting style created by Khyentse Chenmo of Gongkar Chöde (15th century), known for wrathful deities, bright palettes and near-circular haloes: Gongkar Chöde murals, wrathful protector thangkas. [aka Khyenri style;mKhyen ris]
A4-11 Karma Gadri: The 'encampment' painting tradition of the Karma Kagyu, founded by Namkha Tashi (16th century) and renewed by Situ Panchen at Palpung (18th century), with open Chinese-style landscapes and pale grounds: Situ Panchen's Eight Great Mahasiddhas, Tenth Karmapa paintings. [aka Karma Gardri;Karma Gar-ri;Gardri;Encampment style]
A4-12 Regong Arts: The thangka painting (with murals, sculpture and appliqué) of the monastic artists of Rebkong (Tongren) in Amdo, from the 17th century to the present, on UNESCO's list since 2009: Wutun monastery thangkas, contemporary Rebkong painting. [aka Rebkong art;Repkong painting;Wutun art]
A4-13 Paubha: Newar Buddhist and Hindu painting on cloth of the Kathmandu Valley, from the 12th century to the present, with flat red grounds and donor registers: Vasudhara mandalas, Chakrasamvara paubhas, long narrative vratha paintings. [aka pauba;Newar scroll painting]
A4-14 Dunhuang Paintings: The portable Buddhist paintings and banners on silk, hemp and paper deposited in the Library Cave (Cave 17) at the Mogao Caves, 8th–10th century: Tejaprabha and the Five Planets (897), bodhisattva banners, Famous Images of the Buddha. [aka Dunhuang banners;Library Cave paintings]
A4-15 Kamasan Painting: The classical wayang-style painting of Kamasan village in Klungkung, Bali, on cloth and bark paper for temples and palaces since the Gelgel court (16th century): ider-ider friezes, langse hangings, palelintangan calendars, Kerta Gosa ceilings. [aka Kamasan wayang painting;wayang style painting;sangging Kamasan]
A4-16 Wayang Beber: Javanese narrative scroll painting on dluwang bark paper, unrolled scene by scene by a dalang: Panji scrolls of Pacitan and Gunung Kidul.
A4-17 Ethiopian Magic Scrolls: Protective parchment scrolls cut to the owner's height and painted by däbtära (church-trained scholars) with talismanic figures, eyes, angels and nets beside Ge'ez prayers: healing scrolls, talismanic eyes and faces, Solomon's net designs. [aka Ethiopian healing scrolls;protective scrolls;ketab]
A4-18 Plains Hide Painting: Painting on tanned or raw hide by the peoples of the Great Plains: men's figurative war-exploit and calendar painting and women's geometric designs, on painted buffalo robes, tipi covers and liners, parfleches and winter counts. [aka hide painting;painted robes;parfleche painting]
### A5 Easel Painting — Axis: period style, school or movement. Built at scale for the European lineage and modern and contemporary painting; the pilot holds one non-European court tradition.
A5-1 Qajar Painting: court painting of Qajar Iran (1789–1925) in oil on canvas and in watercolour: life-size royal portraits and figures of pleasure (Mihr 'Ali), and the realist watercolours of Abu'l-Hasan Ghaffari (Sani' al-Mulk). [aka Qajar art (painting)]
A5-2 Balinese Painting: The modern painting of Bali from the late 1920s on paper and canvas, in the village schools that grew up around the Pita Maha association: Ubud genre and landscape, Batuan dense ink-and-wash scenes, Lempad's line drawings. [aka Balinese modern painting;Pita Maha]
A5-3 Sơn Mài: Vietnamese lacquer painting on panel, developed as a fine art at the École des Beaux-Arts de l'Indochine from the 1930s: landscape and genre panels, gold- and eggshell-inlaid screens. [aka son mai;Vietnamese lacquer painting]
A5-4 Fayum Mummy Portraits: Portraits painted in encaustic or tempera on wooden panels and bound over the faces of mummies in Roman Egypt, 1st–3rd century: Hawara and Er-Rubayat portraits, Tomb of Aline, portraits from Antinoöpolis. [aka Faiyum portraits;mummy portraits]
A5-5 New Spanish Baroque: The painting of the Viceroyalty of New Spain in the Baroque idiom, 17th–18th century, from the large altarpieces and sacristy canvases of Mexico City to devotional and civic pictures: Villalpando's Puebla cathedral dome, Juan Correa's sacristy canvases, Miguel Cabrera's Virgins of Guadalupe. [aka Novohispanic painting;New Spain painting;pintura novohispana]
A5-6 Casta Painting: Sets of paintings made in 18th-century New Spain (and occasionally Peru) depicting the hierarchy of racial mixtures as family groups with their dress and produce: Miguel Cabrera's casta series (1763), José de Páez series. [aka pintura de castas]
A5-7 Enconchado: Paintings of late 17th- and early 18th-century New Spain in oil on panel with inlaid fragments of mother-of-pearl under translucent glazes, influenced by Japanese namban lacquer: González family Conquest of Mexico series, Virgin of Guadalupe (1698). [aka enconchados;mother-of-pearl inlay painting]
A5-8 Biombo: Painted folding screens made in New Spain from the early 17th century after Japanese byōbu, with views of Mexico City, the Conquest and festivities: Biombo of the Conquest and View of Mexico City, Indian wedding and flying pole screen. [aka Mexican folding screen]
A5-9 Cusco School: The school of religious painting centred on Cusco in the Viceroyalty of Peru, 16th–18th century, with Andean painters working from Flemish prints and adding brocade gold (brocateado) and local flora: Corpus Christi series, archangel arquebusiers, Diego Quispe Tito's zodiac series. [aka Escuela cuzqueña;Cuzco School]
A5-10 Potosí School: The school of painting of the silver city of Potosí in Upper Peru (Bolivia), 17th–18th century, led by Melchor Pérez de Holguín: Entry of Viceroy Morcillo into Potosí, Virgin of the Cerro. [aka Escuela potosina;Collao school]
A5-11 Quito School: The painting and polychrome sculpture of the Audiencia of Quito, 16th–18th century, known for its realist carved and estofado images and its painting workshops: Miguel de Santiago's Augustinian series, Legarda's winged Virgin of Quito, Caspicara's sculptural groups. [aka Escuela quiteña]
A5-12 Western Desert Painting: The acrylic painting movement of the Central and Western Desert begun at Papunya in 1971, in which Dreaming (Tjukurrpa/Jukurrpa) designs once made in sand, body paint and ceremony are painted on canvas and board in dotted and concentric-circle fields, carried on by community art centres: Papunya Tula boards, Warlukurlangu Jukurrpa canvases, Utopia and APY Lands painting. [aka Western Desert art movement;dot painting;Papunya Tula painting;acrylic desert painting]
A5-13 Contemporary Indigenous Australian Art: Painting by Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander artists from the 1970s onward outside the Western Desert and bark-painting lineages, in ochre and acrylic on canvas, worked through community art centres: Warmun (Turkey Creek) ochre paintings, Mangkaja and Ngukurr acrylics, Mornington Island painting, Mowanjum Wandjina paintings. [aka contemporary Aboriginal art]
A5-14 Hermannsburg School: The Western Arrernte watercolour landscape school of the Hermannsburg mission (from 1934), founded by Albert Namatjira after lessons with Rex Battarbee and carried on by his family and followers: MacDonnell Ranges landscapes, ghost gums. [aka Hermannsburg watercolourists;Arrernte watercolour school]
A5-15 Romanesque Painting: Panel, book and wall painting of Latin Europe in the 11th–12th centuries, with flat, linear, hieratic figures: the Master of Pedret, painted altar frontals of Catalonia, the Registrum Gregorii.
A5-16 Gothic Painting: Painting in Latin Europe from the 13th to the late 14th century, from Italo-Byzantine panel crosses to the narrative altarpieces of Giotto and his followers: Coppo di Marcovaldo's crucifixes, Giotto's Ognissanti Madonna, Catalan Gothic retables. [aka Proto-Renaissance;Trecento painting;maniera greca]
A5-17 Sienese School: The school of painting of Siena, 13th–15th century, with its gold grounds, lyrical line and colour: Duccio's Maestà, Simone Martini's Annunciation, Lorenzetti's Allegory of Good Government. [aka Sienese school of painting]
A5-18 International Gothic: The courtly late Gothic style shared by painters across Europe c. 1375–1425, elegant, decorative and richly detailed: the Wilton Diptych, Gentile da Fabriano's Adoration of the Magi, the Très Riches Heures. [aka Soft Style;Weicher Stil]
A5-19 Early Netherlandish Painting: Oil painting of the Burgundian and Habsburg Netherlands in the 15th and early 16th centuries, with minute naturalism and luminous glazes: the Ghent Altarpiece, the Arnolfini Portrait, Rogier van der Weyden's Descent from the Cross. [aka Flemish Primitives]
A5-20 Italian Renaissance Painting: Painting in Italy c. 1400–1530, from the perspective and naturalism of the Florentine Early Renaissance to the High Renaissance: Masaccio's Trinity, Piero della Francesca's Flagellation, Leonardo's Last Supper, Raphael's School of Athens. [aka Italian Renaissance;Early Renaissance;High Renaissance;Quattrocento]
A5-21 Venetian School: The painting of Venice and its terraferma from the Bellini to the 18th century, built on colour and light: Giovanni Bellini's San Zaccaria Altarpiece, Giorgione's Tempest, Titian's Assumption, Veronese's Wedding at Cana. [aka Venetian painting]
A5-22 Northern Renaissance: The Renaissance north of the Alps (German lands, the Low Countries, France, England), c. 1490–1580: Dürer's Self-Portrait of 1500, the Isenheim Altarpiece, Holbein's Ambassadors, Bruegel's Hunters in the Snow. [aka German Renaissance;Dutch and Flemish Renaissance painting;French Renaissance]
A5-23 Mannerism: The artificial, elongated and complex style that followed the High Renaissance, c. 1520–1600: Pontormo's Deposition, Parmigianino's Madonna with the Long Neck, Bronzino's Allegory with Venus and Cupid. [aka Late Renaissance;Northern Mannerism]
A5-24 School of Fontainebleau: The Mannerist decoration and painting of the French court at Fontainebleau, led by Rosso and Primaticcio (c. 1530–1610): the Galerie François I, Gabrielle d'Estrées and One of Her Sisters. [aka Fontainebleau School]
A5-25 Baroque Painting: European painting of the 17th and early 18th centuries, dramatic in light, movement and emotion, from Rome to Madrid and the colonial Americas: Rubens's Elevation of the Cross, Velázquez's Las Meninas, Pietro da Cortona's Barberini ceiling. [aka Baroque;Italian Baroque painting;Spanish Baroque painting]
A5-26 Caravaggisti: Followers of Caravaggio's dramatic tenebrism and naturalism across Europe, c. 1600–1640: Gentileschi's Judith Slaying Holofernes, Ter Brugghen's Calling of Saint Matthew, Valentin de Boulogne's Concert. [aka Caravaggism]
A5-27 Flemish Baroque Painting: Painting of the Southern Netherlands c. 1585–1700 around Rubens, van Dyck and Jordaens: Rubens's Descent from the Cross, van Dyck's Charles I at the Hunt, Snyders's market stalls.
A5-28 Dutch Golden Age Painting: Painting of the Dutch Republic in the 17th century, its genres sold on an open market: Rembrandt's Night Watch, Vermeer's View of Delft, Ruisdael's Jewish Cemetery, Heda's breakfast pieces. [aka Dutch Golden Age]
A5-29 Rococo: The light, ornamental, intimate style of the 18th century, from the French fêtes galantes to Venetian ceilings: Watteau's Pilgrimage to Cythera, Fragonard's The Swing, Tiepolo's Würzburg ceiling. [aka Rocaille;Late Baroque]
A5-30 Neoclassicism: Painting of the later 18th and early 19th century modelled on antiquity, severe in line and moral in subject: David's Oath of the Horatii, Mengs's Parnassus, Ingres's Grande Odalisque. [aka Neoclassical painting]
A5-31 Romanticism: Painting c. 1780–1850 that put feeling, the sublime and the imagination before rule: Friedrich's Wanderer above the Sea of Fog, Géricault's Raft of the Medusa, Delacroix's Liberty Leading the People, Turner's Slave Ship. [aka Romantic painting]
A5-32 Nazarene Movement: German Romantic painters in Rome (from 1809) who revived the religious art of Dürer and early Raphael: Overbeck's Italia and Germania, the Casa Bartholdy frescoes. [aka Nazarenes]
A5-33 Danish Golden Age: Danish painting of the first half of the 19th century, around Eckersberg and his pupils: Købke's View of Lake Sortedam, Eckersberg's views of Rome. [aka Golden Age of Danish Painting]
A5-34 Düsseldorf School of Painting: Painters trained at the Düsseldorf Academy c. 1819–1918, known for detailed landscapes and history scenes: Achenbach's seascapes, Leutze's Washington Crossing the Delaware. [aka Düsseldorf school]
A5-35 Barbizon School: French landscape painters working from nature in the forest of Fontainebleau c. 1830–1870: Rousseau's Forest of Fontainebleau, Millet's The Gleaners, Corot's Souvenir de Mortefontaine.
A5-36 Hudson River School: American Romantic landscape painting c. 1825–1875: Cole's The Oxbow, Church's Heart of the Andes, Bierstadt's Rocky Mountains.
A5-37 Norwich School of Painters: The regional landscape school of Norwich (1803–1833) around John Crome and John Sell Cotman: Crome's Mousehold Heath, Cotman's Greta Bridge. [aka Norwich School]
A5-38 Academic Art: Painting shaped by the European academies and their Salons in the 19th century, finished, idealising and hierarchical in subject: Cabanel's Birth of Venus, Bouguereau's Nymphs and Satyr, Gérôme's Pollice Verso. [aka Academicism;L'art pompier;Salon painting]
A5-39 Neo-Grec: A mid-19th-century French current of small-scale, archaeologically detailed antique genre scenes: Gérôme's Cock Fight, Hamon's My Sister Is Not In. [aka Neo-Pompeian;néo-grec]
A5-40 Realism: The mid-19th-century movement that painted contemporary life and labour without idealisation: Courbet's A Burial at Ornans, Millet's The Angelus, Repin's Barge Haulers on the Volga. [aka French Realism;Realism (art movement)]
A5-41 Costumbrismo: Spanish and Latin American painting of local customs, types and manners in the 19th century: Bécquer's Andalusian scenes, Pancho Fierro's Lima watercolours.
A5-42 Pre-Raphaelite Brotherhood: The English brotherhood (1848) and its circle that painted with the detail and colour of art before Raphael: Millais's Ophelia, Rossetti's Beata Beatrix, Hunt's The Light of the World. [aka Pre-Raphaelites;Pre-Raphaelitism]
A5-43 Hague School: Dutch painters working in The Hague c. 1860–1900 in grey-toned realist landscape and genre: Mesdag's Panorama, Mauve's Morning Ride on the Beach. [aka Haagse School]
A5-44 Macchiaioli: Tuscan painters of the 1850s–1870s who built pictures from patches (macchie) of light and shade: Fattori's The Rotonda di Palmieri, Lega's The Visit.
A5-45 Naturalism: Late-19th-century plein-air painting of rural and working life with photographic truth: Bastien-Lepage's Haymaking, Stanhope Forbes's A Fish Sale on a Cornish Beach, the Skagen Painters. [aka Naturalism (art)]
A5-46 Munich School: Painters trained at the Munich Academy c. 1850–1914 in dark, painterly realism: Leibl's Three Women in Church, Gyzis's works, Chase's early portraits. [aka Münchner Schule]
A5-47 Impressionism: The French movement of the 1860s–1880s that painted modern life and light in broken colour outdoors, and its national offshoots: Monet's Impression, Sunrise, Renoir's Bal du moulin de la Galette, Morisot's The Cradle.
A5-48 Neo-Impressionism: The systematic colour-division of Seurat and Signac from 1886 and its Italian divisionist branch: Seurat's A Sunday on La Grande Jatte, Signac's Portrait of Félix Fénéon. [aka Pointillism;Divisionism]
A5-49 Post-Impressionism: Painting after Impressionism c. 1886–1905 that turned colour and form to structure and expression: Cézanne's Mont Sainte-Victoire, Van Gogh's Starry Night, Gauguin's Vision after the Sermon.
A5-50 Pont-Aven School: Painters around Gauguin and Émile Bernard in Brittany from 1886, flat colour and bounding line (synthetism, cloisonnism): Bernard's Breton Women in the Meadow, Sérusier's The Talisman. [aka Synthetism;Cloisonnism]
A5-51 Symbolism: Late-19th-century painting of dream, myth and inner states: Moreau's The Apparition, Redon's The Cyclops, Khnopff's I Lock My Door Upon Myself. [aka Symbolist painting]
A5-52 Aestheticism: The British "art for art's sake" painting of the 1860s–1890s: Whistler's Symphony in White, Leighton's Flaming June, Albert Moore's Midsummer. [aka Aesthetic movement]
A5-53 Tonalism: American (and Australian) landscape painting c. 1880–1915 in muted, atmospheric tones: Inness's Home of the Heron, Dewing's The Recitation.
A5-54 Luminism: American landscape painting c. 1850–1875 of calm, hidden-brushwork light: Fitz Henry Lane's Owl's Head, Heade's Approaching Thunder Storm.
A5-55 Glasgow School: The Glasgow Boys (c. 1880–1900), Scottish painters of plein-air naturalism and decorative colour: Guthrie's A Highland Funeral, Henry and Hornel's The Druids. [aka Glasgow Boys]
A5-56 Expressionism: Painting from c. 1905 that distorted colour and form for emotional force, centred in Germany and Austria: Kirchner's Street, Berlin, Kokoschka's The Bride of the Wind, Marc's Blue Horse. [aka German Expressionism]
A5-57 Fauvism: The French painting of pure, arbitrary colour around Matisse and Derain, 1904–1908: Matisse's Woman with a Hat, Derain's Charing Cross Bridge. [aka Les Fauves]
A5-58 Cubism: The movement of Picasso and Braque (from 1907) that fractured objects into planes and multiple viewpoints, with its Salon Cubist and Section d'Or circles: Les Demoiselles d'Avignon, Braque's Violin and Candlestick, Gris's Portrait of Picasso.
A5-59 Futurism: The Italian movement (1909) celebrating speed, machines and the city, and its Russian Cubo-Futurist branch: Boccioni's The City Rises, Balla's Dynamism of a Dog on a Leash, Severini's Armoured Train.
A5-60 Orphism: Abstract painting of prismatic colour around Robert and Sonia Delaunay and Kupka, c. 1912: Robert Delaunay's Simultaneous Windows, Kupka's Amorpha. [aka Orphic Cubism;Simultanism]
A5-61 Vorticism: The British avant-garde of 1914–1915 around Wyndham Lewis and the magazine BLAST: Bomberg's The Mud Bath, Lewis's The Crowd.
A5-62 Suprematism: Malevich's abstraction of pure geometric forms from 1915: Black Square, Suprematist Composition: White on White.
A5-63 Constructivism: The Russian movement from c. 1915 of art as construction and production, and its international heirs: Tatlin's Monument to the Third International, Rodchenko's Pure Colours, El Lissitzky's Proun.
A5-64 De Stijl: The Dutch movement (1917) of Neoplasticism, reducing art to primaries and the right angle: Mondrian's Composition with Red, Blue and Yellow, van Doesburg's Counter-Composition. [aka Neoplasticism]
A5-65 Dada: The anti-art movement born in Zurich in 1916 and spread to Berlin, Paris and New York: Duchamp's Fountain, Höch's Cut with the Kitchen Knife, Arp's collages. [aka Dadaism]
A5-66 Surrealism: The movement launched by Breton in 1924 that painted dream and the unconscious: Dalí's The Persistence of Memory, Magritte's The Treachery of Images, Ernst's Europe after the Rain.
A5-67 Metaphysical Painting: Giorgio de Chirico's and Carrà's painting of uncanny piazzas and mannequins, 1910–1920: The Disquieting Muses, Mystery and Melancholy of a Street. [aka Pittura metafisica]
A5-68 New Objectivity: The sober, sharp-focus realism of Weimar Germany in the 1920s: Dix's Portrait of Sylvia von Harden, Grosz's Pillars of Society, Schad's Self-Portrait with Model. [aka Neue Sachlichkeit]
A5-69 Magic Realism: Realist painting of the 1920s–1950s that makes the everyday strange: Carel Willink's Wilma, Pyke Koch's Shooting Gallery, Ivan Albright's The Picture of Dorian Gray. [aka Magischer Realismus]
A5-70 Precisionism: American painting of the 1920s–1930s rendering industry and the city in crisp geometric planes: Sheeler's American Landscape, Demuth's My Egypt. [aka Cubist-Realism]
A5-71 Regionalism: American painting of the 1930s of the rural Midwest: Wood's American Gothic, Benton's America Today, Curry's Tragic Prelude. [aka American Regionalism]
A5-72 Ashcan School: New York realist painters of the early 20th century who depicted the city's streets and poor: Bellows's Stag at Sharkey's, Sloan's McSorley's Bar. [aka The Eight]
A5-73 Social Realism: Politically engaged realist painting of the 1920s–1940s on labour and poverty: Ben Shahn's The Passion of Sacco and Vanzetti, Isabel Bishop's working women.
A5-74 Socialist Realism: The official style of the Soviet Union from 1932 and of the socialist states: Gerasimov's Lenin on the Tribune, Deineka's Defence of Petrograd.
A5-75 Harlem Renaissance: The flowering of African-American art in the 1920s–1930s centred in Harlem: Aaron Douglas's Aspects of Negro Life, Archibald Motley's Nightlife, Augusta Savage's The Harp. [aka New Negro Movement]
A5-76 School of Paris: The international, often émigré artists working in Paris c. 1900–1940 outside a single movement: Modigliani's Jeanne Hébuterne, Chagall's I and the Village, Soutine's Carcass of Beef. [aka École de Paris]
A5-77 Scuola Romana: Roman painters of the 1920s–1930s around Scipione and Mafai, expressionist in colour against the classicising Novecento: Mafai's Demolitions, Scipione's Cardinal Decano. [aka Roman School]
A5-78 Neo-Romanticism: Figurative painting of the 1930s–1950s (Berman, Tchelitchew; the British Neo-Romantics) reviving Romantic landscape and mood: Paul Nash's Totes Meer, Sutherland's Entrance to a Lane.
A5-79 Naïve Art: Painting by artists without academic training, flat, detailed and frontal: Henri Rousseau's The Dream, Séraphine Louis's Tree of Paradise, Ivan Generalić's Hlebine paintings. [aka Naive art;Primitive art]
A5-80 Art Brut: Dubuffet's "raw art" of outsiders, the untrained and the institutionalised, and its successor outsider art: Adolf Wölfli's illustrated books, Aloïse Corbaz's theatres. [aka Outsider art;Art singulier]
A5-81 Abstract Expressionism: The New York painting of the 1940s–1950s, gestural or field-based: Pollock's Autumn Rhythm, de Kooning's Woman I, Kline's Chief. [aka Action painting;New York School;abstract art (American)]
A5-82 Color Field: Abstraction of large expanses of flat or stained colour from the late 1940s: Rothko's No. 61 (Rust and Blue), Frankenthaler's Mountains and Sea, Newman's Vir Heroicus Sublimis. [aka Color field painting]
A5-83 Lyrical Abstraction: Post-war gestural abstraction in Paris (abstraction lyrique) and its American 1960s sense: Georges Mathieu's Capetians Everywhere, Zao Wou-Ki's large canvases. [aka Abstraction lyrique]
A5-84 Informalism: European post-war abstraction of matter, sign and gesture (art informel, tachisme, matter painting): Fautrier's Hostages, Wols's It's All Over, Tàpies's matter paintings. [aka Art informel;Tachisme;Matter painting]
A5-85 COBRA: The Copenhagen–Brussels–Amsterdam movement (1948–1951) of spontaneous, childlike expressionist painting: Appel's Questioning Children, Jorn's Letter to My Son. [aka CoBrA]
A5-86 Hard-edge Painting: Abstraction of crisp-edged flat colour areas from the late 1950s: Ellsworth Kelly's Red Blue Green, Karl Benjamin's compositions.
A5-87 Concrete Art: Geometric abstraction built from pure plastic means, after van Doesburg's 1930 manifesto, in Switzerland, Italy and Latin America: Max Bill's Endless Ribbon, Lygia Clark's Bichos, Tomás Maldonado's works. [aka Art concret]
A5-88 Op Art: Abstraction of the 1960s exploiting optical effects: Bridget Riley's Movement in Squares, Vasarely's Vega series. [aka Optical art]
A5-89 Pop Art: Art of the late 1950s–1960s drawn from mass media, advertising and consumer goods: Warhol's Campbell's Soup Cans, Lichtenstein's Whaam!, Hamilton's Just What Is It ....
A5-90 Neo-Dada: American art of the late 1950s reviving Dada assemblage and found imagery: Rauschenberg's Monogram, Johns's Flag.
A5-91 Nouveau Réalisme: The French movement founded in 1960 around Restany, appropriating the real (décollage, accumulation, wrapping): Arman's Accumulations, Yves Klein's Anthropometries, Villeglé's torn posters. [aka New Realism]
A5-92 Funk Art: Irreverent figurative art of the San Francisco Bay Area in the 1960s, often in ceramics: Robert Arneson's self-portrait busts, Roy De Forest's paintings.
A5-93 Bay Area Figurative Movement: San Francisco painters of the 1950s–1960s who returned from abstraction to the figure: Diebenkorn's Cityscape I, David Park's Four Men.
A5-94 Northwest School: Painters of the Pacific Northwest in the 1930s–1950s, mystical and Asian-influenced: Mark Tobey's white writing, Morris Graves's Blind Bird. [aka Northwest Mystics]
A5-95 Pattern and Decoration: The American movement of the mid-1970s that reclaimed ornament, textile and craft: Miriam Schapiro's femmages, Robert Kushner's costumes. [aka P&D]
A5-96 Photorealism: Painting from the late 1960s that reproduces photographs with exacting fidelity: Richard Estes's storefronts, Chuck Close's Big Self-Portrait. [aka Hyperrealism;Superrealism]
A5-97 Neo-figurative: The return to the figure in 1960s Latin America and Spain (Otra Figuración): Jorge de la Vega's works, Luis Felipe Noé's Introduction to Hope. [aka Nueva figuración;New Figuration]
A5-98 Neo-Expressionism: Figurative, expressive painting of the late 1970s–1980s: Baselitz's upside-down figures, Kiefer's Margarethe, Basquiat's Untitled (Skull). [aka Neue Wilde;Transavantgarde;Figuration Libre]
A5-99 Lettrism: Isidore Isou's movement (from 1945) of letters and signs as the material of poetry and painting: Isou's hypergraphic novels, Lemaître's works. [aka Letterism]
A5-100 Black Arts Movement: The African-American art movement of 1965–1975 tied to Black Power (AfriCOBRA, the Wall of Respect): Jeff Donaldson's Wives of Shango, Wadsworth Jarrell's Revolutionary. [aka BAM]
A5-101 British Black Arts Movement: Black British artists of the 1980s (the BLK Art Group) addressing race and diaspora: Lubaina Himid's A Fashionable Marriage, Keith Piper's works. [aka BLK Art Group]
A5-102 Lowbrow: Californian pop-surrealist painting from the late 1970s rooted in comics, hot-rod and tattoo culture: Robert Williams's paintings, Mark Ryden's The Meat Show. [aka Pop surrealism]
A5-103 Mission School: San Francisco artists of the 1990s working from street, folk and graffiti culture: Barry McGee's installations, Margaret Kilgallen's murals.
A5-104 Stuckism: The figurative painting movement founded in London in 1999 against conceptual art: Charles Thomson's Sir Nicholas Serota Makes an Acquisitions Decision.
A5-105 Superflat: Takashi Murakami's Japanese postmodern movement (2000) of flat anime and print-derived imagery: Murakami's Tan Tan Bo, Aya Takano's paintings.
A5-106 Hurufiyya Movement: Modern art of the Arab world and Iran (from the 1950s) that uses Arabic letters as abstract form: Madiha Omar's works, Shakir Hassan Al Said's The Contemplative Dimension, Parviz Tanavoli's Heech. [aka Hurufiyya;Letterism (Arabic)]
A5-107 Dakar School: Senegalese painting of the 1960s–1970s fostered by Senghor's Négritude policy: Papa Ibra Tall's tapestries, Iba N'Diaye's works. [aka École de Dakar]
A5-108 Yōga: Japanese painting in Western oil technique from the Meiji period: Kuroda Seiki's Lakeside, Takahashi Yuichi's Salmon, Kishida Ryūsei's Reiko. [aka Western-style painting (Japan)]
A5-109 Bengal School of Art: The Indian nationalist painting movement of Abanindranath Tagore and his pupils (c. 1900–1940) reviving Mughal and Ajanta idioms: Abanindranath Tagore's Bharat Mata, Nandalal Bose's Haripura posters. [aka Bengal School]
A5-110 Modern Art: The period style of painters born c. 1860–1944 working outside a recorded movement, across the global lineages of easel painting: academic-trained modern portraitists, independent landscape painters, national modernisms. [aka Modernism]
A5-111 Contemporary Art: The period style of painters born from 1945, working after modernism in global, plural practices: post-colonial figurative painting, new abstraction.
A5-112 Hispano-Flemish Style: Fifteenth-century painting of Castile and Aragon shaped by Early Netherlandish oil technique and realism, under the patronage of Isabella I: Bartolomé Bermejo's Saint Michael Triumphant, Fernando Gallego's Pietà, Pedro Berruguete's altarpieces. [aka Hispano-Flemish painting;Spanish-Flemish style]
A5-113 Dansaekhwa: The Korean monochrome painting movement of the 1970s: Park Seo-bo's Ecriture, Yun Hyong-keun's Umber-Blue, Chung Sang-hwa's grids, Ha Chong-hyun's Conjunction. [aka Korean monochrome painting;Tansaekhwa]
### A6 Folk & Popular Painting — Axis: tradition. Built at scale.
A6-1 Madhubani Painting: The Mithila painting of women of Bihar and Nepal's Terai, moved from house walls to paper from the 1960s, in bharni, kachni and godna styles: kohbar ghar marriage paintings, Baua Devi nagas. [aka Mithila painting]
A6-2 Warli Painting: White rice-paste painting of the Warli of Maharashtra, from marriage chauk on house walls to works on paper (Jivya Soma Mashe): Palaghata chauk, tarpa dance circles.
A6-3 Gond Painting: The painting of the Pardhan Gond of Madhya Pradesh in dot-and-dash infill on paper and canvas since Jangarh Singh Shyam (Jangarh kalam): trees of life, Bhajju Shyam animals. [aka Pardhan Gond art;Jangarh kalam]
A6-4 Tanjore Painting: Devotional painting of Thanjavur on wooden board with gesso relief, gold leaf and glass stones, 18th–19th century: Balakrishna, Rama pattabhishekam. [aka Thanjavur painting]
A6-5 Mysore Painting: Devotional painting of the Mysore court in gesso and gold leaf, finer and lighter than Tanjore work: Krishna and Rama icons, Sritattvanidhi illustrations.
A6-6 Truck Art: The painted, mirrored and calligraphic decoration of lorries in Pakistan and India, from Rawalpindi, Peshawar and Karachi workshops: jingle trucks, Horn OK Please tailboards. [aka jingle truck;Truck art in South Asia]
A6-7 Rickshaw Painting: Hand-painted cycle-rickshaw panels and hoods of Dhaka (UNESCO 2023), with film stars, landscapes and animals: backboard paintings, applique hoods. [aka rickshaw art;rickshawchitra]
A6-8 Letras y Figuras: The 19th-century Philippine genre of watercolour letters composed of figures and scenes, with the costume pictures (tipos del país) of the same Manila painters: name monograms, Damián Domingo costume albums. [aka tipos del país]
A6-9 Ōtsu-e: Folk paintings sold to travellers at Ōtsu on the Tōkaidō from the 17th century, brushed quickly with stencils and few colours: the Demon Chanting the Nembutsu, Wisteria Maiden, Fujimusume, Buddhist icons. [aka Otsu-e;Otsu painting]
A6-10 Ema: Painted wooden votive plaques offered at Shinto shrines and Buddhist temples, from small mass-made tablets to large framed ema halls painted by town and ukiyo-e painters: horse ema, Kitano Tenmangū votive tablets. [aka ema votive plaques;emaki-dō]
A6-11 Minhwa: Korean folk painting of the late Joseon period, anonymous decorative screens and scrolls for houses: chaekgeori book-and-scholar's-object screens, munjado ideograph paintings, magpie and tiger, sipjangsaeng ten longevity symbols. [aka Korean folk painting]
A6-12 Tingatinga Painting: The Dar es Salaam school of enamel painting on board founded by Edward Said Tingatinga in 1968 and carried on by his followers and the Tingatinga Arts Cooperative: flat-coloured animals and birds, crowded village and spirit scenes. [aka Tinga Tinga]
A6-13 Retablo: Small devotional paintings of saints in oil on tin (lámina) made by popular painters in Mexico in the 19th century, with the related ex-voto paintings giving thanks for a miracle: Santo Niño de Atocha láminas, ex-votos of San Juan de los Lagos. [aka lámina;retablo santo;ex-voto]
A6-14 Amate Painting: Painting in bright colours on amate bark paper by Nahua artists of the Alto Balsas in Guerrero since the 1960s, with Otomí paper cut-figures from San Pablito: village fiesta scenes, birds and flowers. [aka papel amate;amatl]
A6-15 Tablas de Sarhua: Painted boards of Sarhua (Ayacucho), originally genealogical beams given at house-raisings, later sold as narrative paintings of village life: house-beam tablas, Piraq Causa series. [aka Sarhua painted boards]
A6-16 Tigua Painting: Painting on stretched sheepskin by Kichwa artists of Tigua in Ecuador's central highlands, begun by Julio Toaquiza in the 1970s, depicting fiestas and Andean landscapes: Corpus Christi dancers, Cotopaxi landscapes. [aka Tigua art]
A6-17 Fileteado: The decorative painting of Buenos Aires carts, buses and shop signs with scrolls, flowers and lettering, inscribed by UNESCO in 2015: colectivo panels, filete signboards. [aka filete porteño]
A6-18 Bark Painting: Painting in natural ochres on prepared stringybark sheets, the classic medium of Arnhem Land, with clan designs in fine cross-hatching (rarrk) in the east and figurative and x-ray figures in the west: Yolŋu miny'tji clan paintings, Kunwinjku x-ray figures, Groote Eylandt black-ground barks. [aka Arnhem Land bark painting;rarrk (cross-hatching)]
A6-19 Theorem Painting: American stencilled still lifes of fruit and flowers painted on velvet or paper with cut stencils (theorems), a schoolgirl and amateur art of c. 1810–1860: fruit baskets on velvet, bowls of flowers. [aka theorem stencil;velvet painting]
A6-20 Reverse Glass Painting: Folk pictures painted in reverse on the back of a glass pane, made in glassworks districts of Austria, Bavaria and Bohemia from the 18th century and sold at fairs and shrines: Sandl devotional pictures with the Sandl rose, saints and Madonnas for the home altar. [aka Hinterglasmalerei;reverse painting on glass;Sandlbild]
A6-21 Petrykivka Painting: Ornamental painting of the village of Petrykivka (Dnipropetrovsk Oblast, Ukraine) with flowers, berries and birds brushed with fingers, cat-hair brushes and reeds on house walls, paper sheets and wooden objects (UNESCO 2013): painted malyovky sheets, painted chests and plates. [aka Petrykivka decorative painting;Petrykivka]
A6-22 Tauric Painting: Traditional decorative painting of Mykolaiv Oblast in southern Ukraine, inscribed on the National Inventory of Intangible Cultural Heritage of Ukraine.
A6-23 Gorodets Painting: Painting of the Gorodets district on the Volga from the mid-19th century, first on distaff bottoms and later on boards, boxes and furniture, with horsemen, ladies, birds and roses in a quick brush style on bright grounds: distaff bottoms (donce), painted panels. [aka Gorodetskaya rospis]
A6-24 Mezen Painting: Red-and-black painting on distaffs, boxes and ladles from the lower Mezen River (Arkhangelsk region), with rows of horses, deer and birds in a graphic, archaic style: Palashchelye distaffs. [aka Mezen distaff painting;Palashchelye painting]
A6-25 Northern Dvina Painting: The painting of distaffs, chests and utensils in the villages along the Northern Dvina (Arkhangelsk region), in local styles with red floral grounds, birds, sirins and scenes of sleighing: Borok distaffs, Permogorye distaffs, Rakulka distaffs. [aka Severodvinsk painting;Severodvinskaya rospis]
A6-26 Ural-Siberian Painting: Floral painting of the Old Believer and peasant households of the Urals and western Siberia (18th–19th century) on furniture, chests, walls and utensils, with large peonies and roses in a free brush. [aka Ural painting;Uralo-Sibirskaya rospis]
A6-27 Zhostovo Painting: Oil painting of flower bouquets on black-lacquered iron trays made in Zhostovo near Moscow since the early 19th century, built up in layers and varnished: bouquet trays, gilded-rim trays. [aka Zhostovo trays]
A6-28 Nizhny Tagil Tray Painting: Painted and lacquered iron trays made in Nizhny Tagil in the Urals from the 18th century, with flowers and genre scenes under a translucent varnish, the forerunner of Zhostovo. [aka Tagil tray painting]
A6-29 Naïve Painting of Kovačica: Naïve painting of the Slovak community of Kovačica in Vojvodina (Serbia) from the 1950s, in oil on canvas and on objects, with village life, harvests and weddings (UNESCO 2024). [aka Kovačica naive art;Slovak naive art painting]
A6-30 Pysanka: Easter eggs decorated in the Slavic and East European wax-resist (batik) technique with layered dyes and symbolic patterns; regional schools and related types (krashanka, krapanka, drapanka) are kinds: Hutsul pysanky, Boyko pysanky. [aka pysanky;pisanka;pisanica]

## B Drawings & Prints — test: a drawing made as an end in itself or as a design, or an impression pulled from a matrix (woodblock, plate, stone, screen).
  key: 1. Drawn by hand? → B1. 2. Printed from a matrix? → B2.
### B1 Drawing — Axis: school or style.
B1-1 Saz Style: the Ottoman court style of feathery serrated leaves, rosettes and fabulous creatures drawn in ink by the nakkaşhane under Shahkulu and Kara Memi (16th century), and carried from drawings into tiles, textiles and bindings. [aka saz yolu; saz leaf style]
B1-2 Ledger Art: Plains narrative drawing in pencil, crayon and ink on the pages of account ledgers and on muslin, from the 1860s, continuing hide painting on paper: the Fort Marion drawings, Cheyenne and Kiowa warrior ledgers, Lakota ledger books. [aka ledger drawing;ledger book art]
B1-3 Old Master Drawing: European drawing before c. 1800 as preparatory study and finished work, in metalpoint, chalk, pen and wash: Leonardo's Vitruvian Man, Dürer's Praying Hands, Rembrandt's landscape drawings. [aka Old Master drawings]
B1-4 Illustration: Drawing made for books, periodicals, posters and caricature from the 18th century to the present: Gillray's caricatures, Tenniel's Alice illustrations, Saul Steinberg's New Yorker covers. [aka Caricature;book illustration]
B1-5 Golden Age of Illustration: Book and magazine illustration c. 1880–1920, when colour printing made illustrators celebrities: Arthur Rackham's Peter Pan, Howard Pyle's pirates, Edmund Dulac's Arabian Nights.
B1-6 Ligne Claire: The clear-line drawing style of Hergé and the Brussels school of Franco-Belgian comics: The Adventures of Tintin, Blake and Mortimer. [aka Clear line;School of Brussels]
B1-7 Marcinelle School: The dynamic, rounded Franco-Belgian comics style of Spirou magazine (Jijé, Franquin, Morris): Spirou et Fantasio, Lucky Luke, Gaston. [aka Charleroi school]
B1-8 Manga: Japanese comics and their drawn idiom from the 20th century: Tezuka's Astro Boy, Oda's One Piece, Takeuchi's Sailor Moon.
### B2 Printmaking — Axis: school and tradition; the process (woodcut, etching, lithography, screen print) is a tag.
B2-1 Đông Hồ Painting: Woodblock prints of Đông Hồ village in Bắc Ninh on điệp (shell-coated) paper with natural colours, sold for Tết: Mouse Wedding, Jealousy, pig and piglets. [aka Dong Ho painting;tranh Đông Hồ]
B2-2 Hàng Trống Painting: Hanoi's urban folk pictures, printed in outline from woodblocks and hand-coloured: Five Tigers, Carp Gazing at the Moon, Mother Goddess images. [aka tranh Hàng Trống]
B2-3 Kim Hoàng Painting: Woodblock prints of Kim Hoàng village west of Hanoi, printed on red or yellow paper: Tết pigs and roosters, calligraphic scrolls. [aka tranh Kim Hoàng]
B2-4 Nianhua: Chinese New Year pictures: popular colour woodblock prints of door gods, auspicious children and opera scenes pasted up at the New Year, made in named print towns: Yangliuqing, Taohuawu, Yangjiabu, Zhuxianzhen. [aka New Year picture;Chinese New Year prints;nianhua woodblock prints]
B2-5 Cordel Literature: The chapbooks of north-eastern Brazil sold hung on strings, whose covers are woodcuts (xilogravura) by popular printmakers: J. Borges woodcuts, cordel covers of Caruaru and Juazeiro. [aka literatura de cordel;xilogravura]
B2-6 Inuit Printmaking: Stonecut, stencil and lithographic printmaking of Inuit community co-operatives in Canada since 1957, issued in annual collections: Kinngait (Cape Dorset) annual print collections, Baker Lake prints, Kenojuak Ashevak's The Enchanted Owl. [aka Cape Dorset prints;Kinngait prints;Inuit prints]
B2-7 Torres Strait Islander Printmaking: The linocut and etching movement of the Torres Strait (Zenadth Kes) from the 1990s, cutting island cosmology, totems and turtle-shell mask forms into large black-and-white prints: Badu and Moa linocuts, Mabuiag etchings. [aka Zenadth Kes printmaking;Torres Strait linocut]
B2-8 Lubok: Russian popular prints from the 17th to the early 20th century, first woodcuts then copper engravings and lithographs, hand-coloured and sold at fairs, with saints, folk tales, satire and the Mice Burying the Cat: The Mice Burying the Cat, Baba Yaga. [aka luboks;lubki;Russian popular print]
B2-9 Old Master Print: Western printmaking before c. 1830 in woodcut, engraving, etching and mezzotint: Dürer's Melencolia I, Rembrandt's Hundred Guilder Print, Piranesi's Carceri.
B2-10 Etching Revival: The re-emergence of etching as an original art c. 1850–1930 in France, Britain and the United States: Whistler's Thames Set, Méryon's Eaux-fortes sur Paris, Seymour Haden's landscapes.
B2-11 Modern Printmaking: Printmaking by artists born c. 1880–1944: the expressionist woodcut, the artist's lithograph and the screenprint: Kollwitz's War cycle, Picasso's Vollard Suite.
B2-12 Contemporary Printmaking: Printmaking by artists born from 1945 across print and multiple practices.
B2-13 Ukiyo-e: Japanese woodblock prints and paintings of the floating world (17th–19th century): Hokusai's Great Wave, Hiroshige's Fifty-three Stations, Utamaro's beauties.
B2-14 Shin-hanga: The 20th-century Japanese revival of ukiyo-e production by publisher Watanabe Shōzaburō: Kawase Hasui's landscapes, Hashiguchi Goyō's beauties. [aka New prints]
B2-15 Sōsaku-hanga: Japanese "creative prints" (from 1904) designed, carved and printed by the artist: Onchi Kōshirō's abstractions, Munakata Shikō's Buddhist prints. [aka Creative prints]

## C Book Arts & Calligraphy — test: the written or decorated book or page is the work: calligraphy, illumination (ornament), binding and paper decoration, or a manuscript tradition studied as the book.
  key: 1. Writing as an art, judged by its script? → C1 (placed by script family).
  key: 2. A manuscript tradition or its illumination (ornament, frontispieces, headings, borders)? → C2.
  key: 3. The binding? → C3. Decorated paper (marbling, paper cutting)? → C4.
### C1 Calligraphy — Axis: script family and its lineage of masters.
C1-1 Kufic: the angular early Arabic script of Qur'ans on parchment and of inscriptions, with its later Eastern (broken cursive), floriated, plaited and square (banna'i) forms. [aka Kufi; Kufic script]
C1-2 Maghribi Script: the rounded scripts of al-Andalus, North Africa and West Africa (mabsut, mujawhar, Andalusi, Sudani), written with a pointed pen. [aka Maghrebi script; Andalusi script]
C1-3 Six Pens (al-Aqlam al-Sitta): the proportioned cursive scripts — thuluth, naskh, muhaqqaq, rayhani, tawqi' and riqa' — codified by Ibn Muqla and Ibn al-Bawwab and perfected by Yaqut al-Musta'simi, then carried by his pupils and by the Ottoman masters (Sheikh Hamdullah, Hafiz Osman). [aka aqlam al-sitta; six scripts; Thuluth; Naskh; Muhaqqaq;Islamic calligraphy;Arabic calligraphy]
C1-4 Nasta'liq: the hanging Persian script attributed to Mir 'Ali Tabrizi (14th century), the script of Persian poetry and album calligraphy (Sultan 'Ali Mashhadi, Mir 'Ali Haravi, Mir 'Imad), with its shikasta (broken) offshoot. [aka Nastaliq; nastaʿlīq; ta'liq; shikasta]
C1-5 Diwani: the Ottoman chancery script of decrees and imperial documents, with the tughra, the sultan's calligraphic monogram. [aka Divani; diwani jali; tughra]
C1-6 Sini: Chinese Arabic calligraphy, written with a brush in a rounded style on scrolls and Qur'ans of the Hui Muslims. [aka Sini script; Chinese Arabic calligraphy]
C1-7 Mongolian Calligraphy: Writing as an art in the vertical Classical Mongolian script, with its six basic strokes, on paper, silk and wood (UNESCO 2013): sutra manuscripts, calligraphic scrolls. [aka Mongol bichig calligraphy]
C1-8 Georgian Calligraphy: Writing of the Georgian scripts (asomtavruli, nuskhuri, mkhedruli) as an art, in manuscripts and inscriptions.
C1-9 Chinese Calligraphy: The art of writing Chinese characters with the brush, in seal, clerical, regular, running and cursive scripts: Wang Xizhi's Lantingji Xu, Huaisu's Autobiography, Mi Fu's Poems Written on a Boat on the Wu River. [aka Shufa]
C1-10 Japanese Calligraphy: Shodō: the Japanese art of the brush in kanji and kana, with its court lineages (Sesonji-ryū) and Zen bokuseki: Ono no Michikaze's works, Fujiwara no Yukinari's Hakubunshū. [aka Shodō]
C1-11 Western Calligraphy: The art of Latin-script writing, from Renaissance chancery hands and writing masters to modern lettering: Arrighi's La Operina, Palatino's writing book.
C1-12 Type Design: The design of typefaces and lettering for print and screen since the 19th century: Johnston's Underground typeface, Frutiger's Univers, Miedinger's Helvetica. [aka Typeface design]
### C2 Illuminated Manuscripts & Illumination — Axis: manuscript tradition.
C2-1 Early Qur'anic Manuscripts: Qur'ans written on parchment in Hijazi and Kufic scripts from the 7th to the 10th century, in horizontal format with sparse illuminated headings (the Blue Qur'an, the Tübingen and Birmingham fragments). [aka early Qur'ans; Hijazi Qur'ans; Kufic Qur'ans]
C2-2 Islamic Illumination: the illumination of Qur'ans and books after about 1000: gilded and painted frontispieces, sura headings, margins and borders, in the Abbasid, Mamluk, Ilkhanid, Timurid and Safavid (tazhib), Ottoman (tezhip) and Mughal traditions, with its named illuminators. [aka tazhib; tezhip; Qur'an illumination]
C2-3 Timbuktu Manuscripts: the manuscripts of the West African Islamic scholarly tradition (Timbuktu, Djenné, the Sahel), in Arabic and in Ajami (African languages in Arabic script), with their Sudani script and leather satchel bindings. [aka West African manuscripts; Ajami manuscripts]
C2-4 Malay Manuscripts: illuminated Malay manuscripts in Jawi script from the Islamic sultanates of the Malay world (Aceh, Terengganu, Java), with their floral frontispieces. [aka Jawi manuscripts; Malay Qur'ans]
C2-5 Palm-Leaf Manuscripts: Books of incised or inked palm leaves strung between wooden boards, the manuscript tradition of South Asia and Sri Lanka: Tamil and Grantha olas, Odia incised and painted pothis, Sinhalese Pali texts, Pala Buddhist Prajnaparamita. [aka ola leaf manuscripts;pothi]
C2-6 Lontar: Manuscripts on incised leaves of the lontar palm, blackened with soot, the book form of Bali, Lombok and older Java: Kakawin Sutasoma, Nagarakretagama, illustrated prasi leaves. [aka lontar manuscripts;rontal]
C2-7 Folding-Book Manuscripts: Concertina-folded books of mulberry or khoi paper, lacquered at the ends, in Burma, Thailand, Laos and Cambodia: parabaik, samut khoi, Lanna phap sa, kraing. [aka folding book manuscript;parabaik;samut khoi;samut thai;kraing;phap sa]
C2-8 Kammavaca: Burmese ordination texts written in square tamarind-seed script in lacquer and gold on lacquered palm leaf, cloth or metal: gilt kammavaca leaves, painted covers. [aka kammavacha]
C2-9 Pustaha: The divination and ritual books of the Batak datu, written on folded tree bark between carved wooden covers: Great Pustaha. [aka pustaka]
C2-10 Dunhuang Manuscripts: The manuscripts sealed in the Library Cave (Cave 17) of the Mogao Caves about 1000 and opened in 1900: Buddhist sutras, documents and the earliest dated printed book, the Diamond Sutra of 868, with illustrated sutra frontispieces. [aka Dunhuang library cave manuscripts]
C2-11 Uigwe: Illustrated royal protocol books of the Joseon court (1392–1910), recording ceremonies, weddings and funerals in text and painted processions: Hwaseong seongyeok uigwe, Banchado procession paintings. [aka Joseon royal protocols]
C2-12 Sutra Copying: Buddhist sutras transcribed as acts of devotion and decorated with gold and silver on dyed paper, frontispiece paintings and ornamented rollers in Japan and Korea: the Heike Nōkyō, Lotus Sutra fan papers, Goryeo sagyeong in gold on indigo paper. [aka shakyō;sagyeong;decorated sutras;illuminated sutras]
C2-13 Dongba Manuscripts: Ritual manuscripts of the Naxi Dongba priests of Lijiang in Yunnan, written in pictographic Dongba script and painted with deities, with painted ritual cards and scrolls. [aka Naxi Dongba manuscripts;Dongba literature]
C2-14 Ethiopian Manuscripts: The Ge'ez (and later Amharic) manuscript tradition of the Ethiopian and Eritrean church: parchment codices in wooden boards with blind-tooled leather, written in Ge'ez script with ornamental harag frames: gospel books, Psalters, Arganonä Maryam prayer books. [aka Ge'ez manuscripts;Ethiopic manuscripts]
C2-15 Book of the Dead: Illustrated Egyptian funerary papyri of spells for the afterlife, with painted vignettes, New Kingdom to Ptolemaic: Papyrus of Ani, Papyrus of Hunefer, Greenfield Papyrus. [aka Egyptian funerary papyri;Coming Forth by Day]
C2-16 Maya Codices: The surviving bark-paper screenfold books of the Postclassic Maya, written in hieroglyphs with painted almanacs: Dresden Codex, Madrid Codex, Paris Codex, Maya Codex of Mexico. [aka Maya codex]
C2-17 Late Antique Illumination: Illustrated and decorated Latin books of late antiquity, 4th–7th century, in rustic capitals and uncial: Vergilius Vaticanus, St Augustine Gospels, Codex Argenteus. [aka Early Christian illumination]
C2-18 Insular Illumination: Manuscript art of Ireland and Britain, c. 600–900, joining Celtic, Germanic and Mediterranean ornament in carpet pages and great initials: Book of Durrow, Lindisfarne Gospels, Book of Kells, Book of Armagh. [aka Hiberno-Saxon illumination;Insular art]
C2-19 Merovingian Illumination: Book decoration of Frankish Gaul, 7th–8th century, with zoomorphic initials of fish and birds: Gellone Sacramentary, Gelasian Sacramentary.
C2-20 Carolingian Illumination: Manuscript painting of the Frankish empire, c. 780–900, in its court and monastic schools: Godescalc Evangelistary, Coronation Gospels, Utrecht Psalter, Codex Aureus of St. Emmeram. [aka Carolingian manuscripts]
C2-21 Ottonian Illumination: Manuscript painting of the Ottonian empire, c. 950–1050, at Reichenau, Trier, Echternach, Cologne and Regensburg: Gospels of Otto III, Pericopes of Henry II, Registrum Gregorii, Bamberg Apocalypse. [aka Ottonian manuscripts]
C2-22 Anglo-Saxon Illumination: Late Anglo-Saxon manuscript art, 10th–11th century, in the acanthus-bordered Winchester style and in outline drawing: Benedictional of St Æthelwold, Harley Psalter, Ramsey Psalter. [aka Winchester School;Winchester style]
C2-23 Mozarabic Illumination: Manuscript painting of early medieval Christian Iberia, 10th–11th century, in flat banded colour, above all the illustrated commentaries of Beatus of Liébana: Morgan Beatus, Gerona Beatus, León Bible of 960. [aka Spanish illumination of the Early Middle Ages;Visigothic illumination]
C2-24 Romanesque Illumination: Manuscript painting of Latin Europe c. 1050–1200, with large-format Bibles and monumental figure style: Bury Bible, Winchester Bible, St Albans Psalter, Hortus deliciarum.
C2-25 Renaissance Illumination: Manuscript illumination of the 15th–16th century influenced by Renaissance painting, in Italy, France and the courts that collected it: Farnese Hours, Bible of Borso d'Este, Hours of Étienne Chevalier, Corvinian manuscripts.
C2-26 Hebrew Illuminated Manuscripts: Decorated and illustrated Hebrew books of the Middle Ages and early modern period — Bibles, haggadot and mahzorim — with micrography and painted cycles: Sarajevo Haggadah, Golden Haggadah, Birds' Head Haggadah, Rothschild Miscellany. [aka Jewish illumination]
C2-27 Russian Illuminated Manuscripts: Gospel books, chronicles and service books of Kievan Rus' and Muscovy, 11th–17th century, with Byzantine-derived miniatures and teratological headpieces and initials: Ostromir Gospels, Khitrovo Gospels, Radziwiłł Chronicle. [aka Old Russian manuscript illumination]
C2-28 Fraktur: Illuminated folk documents of the Pennsylvania Germans, 18th–19th century, lettered in a broken script and painted with birds, hearts and tulips: Taufschein (birth and baptismal certificates), Vorschrift (writing samples). [aka Pennsylvania German fraktur;Fraktur (folk art)]
C2-29 Gothic Book Illustration: The illumination of Gothic manuscripts (13th–15th century), from Paris workshops to the Books of Hours: Jean Pucelle's Hours of Jeanne d'Évreux, the Douce Apocalypse, the Bedford Hours. [aka Gothic illumination]
C2-30 Ghent-Bruges School: Flemish manuscript illumination c. 1475–1560 with illusionistic borders: the Grimani Breviary, Simon Bening's Da Costa Hours. [aka Ghent–Bruges school]
### C3 Bookbinding — Axis: tradition.
C3-1 Islamic Bookbinding: the bindings of the Islamic book: tooled, stamped and filigree-cut leather with the envelope flap, and from the 15th century painted lacquer bindings. [aka Islamic book covers]
### C4 Paper Arts — Axis: technique.
C4-1 Ebru: paper marbling as practised in Ottoman Turkey and Iran: colours floated on size and transferred to paper, used for album margins and endpapers. [aka Turkish marbling; abri; paper marbling]
C4-2 Sanjhi: Paper-stencil cutting of the Braj temples of Mathura and Vrindavan, used to lay coloured powder images: Krishna lila stencils. [aka sanjhi paper stencil]
C4-3 Chinese Paper Cutting: Designs cut from red and coloured paper with scissors or knives for windows, doors and festivals across China (UNESCO 2009): window flowers (chuanghua), xi double-happiness cuts, embroidery patterns. [aka jianzhi;Chinese papercut]
C4-4 Papel Picado: Mexican cut tissue paper banners chiselled through stacked sheets with designs for the Day of the Dead and fiestas. [aka papel cortado;papel de china]
C4-5 Vytsinanka: Belarusian folk paper cutting, symmetrical designs cut with scissors from folded paper for interiors and festivals (UNESCO 2024). [aka Belarusian paper cutting;vycinanka]
C4-6 Slovenian Paper Flower Making: The Slovenian craft of making crepe and tissue paper flowers for church feasts, processions and festive decoration. [aka paper flower making]

## D Sculpture & Carving — test: carved, modelled or cast in three dimensions as an image or as carving for its own sake: statues, reliefs, carved stucco and stone ornament, ivory, bone, jade, rock crystal and hardstone, masks and puppets.
  key: 1. Carved stucco, stone or terracotta ornament from buildings? → D1. 2. Ivory or bone? → D2. 3. Rock crystal, jade or other hardstone? → D3.
  key: 4. A mask or puppet made for performance? → D4. 5. Figurative sculpture in stone, wood, metal or clay? → D5.
  key: 6. An object of ritual or office (staff, reliquary, altar, regalia) that is not a mask or a figure? → D6.
### D1 Architectural Carving & Stucco — Axis: tradition.
D1-1 Samarra Stucco: the carved and moulded stucco of the Abbasid capital Samarra (9th century) in its three styles, of which the third, the bevelled style, carried abstract ornament across the early Islamic world. [aka bevelled style; Samarra styles A–C]
D1-2 Nasrid Stucco (Yesería): the carved and painted stucco of Nasrid Granada and the Mudéjar workshops (Alhambra wall panels, muqarnas). [aka yesería; Alhambra stucco]
D1-3 Assyrian Palace Reliefs: Carved gypsum orthostat reliefs from Neo-Assyrian palaces, 9th–7th century BC, with lamassu gateway figures: Ashurnasirpal II at Nimrud, Sennacherib's Lachish reliefs, Ashurbanipal's lion hunt. [aka Assyrian sculpture;Neo-Assyrian reliefs]
### D2 Ivory & Bone Carving — Axis: workshop tradition.
D2-1 Andalusian Ivories: carved ivory boxes and pyxides of the Umayyad caliphal workshops of Córdoba and Madinat al-Zahra and of the taifa courts (10th–11th century). [aka Caliphal ivories; ivories of al-Andalus]
D2-2 Siculo-Arabic Ivories: painted and gilded ivory caskets made in Norman Sicily and southern Italy (12th–13th century) in an Arabic decorative idiom. [aka Siculo-Arabic ivory caskets]
D2-3 Begram Ivories: Carved ivory and bone plaques and furniture fittings of the 1st–2nd century found in the Kushan treasure at Begram (Kapisa) in Afghanistan, in an Indian idiom: river-goddess brackets, openwork throne-leg panels. [aka Begram hoard ivories]
D2-4 Netsuke: Miniature toggles carved in ivory, wood and other materials to secure hanging containers to the kimono sash, Edo to Meiji period, with named carvers and regional schools: katabori figures, manjū netsuke, sashi netsuke.
D2-5 Benin Ivories: Ivory carved for the Oba of Benin by the Igbesanmwan guild and in neighbouring Owo, 15th–19th century: pendant masks of the Iyoba Idia, carved tusks for royal altars, double gongs and armlets. [aka Benin ivory carving;Edo ivories]
D2-6 Afro-Portuguese Ivories: Ivories carved by Sapi (Sierra Leone), Edo (Benin) and Kongo carvers for Portuguese patrons in the 15th–16th century: salt cellars, side-blown oliphants, spoons and pyxes. [aka Sapi-Portuguese ivories;Luso-African ivories]
D2-7 Nimrud Ivories: Carved, gilded and inlaid ivory plaques and figures of the 9th–7th century BC found at the Assyrian capital Nimrud, made in Assyrian, Phoenician and North Syrian styles: Lioness attacking a Nubian, Woman at the Window, furniture plaques. [aka Phoenician ivories;Levantine ivories]
D2-8 Byzantine Ivories: Ivory carving of the Byzantine empire, 5th–12th century: consular diptychs, Barberini Ivory, Romanos group icons, Veroli Casket.
D2-9 Alaska Native Ivory Carving: Carving and engraving of walrus ivory and bone by the Iñupiat, Yupik and St Lawrence Island peoples of the Bering Strait, from ancient engraved hunting gear to figures, cribbage boards and engraved tusks for the curio trade. [aka walrus ivory carving;Bering Strait ivory carving;Eskimo ivory]
D2-10 Mississippian Shell Gorgets: Engraved discs of marine-shell worn at the neck in the Mississippian Southeast (c. 1000–1600), cut with Southeastern Ceremonial Complex imagery: Spiro and Etowah gorgets, rattlesnake and cross-in-circle gorgets, mask gorgets. [aka shell gorget;engraved shell]
D2-11 Gothic Ivories: Elephant-ivory carving of Gothic Europe, c. 1200–1530, led by Parisian workshops: devotional diptychs and polyptychs, statuettes of the Virgin, mirror cases and caskets with romance scenes. [aka Gothic ivory sculpture]
D2-12 Scrimshaw: Engraving and carving on whale teeth, whalebone and walrus ivory by sailors of the whaling fleets, 18th–19th century: engraved sperm-whale teeth, busks, jagging wheels.
D2-13 Duodji: The handicraft of the Sámi of Norway, Sweden, Finland and the Kola peninsula, divided into hard duodji (antler, bone and wood) and soft duodji (leather, textiles and pewter-thread embroidery): antler-handled knives, carved drinking cups (guksi), pewter-thread embroidered bags. [aka Sámi duodji;Sami handicraft;garraduodji;dipmaduodji]
D2-14 Uelen Bone Carving: Engraving and carving of walrus ivory by Chukchi and Yupik carvers at Uelen in Chukotka, institutionalised as a workshop in 1931: engraved walrus tusks with hunting scenes, small figures of walrus and bears. [aka Chukchi walrus-ivory engraving;Chukotka bone carving]
D2-15 Tobolsk Bone Carving: Bone and mammoth-ivory carving of Tobolsk in western Siberia from the 18th century, with small sculptures of northern life (reindeer sleds, hunters); a factory since 1929. [aka Tobolsk carved bone]
D2-16 Yakut Bone Carving: Mammoth-ivory carving of the Sakha (Yakutia), from 19th-century combs and caskets to Soviet-era sculptural compositions combined with silver and leather. [aka Yakut ivory carving;Sakha mammoth-ivory carving]
### D3 Hardstone, Rock Crystal & Jade Carving — Axis: workshop tradition.
D3-1 Fatimid Rock Crystal: vessels and objects carved from rock crystal in Fatimid Egypt (10th–11th century), above all the ewers with relief decoration later mounted in European church treasuries. [aka Fatimid rock crystal ewers]
D3-2 Mughal Jade: jade and hardstone vessels carved for the Timurid and Mughal courts, culminating in Shah Jahan's wine cup (1657). [aka Mughal hardstone carving]
D3-3 Chinese Jade: Nephrite and later jadeite carved in China from the Neolithic to the Qing: ritual bi discs and cong tubes of Hongshan and Liangzhu, Shang and Zhou pendants and Han burial suits, Qing imperial boulders and vessels. [aka Chinese jade carving;yu]
D3-4 Chinese Snuff Bottles: Small stoppered bottles made at the Qing court and in China's workshops (late 17th–20th century) in hardstone, glass, porcelain, enamel and lacquer, including inside-painted glass: Beijing palace glass bottles, carved agate, inside-painted bottles. [aka snuff bottles]
D3-5 Cylinder Seals: Engraved stone cylinders rolled on clay to leave a continuous image, the glyptic art of Mesopotamia, Iran, Syria and Anatolia from the Uruk period to the Achaemenids: Uruk and Jemdet Nasr seals, Akkadian contest scenes, Old Syrian and Mitanni seals, Neo-Assyrian ritual scenes. [aka glyptic art;Mesopotamian seals]
D3-6 Minoan Seals: Engraved sealstones of hardstone, bone and gold made on Bronze Age Crete and adopted on the Mycenaean mainland, 3rd–2nd millennium BC: lentoid and amygdaloid seals, Pylos Combat Agate, gold signet rings. [aka Aegean seals;Minoan sealstones]
D3-7 Engraved Gems: Intaglios and cameos cut in hardstone in the Greek, Etruscan and Roman world, 6th century BC – 4th century AD: Graeco-Persian gems, Etruscan scarabs, Roman Republican ringstones, Gemma Augustea. [aka glyptics;intaglios;cameos]
D3-8 Mesoamerican Jade: Jadeite and greenstone carved by the peoples of Mesoamerica and Costa Rica from the Olmec to the Aztec, as masks, figures, celts, earspools and pendants: Olmec votive axes, Maya jade mosaic masks of Palenque and Calakmul, Costa Rican axe-god pendants. [aka Mesoamerican greenstone]
D3-9 Hei-tiki: Māori ornaments carved from pounamu (New Zealand nephrite and bowenite) with stone tools, worn as heirlooms (taonga): hei-tiki human figure pendants, hei matau hook pendants, kuru ear pendants, mere pounamu clubs. [aka pounamu carving;tiki]
### D4 Masks & Puppets — Axis: tradition.
D4-1 Karagöz Shadow Puppets: the translucent painted leather puppets of the Ottoman shadow theatre of Karagöz and Hacivat. [aka Karagöz and Hacivat; hayal-i zıll]
D4-2 Tholu Bommalata: Shadow-puppet theatre of Andhra Pradesh and Karnataka with large translucent painted-leather figures: Ramayana Ravana puppets, togalu gombeyata figures. [aka leather puppets;Tolu bommalata]
D4-3 Wayang Kulit: The flat, pierced and painted buffalo-hide shadow puppets of Java and Bali (UNESCO 2003/2008), cut by named craftsmen: Arjuna and Bima figures, gunungan, Semar and the punakawan. [aka wayang purwa;shadow puppets]
D4-4 Wayang Golek: Carved and painted wooden rod puppets of Sunda and Java, with the flat wooden wayang klitik of East Java: Sundanese Ramayana and Mahabharata sets, Menak cycle puppets. [aka wayang golek purwa;wayang menak]
D4-5 Topeng: Carved and painted wooden masks of Javanese and Balinese masked dance-drama: Panji masks of Cirebon and Malang, Balinese topeng pajegan sets. [aka wayang topeng;topeng masks]
D4-6 Nang Yai: Large perforated hide panels of the Thai court shadow theatre, each a whole scene from the Ramakien, with the small jointed puppets of southern nang talung: Wat Khanon panels. [aka Thai shadow puppets]
D4-7 Sbek Thom: Large cut-hide panels of the Khmer shadow theatre (UNESCO 2005) performed with the Reamker. [aka Khmer shadow theatre]
D4-8 Yoke Thé: The carved, painted and costumed string marionettes of the Burmese court theatre: zawgyi alchemist, nat and princess figures. [aka Burmese marionettes;yoke thay]
D4-9 Chinese Shadow Puppetry: Translucent puppets cut from leather or paper and painted, used in shadow theatre across China (UNESCO 2011), with regional styles: Shaanxi, Tangshan (Luanzhou), Sichuan puppets. [aka piyingxi;Chinese shadow puppets]
