D4-10 Bunraku: The puppets of the Japanese ningyō jōruri theatre, carved and painted wooden heads (kashira) with mechanisms and costumed bodies worked by three puppeteers, and regional puppet troupes. [aka ningyō jōruri]
D4-11 Korean Masks: Carved wooden and gourd masks of Korean mask-dance drama (talchum) and village rites, by regional tradition: Hahoe and Byeongsan masks (National Treasure), Bongsan, Yangju, Ogwangdae masks. [aka tal;talchum masks]
D4-12 Dogon Masks: Masks of the Dogon Awa society danced at the dama funerary rites and the Sigi, carved in wood with rectangular faces and superstructures: kanaga, satimbe, sirige (storeyed house), imina na (great mask). [aka imina]
D4-13 Chiwara: Antelope crest headdresses of the Bamana Ciwara association, danced in pairs at planting and harvest, in vertical, horizontal and abstract regional styles: male and female pairs, vertical Bamako-region crests, horizontal Ségou crests. [aka Ci wara;Tyiwara]
D4-14 Senufo Masks: Masks and helmet masks of the Senufo Poro and Tyekpa societies of northern Côte d'Ivoire, Mali and Burkina Faso: kpeliye'e face masks, kponyugu fire-spitter helmet masks, wanyugo helmets. [aka Poro masks]
D4-15 Chokwe Masks: Masks of the Chokwe of Angola, Zambia and the DR Congo danced at mukanda initiation and festivals: mwana pwo (young woman) masks, cihongo male masks, resin-and-barkcloth initiation masks. [aka Pwo masks;mwana pwo]
D4-16 Gelede: Masks of the Yoruba (Ketu, Egbado, Anago) Gelede society, danced in pairs to honour the mothers: helmet masks with carved superstructures, Efe night masks. [aka Gẹlẹdẹ]
D4-17 Igbo Masks: Masks and masquerades of the Igbo of south-eastern Nigeria, by village and society: agbogho mmuo maiden-spirit masks, Ijele towering masquerade, Afikpo okumkpa masks, Izzi elephant-spirit helmets. [aka mmanwu]
D4-18 Dan Masks: Wooden face masks of the Dan (Gio, Yacouba) of Liberia and Côte d'Ivoire, with polished oval faces, slit or round eyes and named spirit roles: deangle masks, gunyege racing masks, ma go miniature masks. [aka Yacouba masks;Gio masks]
D4-19 Sowei Masks: Black helmet masks of the women's Sande society of the Mende, Vai, Temne and Gola of Sierra Leone and Liberia, with neck rings and elaborate coiffures: ndoli jowei, sowo gande masterworks. [aka sowo;Sande masks;Bundu masks]
D4-20 Kuba Masks: The royal masks of the Kuba kingdom, danced as a trio of founding figures: mwaash aMbooy, ngady amwaash, bwoom. [aka Kuba masquerade]
D4-21 Kifwebe: Striated masks of the Songye and Luba bwadi bwa kifwebe society: male crested kifwebe, female white kifwebe. [aka Kifwebe masks]
D4-22 Pende Masks: Masks of the Central and Kwilu Pende of the DR Congo, danced at mbuya village masquerades and chiefs' rites, with their ivory miniatures: mbuya character masks, giwoyo and kipoko masks, ikhoko pendants. [aka mbuya]
D4-23 Punu Masks: The white-faced okuyi masks of the Punu and Lumbo of southern Gabon, danced on stilts: okuyi maiden masks. [aka Punu-Lumbo masks;okuyi]
D4-24 Northwest Coast Masks: Carved and painted wooden masks of the Northwest Coast peoples, danced at winter ceremonials and potlatches: Kwakwaka'wakw transformation masks, Hamat'sa bird masks, Nuu-chah-nulth and Heiltsuk masks, Tlingit shaman's masks. [aka transformation mask]
D4-25 Yup'ik Masks: Shamanic dance masks of driftwood, feathers and paint carved by the Yup'ik and Cup'ik of south-western Alaska, made in pairs and destroyed after use, from finger masks to large hanging masks. [aka Cup'ik masks;kegginaquq]
D4-26 Torres Strait Turtle-Shell Masks: Masks of the Torres Strait Islands built from heated and stitched turtle-shell plates, worn in initiation, funerary and increase ceremonies, with composite human-animal forms in the west and human faces in the east: krar (western islands), le op (Mer, Erub), crocodile-head masks. [aka krar;le op;Torres Strait masks]
D4-27 Processional Giants: Huge carried effigies of giants, animals and dragons, built of wicker, wood and papier-mâché and paraded at town festivals of the Low Countries, northern France and Iberia: gegants of Catalonia, giants of Flanders and Wallonia, Jas de Keistamper of Boxtel. [aka gegants;reuzen;géants;gigantes]
### D5 Figurative Sculpture — Axis: period style, school or movement. Built at scale.
D5-1 Gandharan Art: Buddhist sculpture of Gandhara (Peshawar valley, Swat, Taxila), 1st century BCE–5th century CE, in schist and stucco with Hellenistic drapery: standing Buddhas, bodhisattva Maitreya, narrative stupa reliefs. [aka Greco-Buddhist art;Gandhara art]
D5-2 Art of Mathura: The school of red-sandstone sculpture of Mathura (2nd century BCE–12th century), at its height under the Kushans and Guptas: Katra Buddha, yakshi railing pillars, Kanishka statue. [aka Mathura school;Mathura art]
D5-3 Amaravati Art: The Andhra school of limestone relief sculpture of the Krishna valley under the Satavahanas and Ikshvakus: Amaravati stupa drum slabs, Nagarjunakonda friezes. [aka Andhra school;Vengi school]
D5-4 Pala Art: The stone and bronze Buddhist and Hindu sculpture of Bengal and Bihar under the Palas and Senas (8th–12th century), in black basalt and eight-metal alloy: Kurkihar bronzes, Nalanda bodhisattvas, Surya steles. [aka Pala-Sena art;Eastern Indian art]
D5-5 Chola Bronzes: Lost-wax bronze images cast in the Chola kingdom of Tamil Nadu (c. 850–1280) for temple processions: Shiva Nataraja, Somaskanda, Parvati. [aka South Indian bronzes]
D5-6 Dhokra: Lost-wax brass casting of tribal smiths of central and eastern India (Bastar, Bengal, Odisha) with wax-thread surfaces: Bastar horse and elephant figures, measure bowls. [aka dokra;Bastar bronze]
D5-7 Bankura Horse: Stylised terracotta votive horses of Panchmura in Bankura, West Bengal, offered at village shrines. [aka Panchmura horse]
D5-8 Molela Terracotta: Hand-modelled terracotta votive plaques of Molela in Rajasthan, made for shrines of Dharmaraja and Devnarayan. [aka Molela clay work]
D5-9 Nepalese Sculpture: The metal, stone and wood sculpture of the Newars of the Kathmandu Valley from the Licchavi period on, above all gilt-copper images cast by lost wax and repoussé: standing Vajrapani (6th–7th c.), gilt-copper Taras, dancing Ganeshas. [aka Newar sculpture;Newar metal sculpture;Nepalese bronzes]
D5-10 Kashmiri Bronzes: Buddhist and Hindu images cast in brass with silver and copper inlay in Kashmir (6th–12th century), largely preserved in Tibetan monasteries, the source of the Kashmiri (Kache) style of western Tibet: crowned Buddhas, Vajrasattva with silver-inlaid eyes. [aka Kashmir bronzes;Kashmiri metal sculpture;Kache style]
D5-11 Tibetan Buddhist Sculpture: Images cast in gilt copper and brass, or modelled in clay and stucco, for Tibetan monasteries from the 11th century on, in Indian, Newar and Chinese-derived styles that fused into Tibetan workshops: Densatil stupa figures, portrait sculptures of lamas, Tsang gilt-copper images. [aka Tibetan bronzes;Tibetan sculpture]
D5-12 Zanabazar School: The gilt-bronze sculpture of the first Jebtsundamba Khutukhtu Zanabazar (1635–1723) and his workshop at Tövkhön, in a Nepalese-derived style, and its Mongolian followers: White Tara, Vajradhara (1683), Five Dhyani Buddhas. [aka Zanabazar style]
D5-13 Sino-Tibetan Sculpture: Tibetan Buddhist images made in Chinese imperial and northern workshops for Tibetan and Mongol patrons, from the Yuan atelier of Aniko through the Yongle and Xuande bronzes to Qing court and Dolonnor casting: Yongle-marked gilt bronzes, Kangxi gilt bronzes. [aka Sino-Tibetan bronzes;Yongle bronzes]
D5-14 Khmer Sculpture: The stone and bronze sculpture of Cambodia and the Khmer lands from the pre-Angkor kingdoms to the Bayon (6th–13th century), in its successive temple styles: Harihara of Prasat Andet, Angkor Wat apsaras, Jayavarman VII portrait heads, Prakhon Chai bronzes. [aka Khmer art;Angkorian sculpture]
D5-15 Dvaravati Art: The Buddhist sculpture of the Mon kingdoms of central Thailand (6th–11th century), in stone, bronze, terracotta and stucco: standing Buddhas with double vitarka, Dharmachakra wheels, Ku Bua terracottas. [aka Mon-Dvaravati art]
D5-16 Cham Sculpture: The sandstone and bronze sculpture of the Cham kingdoms of central Vietnam (7th–15th century): Mỹ Sơn E1 pedestal, Đồng Dương Buddha and dvarapalas, Tháp Mẫm guardians. [aka Art of Champa;Champa sculpture]
D5-17 Majapahit Terracotta: Earthenware figurines, heads, reliefs and models from Trowulan and other East Javanese sites of the Majapahit era (13th–15th century): portrait heads, piggy banks, house models. [aka Trowulan terracottas]
D5-18 Bulul: Carved wooden granary guardian figures of the Ifugao of northern Luzon, seated or standing, made in pairs. [aka bul-ul;bulol]
D5-19 Philippine Santos: Carved and polychromed wooden and ivory devotional images made in the Spanish Philippines from the late 16th century, many for export to Mexico and Spain: Santo Niño, Virgin of the Immaculate Conception, ivory Christ child. [aka santo;Hispano-Philippine ivories]
D5-20 Haniwa: Unglazed terracotta cylinders and figures set on and around Kofun-period burial mounds in Japan (3rd–6th century): warriors in armour, horses, houses, dancing figures.
D5-21 Mingqi: Figures and models made to furnish Chinese tombs, from Han pottery houses and attendants to Tang lead-glazed camels, horses and tomb guardians: sancai camel with musicians, zhenmushou tomb guardians, Northern Wei soldiers. [aka Chinese tomb figures;tomb pottery;Tang dynasty tomb figures]
D5-22 Chinese Buddhist Sculpture: Buddhist images in China in stone, bronze, wood and clay from the 4th century, in cave temples and for altars: Yungang and Longmen caves, Xiangtangshan reliefs, Liao painted wooden Guanyin, Dazu rock carvings.
D5-23 Korean Buddhist Sculpture: Buddhist images of the Three Kingdoms, Unified Silla, Goryeo and Joseon in gilt bronze, stone and wood: pensive bodhisattvas, Seokguram, the Seosan rock-carved triad.
D5-24 Japanese Buddhist Sculpture: Buddhist images of Japan in bronze, lacquer and wood from the Asuka period onward, made by workshop lineages of sculptors (busshi): Tori school Shaka triad, Tōdai-ji guardian kings, Kei school Kamakura figures. [aka busshi;Japanese Buddhist sculpture]
D5-25 Japanese Dolls (Ningyō): Japanese dolls and folk toys of clay, wood, papier-mâché and fabric, made in named regional centres: Hakata and Fushimi clay dolls, Kyō-ningyō, hina dolls, inu-hariko papier-mâché dogs. [aka ningyō;kyōdo gangu]
D5-26 Kokeshi: Lathe-turned and painted wooden dolls of the Tōhoku hot-spring towns (from the 19th century), in named lineages: Naruko, Tōgatta, Tsuchiyu kokeshi.
D5-27 Huishan Clay Figurines: Painted clay figurines from Huishan near Wuxi in Jiangsu (Ming to the present): A-fu plump children, opera figures (shouniezi). [aka Wuxi clay figurines;Huishan niren]
D5-28 Clay Figure Zhang: The painted clay figure sculpture of the Zhang family workshop of Tianjin, founded by Zhang Mingshan (1826–1906): genre figures and opera characters modelled with realism. [aka Niren Zhang]
D5-29 Tu'er Ye: Clay figures of the Moon Rabbit dressed as a warrior or official, made in Beijing for the Mid-Autumn Festival since the Ming. [aka Lord Rabbit;Rabbit God]
D5-30 Nok Terracottas: Terracotta sculpture of the Nok culture of central Nigeria (c. 900 BCE–300 CE), hollow-built figures and heads with triangular pierced eyes and elaborate coiffures; the related Sokoto and Katsina Ala styles are kinds: seated figures, heads, animal figures. [aka Nok sculpture]
D5-31 Ife Art: The classical sculpture of Ile-Ife (c. 1000–1450) in copper alloy and terracotta: naturalistic heads and figures of rulers and court: Wunmonije heads, Obalufon mask, terracotta heads from shrine sites. [aka Ife bronzes;Ife terracottas;Ife heads]
D5-32 Benin Bronzes: Cast brass of the Edo court of Benin, 13th–19th century, by the Igun Eronmwon guild: palace plaques, commemorative heads for royal altars, figures of the Iyoba and of Portuguese, altarpieces. [aka Benin brass casting;Benin court art]
D5-33 Lower Niger Bronze Industry: Fagg's name for copper-alloy castings of the 14th–16th centuries from the lower Niger that fit neither Ife nor Benin, among them the Tsoede bronzes of Tada and Jebba: Tada seated figure, Jebba bowman, bells and plaques. [aka Tsoede bronzes]
D5-34 Esie Soapstone Figures: Over a thousand soapstone figures of seated men and women (c. 12th–15th century) found at Esie in Yoruba-speaking Kwara State, the largest corpus of stone sculpture in sub-Saharan Africa. [aka Esie stone images;ere Esie]
D5-35 Djenné Terracottas: Terracotta sculpture of the Inland Niger Delta (c. 1100–1600), from Djenné-Djeno and neighbouring sites: kneeling and seated figures, figures covered with pustules or snakes, equestrian figures; Bankoni is a related style. [aka Inland Niger Delta terracottas;Jenne terracottas;Middle Niger terracottas]
D5-36 Bura Terracottas: Funerary terracottas of the Bura culture of Niger and Burkina Faso (c. 200–1100): cylindrical heads on jar burials, equestrian figures. [aka Bura-Asinda-Sikka]
D5-37 Koma Terracottas: Terracotta figures and heads from burial mounds of Komaland in northern Ghana (c. 1100–1700), many heavily looted: stylised heads, equestrian figures. [aka Komaland terracottas]
D5-38 Sao Terracottas: Terracotta and copper-alloy sculpture of the Sao civilisation south of Lake Chad (c. 500–1600): human and animal heads, funerary jars. [aka Sao art]
D5-39 Lydenburg Heads: Seven hollow terracotta heads of the Early Iron Age (c. 500 CE) from Lydenburg in Mpumalanga, the earliest known sculpture of southern Africa.
D5-40 Nomoli: Small stone figures (soapstone, steatite) of the Sapi peoples of Sierra Leone and Guinea (c. 1400–1600), found in fields by later Mende and Kissi farmers: seated nomoli, larger pomdo heads. [aka nomoli figurines;pomdo]
D5-41 Zimbabwe Birds: The eight soapstone birds on columns from Great Zimbabwe (c. 13th–15th century). [aka Great Zimbabwe soapstone birds]
D5-42 Yoruba Sculpture: Wood sculpture of the Yoruba of Nigeria and Benin, carved by named masters and workshops for shrines, palaces and divination: ere ibeji twin figures, Ifa divination trays and bowls, Ogboni edan brass pairs, veranda posts by Olowe of Ise. [aka Yoruba carving]
D5-43 Dogon Sculpture: Wood sculpture of the Dogon of the Bandiagara escarpment, Mali, with the earlier Tellem figures: primordial couples, figures with raised arms, granary doors and locks, hogon cups. [aka Dogon art]
D5-44 Bamana Sculpture: Wood and iron sculpture of the Bamana (Bambara) of Mali, carved by numu blacksmiths: jonyeleni and gwan maternity figures, boli power objects, n'tomo and kore masks. [aka Bambara sculpture]
D5-45 Senufo Sculpture: Wood sculpture of the Senufo of Côte d'Ivoire, Mali and Burkina Faso by carver groups (kulebele, fonombele): pombia (child of Poro) figures, deble rhythm pounders, madeo divination figures, kalao hornbills. [aka Senufo art]
D5-46 Baule Sculpture: Wood sculpture of the Baule of Côte d'Ivoire, polished and finely detailed: blolo bla and blolo bian spirit-spouse figures, asie usu nature-spirit figures, goli and mblo masks, heddle pulleys. [aka Baoulé sculpture]
D5-47 Lobi Sculpture: Wood figures of the Lobi of Burkina Faso, Ghana and Côte d'Ivoire made for thil shrines: bateba figures in gesture, heads on posts. [aka bateba]
D5-48 Akan Sculpture: Wood and terracotta sculpture of the Akan (Asante, Fante, Anyi) of Ghana and Côte d'Ivoire: akuaba fertility figures, mma terracotta memorial heads, linguist staff finials, stools. [aka Asante sculpture]
D5-49 Chokwe Sculpture: Court sculpture of the Chokwe of Angola and the DR Congo, 19th century: Chibinda Ilunga hunter-hero figures, mwanangana chiefs' figures, royal chairs and stools. [aka Cokwe sculpture]
D5-50 Luba Sculpture: Court sculpture of the Luba of the south-eastern DR Congo: caryatid stools, bowl bearers, headrests, lukasa memory boards, staffs of office. [aka Luba art]
D5-51 Lega Sculpture: Ivory, bone and wood figures and masks owned by grades of the Lega bwami association, eastern DR Congo: iginga figurines, lukwakongo masks. [aka bwami art]
D5-52 Ndop: Commemorative portrait figures of the Kuba kings (nyim), each carved with his emblem (ibol), 18th–19th century. [aka Kuba king figures]
D5-53 Makonde Sculpture: Carving of the Makonde of Tanzania and Mozambique: lipiko helmet masks and figures, and from the 1950s the ebony sculpture of Dar es Salaam: mapiko masks, shetani figures, ujamaa trees of life. [aka Makonde art]
D5-54 Fantasy Coffins: Figurative coffins carved by Ga carpenters in Teshie and Nungua near Accra since the 1950s, shaped after the deceased's trade or emblem: fish, cocoa-pod and car coffins. [aka abebuu adekai;figurative coffins]
D5-55 Ancient Egyptian Sculpture: Statuary and relief of pharaonic Egypt in stone, wood and metal, from the Old Kingdom to the Ptolemies: Menkaure triad, Middle Kingdom block statues and tomb models, Amarna reliefs, sunk temple relief. [aka Egyptian statuary;Egyptian relief]
D5-56 Cycladic Figures: Marble figures, mostly folded-arm female figures, made in the Cyclades in the Early Bronze Age, 3rd millennium BC: Spedos and Dokathismata types, harp player from Keros, marble vessels. [aka Cycladic idols;Cycladic figurines]
D5-57 Ancient Greek Sculpture: Stone and bronze sculpture of the Greek world from the Archaic to the Hellenistic period: kouroi and korai, Parthenon marbles, Riace bronzes, Pergamon Altar. [aka Greek sculpture]
D5-58 Greek Terracotta Figurines: Mould-made and painted clay figurines of the Greek world, votive and decorative, Archaic to Hellenistic: Tanagra figurines, Tarentine votive figures, Myrina figurines, Boeotian figures. [aka Tanagra figurines]
D5-59 Etruscan Sculpture: Sculpture of Etruria in terracotta, bronze, stone and alabaster, 7th–1st century BC: Sarcophagus of the Spouses, Apollo of Veii, Chimera of Arezzo, Volterran cinerary urns. [aka Etruscan bronzes]
D5-60 Roman Portraiture: Portrait sculpture of ancient Rome in marble and bronze, from veristic Republican heads to imperial portraits: Patrician Torlonia, Augustus of Prima Porta, Severan and Tetrarchic portraits. [aka Roman portrait sculpture]
D5-61 Roman Sculpture: Roman sculpture other than portraits: historical relief, ideal statuary and copies after Greek works, and carved sarcophagi: Ara Pacis, Trajan's Column, Ludovisi sarcophagus.
D5-62 Olmec Sculpture: The stone sculpture of the Olmec of the Gulf Coast, c. 1500–400 BCE, from monumental basalt carving to small greenstone figures and ceramic hollow babies: colossal heads of San Lorenzo and La Venta, La Venta Altar 4, Las Limas Monument 1. [aka Olmec art]
D5-63 Maya Sculpture: The carved stone and stucco sculpture of the Classic and Postclassic Maya: dated stelae, altars, lintels and panels with rulers and glyphic texts: Copán stelae, Yaxchilan Lintel 24, Piedras Negras Panel 3, Palenque stuccos. [aka Maya stelae]
D5-64 Aztec Sculpture: The stone sculpture of the Mexica of Tenochtitlan, 14th–16th century, from colossal cult images and calendrical monuments to small household deities: Coatlicue statue, Sun Stone, Stone of Tizoc, Coyolxauhqui Stone. [aka Mexica sculpture]
D5-65 Classic Veracruz Sculpture: The sculpture of the Classic-period Gulf Coast (c. 100–1000 CE): carved stone ballgame yokes, hachas and palmas with scrollwork, and hollow ceramic figures: El Tajín ballcourt panels, Remojadas smiling figures, stone yokes. [aka Classic Veracruz art]
D5-66 West Mexican Shaft-Tomb Figures: Hollow and solid ceramic figures and village tableaux placed in shaft tombs of Jalisco, Nayarit and Colima, c. 300 BCE–400 CE: Colima dogs, Nayarit house and ballgame models, Jalisco Ameca figures. [aka shaft tomb tradition;West Mexico ceramics]
D5-67 Chavín Sculpture: The carved granite of Chavín de Huántar and the Chavín horizon, c. 900–200 BCE, with fanged composite deities in contour-rivalry: Lanzón, Raimondi Stele, Tello Obelisk, tenon heads. [aka Chavín art]
D5-68 Valdivia Figurines: Small fired-clay female figures of the Valdivia culture of coastal Ecuador (c. 3500–1500 BCE), among the earliest ceramics of the Americas: Venus of Valdivia. [aka Venus of Valdivia]
D5-69 Estofado: Polychrome wooden sculpture of Spain and Spanish America in which gilded surfaces are painted and scratched to imitate brocade, the technique of viceregal image-makers (imagineros) of Mexico, Guatemala and Quito: Antigua Guatemala saints, Quito sculptural groups. [aka estofado sculpture;imaginería]
D5-70 Brazilian Baroque: The sculpture and church decoration of colonial Brazil, 17th–early 19th century, culminating in the Rococo of Minas Gerais: Aleijadinho's soapstone prophets at Congonhas, Mestre Ataíde's ceilings, Bahia gilded talha. [aka Barroco mineiro;Mineiro Baroque;Baroque in Brazil]
D5-71 Santos: Carved and painted devotional images of saints made by santeros in the Hispanic Caribbean and New Mexico: bultos in the round and painted retablo panels: New Mexican bultos, Puerto Rican santos de palo, Three Kings figures. [aka santo;bulto;santos de palo]
D5-72 Alebrije: Brightly painted fantastic creatures, first made in papier-mâché by Pedro Linares in Mexico City and from the 1980s carved in copal wood by Zapotec carvers of Oaxaca: Arrazola carvings, San Martín Tilcajete tonas. [aka alebrijes;Oaxacan wood carving]
D5-73 Cartonería: Mexican papier-mâché sculpture for festivals: Judas figures burned on Holy Saturday, skeletons for the Day of the Dead and mojigangas: Judas figures, Linares calaveras. [aka cartonería;Judas figures]
D5-74 Caruaru Clay Figures: Small painted clay figures of north-eastern rural life made at Alto do Moura, Caruaru (Pernambuco), after Mestre Vitalino: bands of zabumba players, retirantes, cangaceiros. [aka bonecos de barro;figureiros]
D5-75 Haitian Metal Art: Sculpture cut and hammered from flattened oil drums by the smiths of Croix-des-Bouquets after Georges Liautaud: vodou lwa figures, trees of life, mermaids. [aka fer découpé;Haitian steel drum art]
D5-76 Totem Poles: Monumental carved cedar poles of the Northwest Coast in formline style — memorial, mortuary, house-frontal and welcome poles carrying crests: Haida poles of SGang Gwaay, Tlingit and Tsimshian crest poles, Kwakwaka'wakw poles. [aka totem pole;crest pole]
D5-77 Inuit Sculpture: Figurative carving of the Canadian and Greenlandic Inuit in soapstone, serpentine, ivory and bone, above all the stone sculpture made for sale through co-operatives since the late 1940s. [aka Inuit carving;soapstone carving]
D5-78 Kachina Dolls: Carved and painted cottonwood-root figures of the katsinam given to Hopi (and Zuni) children at ceremonies, from flat cradle dolls to the action figures of the 20th-century market. [aka katsina dolls;katsina figures;tihu]
D5-79 Zuni Fetishes: Small carved stone, shell and antler animal figures of Zuni Pueblo, made for ritual and since the 20th century for collectors, often with inlaid eyes and bundled offerings. [aka Zuni carvings]
D5-80 Storyteller Figures: Painted clay figures of a seated storyteller covered with listening children, created by Helen Cordero at Cochiti Pueblo in 1964 and taken up across the Rio Grande pueblos. [aka storyteller dolls;storyteller pottery]
D5-81 Whakairo: Māori wood carving: the carved figures, panels, gateways and canoe prows of meeting houses (wharenui), storehouses and canoes, cut in regional and tribal schools with manaia, spiral and figure motifs: poupou wall panels, pātaka storehouse carvings, tauihu canoe prows, waka huia treasure boxes. [aka Māori carving;whakairo rākau]
D5-82 Malagan: The carved and painted funerary sculpture of northern New Ireland, made for malagan memorial ceremonies and discarded after display: openwork friezes, figures and masks of softwood with opercula eyes: vavara friezes, totok poles, tatanua masks. [aka malanggan;malangan]
D5-83 Uli: The massive hermaphroditic ancestor figures of central New Ireland (Mandak and Barak), displayed in the long ceremonies of skull reburial: uli figures with raised arms and crested headdresses. [aka uli figures]
D5-84 Asmat Woodcarving: The wood carving of the Asmat of south-west New Guinea (Papua, Indonesia), carvers (wowipits) working in the tradition of the culture hero Fumeripits: bisj ancestor poles, carved shields, canoe prows, ancestor figures. [aka Asmat carving;Asmat art]
D5-85 Sepik Carving: The figure sculpture, masks and ceremonial-house carving of the peoples of the Sepik River basin of Papua New Guinea: Iatmul orator's stools and ancestor figures, Sawos malu boards, Abelam yam masks and ancestor figures. [aka Sepik art;Sepik style]
D5-86 Massim Carving: The curvilinear carving of the Massim region (Milne Bay, PNG), above all the Trobriand Islands, in hardwood incised and filled with lime: tabuya and lagim canoe prow boards for kula voyages, lime spatulas, dance shields. [aka Massim style;Trobriand carving]
D5-87 Nguzu Nguzu: The blackened wood canoe-prow figureheads of New Georgia in the western Solomon Islands, inlaid with nautilus shell, the emblem of the Solomon Islands shell-inlay tradition: nguzu nguzu figureheads, shell-inlaid bowls. [aka nguzunguzu;toto isu;Solomon Islands shell inlay]
D5-88 Marquesan Carving: The carving of the Marquesas Islands (Te Henua ʻEnana) in wood, bone and stone, dense with the eye-and-face (mata) motifs shared with Marquesan tattoo: uʻu clubs, tiki figures, carved bowls and fan handles, bone ear ornaments (taiana). [aka Enata carving]
D5-89 Moai Kavakava: The small wooden figures of Rapa Nui (Easter Island) carved in toromiro, emaciated male ancestor images worn at feasts, with their companion carvings: moai kavakava, moai paepae, rei miro pectorals. [aka Rapa Nui wood carving]
D5-90 Aurukun Sculpture: The ochre-painted softwood sculpture of the Wik and Kugu peoples of Aurukun on Cape York, made for ceremony and since the 1990s through the art centre: Ku' (camp dog) figures, ancestral beings. [aka Wik sculpture;Aurukun camp dogs]
D5-91 Neapolitan Nativity Scene: Elaborate crèches of 18th-century Naples, with articulated figures of terracotta heads on tow bodies, dressed in silk, set among angels, Magi's retinues and street life: Met Neapolitan Baroque Crèche, Presepe Cuciniello. [aka presepe napoletano;Neapolitan crib;Neapolitan crèche]
D5-92 Wildfowl Decoys: Carved and painted wooden decoys of ducks, geese and shorebirds made by North American hunters and carvers, 19th–20th century, collected as folk sculpture: Shang Wheeler's black duck, Elmer Crowell's shorebirds. [aka decoys]
D5-93 Weathervanes: American weathervanes of carved wood and moulded sheet copper, 18th–19th century, and the related whirligigs, collected as folk sculpture: Ames and Dexter heart-and-hand vane, J. W. Fiske and Cushing copper horses. [aka weathervanes and whirligigs]
D5-94 Estremoz Clay Figures: Small modelled and painted clay figures of Estremoz in the Alentejo, made since the 17th century, first for nativity scenes and then of saints and rural types in regional dress: presépios, Primavera, Amor é cego. [aka Bonecos de Estremoz;figurado de barro de Estremoz]
D5-95 Matryoshka Doll: Russian nesting dolls of turned and painted lime wood, first made at the Abramtsevo circle in 1890 (Malyutin, Zvyozdochkin) and then in Sergiev Posad, Semyonov and Polkhovsky Maidan. [aka matryoshka;nesting doll;babushka doll]
D5-96 Polkhovsky Maidan: Turned and brightly painted wooden toys and matryoshkas of Polkhovsky Maidan (Nizhny Novgorod region), with large aniline-coloured flowers over ink outlines. [aka Polkhov-Maidan painting]
D5-97 Bogorodskoye Carving: Carved lime-wood toys and figures of the village of Bogorodskoye near Sergiev Posad, often with moving parts: the Blacksmiths (bear and peasant), pecking hens. [aka Bogorodskoe carving;Bogorodsk wood carving]
D5-98 Dymkovo Toys: Moulded, whitewashed and brightly painted clay figures of the Dymkovo settlement near Vyatka (Kirov): ladies in crinolines, turkeys, water-carriers. [aka Dymkovo toy;Vyatka toy]
D5-99 Filimonovo Toys: Clay whistles and figures of Filimonovo (Tula region) with elongated bodies painted in stripes of red, green and yellow. [aka Filimonovo toy]
D5-100 Kargopol Toys: Clay figures of the Kargopol district (Arkhangelsk region), squat and painted in earth colours: Polkan, bereginya, peasant figures (Babkin family). [aka Kargopol toy]
D5-101 Abashevo Toys: Clay whistles of Abashevo (Penza region) shaped as goats, deer and rams with long necks and horns, glazed or painted. [aka Abashevo toy]
D5-102 Yavoriv Toy: Turned and painted wooden toys of Yavoriv in Lviv Oblast (Ukraine): carts, horses and rattles painted in red, green and black. [aka Yavoriv wooden toy]
D5-103 Valky Whistle Toy: Small ceramic whistle toys of the town of Valky (Kharkiv Oblast, Ukraine). [aka Valky clay whistle]
D5-104 Wooden Toys of Hrvatsko Zagorje: Hand-carved and painted willow and lime-wood toys of Hrvatsko Zagorje (Croatia), sold at pilgrimage fairs (UNESCO 2009). [aka Croatian wooden toys]
D5-105 Pauper Statues: Carved and painted wooden figures of beggars holding alms boxes placed outside Lutheran churches in Finland and Sweden, 18th–19th century. [aka vaivaisukko;poor man statue]
D5-106 Archaic Greek Sculpture: Greek sculpture c. 650–480 BC: kouroi, korai and temple pediments: the New York Kouros, the Peplos Kore, the Aegina pediments.
D5-107 Classical Greek Sculpture: Greek sculpture c. 480–323 BC from the Severe Style to Praxiteles and Lysippos: the Riace bronzes, the Parthenon marbles, Polykleitos's Doryphoros. [aka Severe style]
D5-108 Hellenistic Sculpture: Greek sculpture 323–31 BC, theatrical and realistic: the Pergamon Altar, the Winged Victory of Samothrace, the Laocoön. [aka Hellenistic art]
D5-109 Romanesque Sculpture: Carved portals, capitals and church furnishings of the 11th–12th centuries: Gislebertus's Last Judgement at Autun, Wiligelmo's Modena reliefs, the Pórtico de la Gloria.
D5-110 Gothic Sculpture: Sculpture of the Gothic cathedrals and their workshops (c. 1140–1500): the Chartres Royal Portal, the Naumburg donor figures, Giovanni Pisano's pulpits.
D5-111 Renaissance Sculpture: European sculpture c. 1400–1560 reviving antique form: Donatello's David, Ghiberti's Gates of Paradise, Michelangelo's David. [aka Italian Renaissance sculpture]
D5-112 Baroque Sculpture: Sculpture of the 17th and early 18th centuries, dynamic and theatrical: Bernini's Ecstasy of Saint Teresa, the Fontana dei Quattro Fiumi, Gregorio Fernández's pasos.
D5-113 Neoclassical Sculpture: Sculpture c. 1750–1840 on antique models in white marble: Canova's Psyche Revived by Cupid's Kiss, Thorvaldsen's Jason, Houdon's Voltaire.
D5-114 Academic Sculpture: Salon and public-monument sculpture of the 19th-century academies, Romantic to Beaux-Arts: Rude's La Marseillaise, Carpeaux's The Dance, Bartholdi's Statue of Liberty. [aka Beaux-Arts sculpture]
D5-115 New Sculpture: The British movement (1880s–1910s) of naturalistic, symbolist bronze sculpture: Leighton's Athlete Wrestling with a Python, Gilbert's Eros.
D5-116 Animalier School: 19th-century French sculpture of animals in small bronzes: Barye's Lion Crushing a Serpent, Mêne's hunting groups. [aka Animaliers]
D5-117 Modern Sculpture: Sculpture by artists born c. 1850–1944, from Rodin to abstraction, assemblage and constructed sculpture: Rodin's The Thinker, Brâncuși's Bird in Space, Hepworth's Single Form.
D5-118 Contemporary Sculpture: Sculpture by artists born from 1945, object, figure and installation-based.
D5-119 Kinetic Art: Sculpture that moves or appears to move, from Calder's mobiles to the ZERO group: Calder's Lobster Trap and Fish Tail, Tinguely's Homage to New York.
D5-120 Minimalism: American sculpture of the 1960s of industrial, serial, geometric units: Judd's Untitled stacks, Carl Andre's Equivalent VIII, Dan Flavin's fluorescent works. [aka Minimal art;ABC art]
D5-121 Postminimalism: Sculpture after Minimalism (late 1960s) of process, materials and the body: Eva Hesse's Contingent, Serra's Splash Piece. [aka Process art]
D5-122 Kei School: The Japanese Buddhist sculptors' lineage of Unkei and Kaikei (Kamakura period), realistic in joined-wood technique: the Tōdai-ji Niō, Unkei's Muchaku.
### D6 Ritual Objects & Regalia — Axis: tradition. Carved or assembled objects made for ritual, office and power that are neither masks nor figures (staffs, reliquaries, altars, ceremonial weapons of office).
D6-1 Kaavad: Portable painted wooden shrines of Bassi in Rajasthan whose hinged panels are opened by kaavadiya storytellers: Ram and Krishna kaavads. [aka kavad;kavad shrine]
D6-2 Minkisi: Power objects of the Kongo peoples, figures and containers charged with medicines by a nganga; Songye and Teke power figures are kinds: nkondi nail figures, nkisi in animal form, Songye mankishi. [aka nkisi;nkondi;power figures]
D6-3 Fang Reliquary Guardians: Wooden figures and heads set on bark boxes of ancestral relics by the Fang of Gabon, Cameroon and Equatorial Guinea: eyema byeri figures, byeri heads. [aka byeri;bieri;eyema byeri]
D6-4 Asen: Iron and brass memorial altars of the Fon of Benin (Abomey, Ouidah): a staff topped by a cone with cut-out figures and emblems commemorating the dead. [aka asen altars]
D6-5 Taíno Zemís: Sculptural objects of the Taíno of the Greater Antilles that housed deities and ancestral spirits, in stone, wood, shell and cotton, with the ritual seats and cohoba stands of the same cult: three-pointer stones, Vere zemís of Jamaica, duhos. [aka cemí;zemi]
D6-6 Peruvian Retablo: Portable box altars of Ayacucho (Huamanga) filled with painted plaster and potato-paste figures, from the cajón de San Marcos to scenes of village life: cajón de San Marcos, Joaquín López Antay retablos. [aka retablo ayacuchano;cajón de San Marcos]
D6-7 Ceremonial Pipes: Carved stone pipes of Native North America made for ceremony and diplomacy: Hopewell platform pipes, Adena tubular pipes, Mississippian figural pipes, Iroquoian effigy pipes, catlinite (pipestone) pipes of the Plains. [aka catlinite pipes;pipestone pipes;effigy pipes]
D6-8 Pukumani Poles: The carved and ochre-painted ironwood grave posts (tutini) of the Tiwi Islands, erected round the grave at the Pukumani mourning ceremony and crowned with painted bark baskets: tutini, tunga bark baskets, carved figures. [aka tutini;pukamani poles;Tiwi carving]
D6-9 Toas: Small painted direction-markers of gypsum, wood and found materials made by the Diyari and neighbouring peoples of the Lake Eyre basin about 1904, left to show where a group had gone. [aka toa]
D6-10 Khachkar: Armenian carved memorial stones bearing a cross over a rosette or sun disc, surrounded by lace-like interlace, from the 9th century (Noratus, Julfa): Amenaprkich khachkar. [aka cross-stone;khachkars]
D6-11 Lithuanian Cross Crafting: Lithuanian making of wooden crosses, roofed poles and shrines with carved figures of saints (rūpintojėlis), set at roadsides and farms (UNESCO 2008). [aka Lithuanian cross-crafting;kryždirbystė]

## E Ceramics — test: made of fired clay: pottery, tiles and ceramic objects, placed by their body (fabric) and then their named ware.
  key: 1. Earthenware body (porous, fired low), whether unglazed, slip-decorated, lead-glazed, tin-glazed or lustred? → E1.
  key: 2. Fritware (stonepaste: ground quartz, glass frit and a little clay)? → E2.
  key: 3. Stoneware? → E3. 4. Porcelain? → E4.
  key: Then the named ware; lustre and tin-glaze are techniques inside a body, not sub-families (P4).
### E1 Earthenware — Axis: named ware (decoration technique and centre).
E1-1 Abbasid Tin-Glazed Ware: earthenware with an opaque white tin glaze made in Basra and southern Iraq from the 9th century in imitation of Chinese porcelain, painted in cobalt blue and green (Abbasid blue-and-white). [aka opaque white ware; Basra ware; Abbasid blue-and-white]
E1-2 Abbasid Lustreware: the first lustre pottery: tin-glazed earthenware painted in metallic lustre in 9th–10th-century Iraq, polychrome then monochrome, exported as far as Samarra and Kairouan. [aka Iraqi lustreware; Samarra lustre]
E1-3 Fatimid Lustreware: lustre-painted earthenware of Fatimid Egypt (10th–12th century), often figural, signed by potters such as Muslim ibn al-Dahhan and Sa'd. [aka Egyptian lustreware]
E1-4 Samanid Slipware: earthenware of Nishapur and Samarqand (9th–10th century) painted in coloured slips under a clear glaze, above all the epigraphic ware with Arabic aphorisms on a white ground. [aka Nishapur ware; epigraphic slipware; Samanid epigraphic ware]
E1-5 Splashware: earthenware splashed with green, yellow and brown glazes in imitation of Chinese sancai, made in Iraq and Iran from the 9th century. [aka splashed ware; Islamic sancai imitations]
E1-6 Persian Sgraffito Ware: slip-covered earthenware incised through the slip and glazed, made in Iran and the Caucasus from the 10th to the 13th century (Garrus, Aghkand and Amol wares). [aka Garrus ware; Aghkand ware; Amol ware; sgraffito]
E1-7 Islamic Unglazed Pottery: unglazed earthenware with carved, moulded, stamped or barbotine decoration, above all the pierced filters of water jugs (Egypt, Syria, Iraq, 10th–14th century). [aka jug filters; barbotine ware]
E1-8 Hispano-Moresque Ware: tin-glazed and lustred earthenware of Islamic and Christian Spain (Málaga, Manises, Paterna, Valencia), 13th–16th century, carried to Italy as the model of maiolica. [aka Valencian lustreware; Manises ware; Málaga ware]
E1-9 Çanakkale Pottery: the boldly modelled and splash-painted earthenware of Çanakkale on the Dardanelles (17th–19th century). [aka Chanak ware; Çanakkale ceramics]
E1-10 Fez Pottery: the urban and rural earthenware of Morocco: the blue-and-polychrome tin-glazed wares of Fez, the pottery of Safi and the green glazed ware of Tamegroute. [aka Fassi pottery; Moroccan pottery; Safi pottery; Tamegroute pottery]
E1-11 Kabyle Pottery: hand-built earthenware painted in red and black slips by Kabyle women potters of northern Algeria. [aka Berber pottery; Amazigh pottery]
E1-12 Sejnane Pottery: hand-modelled earthenware of the women of Sejnane in northern Tunisia, inscribed by UNESCO in 2018. [aka pottery skills of the women of Sejnane]
E1-13 Rishton Ceramics: the blue-glazed (ishkor) earthenware of Rishtan and the centres of the Fergana Valley and Bukhara oasis (Gijduvan) in Uzbekistan. [aka Uzbek ceramics; Gijduvan ceramics]
E1-14 Ban Chiang Pottery: Burial earthenware of the Ban Chiang culture of north-east Thailand, its late phase (c. 300 BCE–200 CE) painted with red swirls on buff: carinated jars with spirals, pedestal bowls. [aka Ban Chiang painted pottery]
E1-15 Cham Pottery: Hand-built, open-fired earthenware made by Cham women of Bình Thuận and Ninh Thuận (UNESCO urgent safeguarding 2022): water jars, cooking pots. [aka Art of pottery-making of Chăm people;Bàu Trúc pottery]
E1-16 Kasongan Pottery: Earthenware of Kasongan village near Yogyakarta, from votive animals to large decorated jars.
E1-17 Yangshao Painted Pottery: Hand-built red earthenware painted in black and red of the Neolithic Yellow River cultures (c. 5000–2000 BCE): Banpo fish-and-face basins, Miaodigou bowls, Majiayao and Banshan spiral jars of Gansu. [aka Neolithic Chinese painted pottery;Majiayao pottery;Gansu painted pottery]
E1-18 Jōmon Pottery: Cord-marked earthenware of the Jōmon hunter-gatherers of Japan (c. 14,000–300 BCE), among the oldest pottery in the world: flame-style (kaen) vessels, deep cord-marked jars, and clay dogū figurines. [aka Jomon ware]
E1-19 Yayoi Pottery: Wheel-finished, smooth reddish earthenware of the Yayoi period in Japan (c. 300 BCE–300 CE): storage jars, pedestalled bowls and cooking pots, first found at Yayoi in Tokyo. [aka Yayoi ware]
E1-20 Tang Sancai: Lead-glazed earthenware splashed in amber, green and cream (and rarely cobalt) made in Tang China for tombs and for use: phoenix-head ewers, dishes with stamped designs, camels and horses, and its Liao and Song successors. [aka sancai;three-colour ware]
E1-21 Raku Ware: Hand-modelled, low-fired tea bowls made in Kyoto from the 1570s by Chōjirō and the Raku family for the wabi tea ceremony: black Raku and red Raku bowls, and Hon'ami Kōetsu's bowls. [aka raku-yaki]
E1-22 Satsuma Ware: Pottery of Satsuma province in southern Kyushu founded by Korean potters (from 1598), from dark stoneware (kuro-satsuma) to the crackled ivory earthenware painted in enamels and gold for export in the Meiji era (Kyō-Satsuma, Yokohama-Satsuma). [aka Satsuma-yaki;Satsuma pottery]
E1-23 Onggi: Korean earthenware storage jars glazed with wood-ash and leaf-mould glaze, coil-built and paddled for fermenting kimchi, soy and pastes; master potters designated onggijang. [aka onggijang;Korean earthenware]
E1-24 Kgatleng Pottery: Earthenware pottery made by Bakgatla women potters in the Kgatleng District of Botswana, burnished and decorated (UNESCO urgent safeguarding 2012).
E1-25 Geometric Pottery: Greek painted pottery of c. 900–700 BC with banded meanders and, in its late phase, figural funeral and battle scenes: Dipylon amphora, Hirschfeld Krater, Protogeometric amphorae. [aka Geometric art;Greek Geometric style]
E1-26 Corinthian Vase Painting: Painted pottery of Corinth, 725–550 BC, where black-figure was invented: miniature Protocorinthian aryballoi such as the Chigi olpe, and Corinthian animal-frieze kraters and alabastra. [aka Protocorinthian;Corinthian pottery;Corinthian ware]
E1-27 Black-Figure Pottery: Greek vase painting with figures painted in black slip and incised on the red clay, above all in Attica, c. 620–480 BC: François Vase, Exekias's Achilles and Ajax amphora, Panathenaic prize amphorae. [aka Attic black-figure;black-figure vase painting;Greek vase painting;Greek pottery;Greek vase]
E1-28 Red-Figure Pottery: Greek vase painting with figures reserved in the red clay on a black ground, invented in Athens c. 530 BC and carried to South Italy: Euphronios Krater, Berlin Painter's amphorae, Niobid Krater. [aka Attic red-figure;red-figure vase painting;Greek vase painting;Greek vase]
E1-29 Laconian Vase Painting: Black-figure and earlier painted pottery of Sparta and Laconia, 7th–6th century BC: Arkesilas Cup, cups of the Hunt and Naucratis Painters. [aka Laconian pottery]
E1-30 East Greek Vase Painting: Painted pottery of the Ionian coast and East Aegean islands, 7th–6th century BC: Wild Goat Style oinochoai, Fikellura amphorae, Klazomenian sarcophagi and vases, Chian chalices. [aka Ionian vase painting]
E1-31 South Italian Vase Painting: Red-figure pottery made by Greek workshops of Magna Graecia and Sicily, late 5th–3rd century BC, in its Apulian, Lucanian, Campanian, Paestan and Sicilian fabrics: Darius Vase, Asteas's phlyax kraters. [aka South Italian ancient Greek pottery;Italiote vase painting]
E1-32 Gnathia Vases: Black-glazed pottery of Apulia painted over the glaze in white, yellow and red, c. 370–250 BC: Gnathia skyphoi and oinochoai with vines, masks and garlands. [aka Gnathia ware;Gnathian]
E1-33 Centuripe Ware: Hellenistic Sicilian pottery painted in tempera after firing, with pink grounds and figures in relief, 3rd–2nd century BC: lebetes and lekanides from Centuripe and Morgantina.
E1-34 Etruscan Vase Painting: Painted pottery of Etruria, 7th–3rd century BC, from Etrusco-Corinthian and black-figure to red-figure workshops at Vulci and Falerii: Pontic Group amphorae, Caeretan hydriae, Genucilia plates.
E1-35 Bucchero: The black, burnished, reduction-fired pottery of the Etruscans, 7th–5th century BC, from thin-walled bucchero sottile to moulded heavy bucchero: kantharoi, stamped oinochoai, rhyta. [aka bucchero ware]
E1-36 Minoan Pottery: Painted pottery of Bronze Age Crete, from Vasiliki ware through polychrome Kamares ware to the Marine and Floral Styles: Kamares cups, Octopus flask from Palaikastro, Palace Style jars.
E1-37 Mycenaean Pottery: Painted pottery of Late Bronze Age mainland Greece, c. 1600–1100 BC, traded across the Mediterranean: stirrup jars, pictorial chariot kraters, Warrior Vase. [aka Late Helladic pottery]
E1-38 Terra Sigillata: The glossy red-slipped Roman tableware, plain and mould-decorated, made in Arezzo, Gaul, Germany and North Africa, 1st century BC – 3rd century AD: Arretine ware, South Gaulish samian bowls, African red slip ware. [aka samian ware;Arretine ware]
E1-39 Hadra Vases: Hellenistic cinerary hydriae painted on a light slip, used for burials at the Hadra cemetery of Alexandria, 3rd century BC: Laurel Group and Dolphin Group hydriae. [aka Hadra hydriae]
E1-40 Kerma Ware: The thin, polished, black-topped red pottery of the Kerma kingdom of Upper Nubia, c. 2500–1500 BC: Classic Kerma bell-shaped beakers with a grey band. [aka Kerma beakers;Kerma pottery]
E1-41 Meroitic Pottery: Wheel-made fine ware of the Meroitic kingdom of Kush, 1st century BC – 4th century AD, painted with religious emblems, plants and animals: painted jars and cups from Karanog and Faras. [aka Meroitic painted ware]
E1-42 Nabataean Pottery: The eggshell-thin painted fine ware of the Nabataeans of Petra, 1st century BC – 2nd century AD, decorated with leaves and palmettes: Nabataean painted bowls. [aka Nabataean fine ware]
E1-43 Maya Ceramics: The painted and carved pottery of the Classic Maya, above all the polychrome and codex-style cylinder vases and plates with courtly and mythological scenes and glyphic texts: codex-style vases of the Mirador Basin, Ik'-style vases of Motul de San José, Jaina figurines. [aka Maya polychrome pottery;Maya pottery]
E1-44 Teotihuacan Ceramics: The pottery of Teotihuacan (c. 100–650 CE): cylindrical tripod vessels with painted stucco, Thin Orange ware and composite theatre-type censers: stuccoed tripod vases, Thin Orange effigy vessels, theatre censers. [aka Teotihuacan pottery]
E1-45 Gran Coclé Pottery: The polychrome pottery of central Panama (c. 200–1500 CE) in its named styles, painted with stylised crocodiles, birds and rays: Conté style plates from Sitio Conte, Macaracas pedestal dishes, Parita style. [aka Coclé pottery]
E1-46 Nicoya Polychrome: The white-slipped polychrome pottery of Greater Nicoya (north-western Costa Rica and Nicaragua), c. 800–1500 CE, with Mexican-derived feathered-serpent motifs: Papagayo polychrome vessels, effigy jars. [aka Greater Nicoya pottery]
E1-47 Moche Ceramics: Mould-made and painted stirrup-spout vessels of the Moche of Peru's north coast (c. 100–800 CE): naturalistic portrait heads, effigy vessels and fineline painted narrative scenes: portrait vessels, Presentation Theme fineline vessels. [aka Moche pottery;Moche portrait vessels]
E1-48 Nazca Pottery: Slip-painted polychrome pottery of the Nazca of Peru's south coast (c. 100 BCE–700 CE) in up to twelve colours, with mythical beings and trophy heads: double-spout and bridge bottles, Anthropomorphic Mythical Being bowls. [aka Nasca pottery;Nasca polychrome]
E1-49 Chimú Blackware: The burnished reduction-fired black pottery of the Chimú kingdom of Peru's north coast (c. 900–1470 CE), mould-made in stirrup-spout and effigy forms: stirrup-spout bottles with monkey adornos, double-chambered whistling vessels. [aka Chimú pottery]
E1-50 Shipibo-Conibo Pottery: Pottery painted by Shipibo-Conibo women of the Peruvian Amazon with the maze-like kené designs that also cover their textiles and bodies: chomo jars, quenti cooking pots. [aka kené pottery]
E1-51 Talavera Poblana: Tin-glazed earthenware of Puebla and Tlaxcala made since the 16th century by guild potters after Spanish maiolica, in blue-and-white and polychrome: tibores, lebrillos, azulejos of Puebla façades. [aka Puebla maiolica;Talavera of Puebla]
E1-52 Barro Negro Pottery: Black burnished pottery of San Bartolo Coyotepec (Oaxaca), reduction-fired and polished to a metallic sheen since Doña Rosa in the 1950s: cántaros, openwork vessels. [aka barro negro]
E1-53 Tonalá Pottery: The burnished and painted pottery of Tonalá and Tlaquepaque in Jalisco, in its colonial and modern styles: bruñido, petatillo, bandera, canelo. [aka Ceramics of Jalisco;loza de Tonalá]
E1-54 Mata Ortiz Pottery: Fine-walled painted pottery of Juan Mata Ortiz (Chihuahua) revived by Juan Quezada from the 1970s after Casas Grandes (Paquimé) wares. [aka Paquimé-style pottery]
E1-55 Tree of Life: Multi-branched painted clay candelabra-sculptures of Metepec and Izúcar de Matamoros depicting Eden and other narratives: Adam and Eve trees, Soteno family trees. [aka árbol de la vida]
E1-56 Pucará Bulls: Paired glazed clay bulls of Pucará and Santiago de Pupuja (Puno) set on house roofs for protection and fertility. [aka toritos de Pucará]
E1-57 Chulucanas Pottery: Burnished and smoke-resist decorated pottery of Chulucanas (Piura), revived from Vicús and Tallán techniques, with a denomination of origin. [aka Cerámica de Chulucanas]
E1-58 Quinchamalí Pottery: Black burnished pottery with white incised inlay made by women potters of Quinchamalí and Santa Cruz de Cuca in central Chile (UNESCO urgent safeguarding 2022): guitarrera figure, chanchito alcancía. [aka Quinchamalí and Santa Cruz de Cuca pottery]
E1-59 Awajún Pottery: Pottery made by Awajún women of the Peruvian Amazon with its knowledge and ritual, inscribed by UNESCO in 2021. [aka Awajún ceramics]
E1-60 Goiabeiras Clay Pots: Black clay cooking pots (panelas) of the paneleiras of Goiabeiras in Vitória (Espírito Santo), smoke-blackened with tannin, the first Brazilian intangible heritage registered by IPHAN (2002). [aka panelas de barro de Goiabeiras;craft of the clay pot makers of Goiabeiras]
E1-61 Ancestral Puebloan Pottery: Pottery of the Ancestral Puebloans of the Four Corners (c. 500–1300), above all the painted black-on-white wares and corrugated grey wares: Mesa Verde black-on-white mugs, Chaco cylinder jars, Kayenta and Tusayan wares. [aka Anasazi pottery;black-on-white pottery]
E1-62 Mimbres Pottery: Black-on-white bowls of the Mimbres branch of the Mogollon in south-western New Mexico (c. 1000–1130), painted with figural and geometric designs and buried, 'killed', with the dead. [aka Mimbres black-on-white;Mogollon Mimbres pottery]
E1-63 Hohokam Red-on-Buff Ware: Buff-bodied pottery of the Hohokam of the Sonoran Desert (c. 300–1450) painted in iron-red with geometric, animal and human figures: Sacaton Red-on-buff, Santa Cruz Red-on-buff, Casa Grande Red-on-buff. [aka Hohokam buff ware;Hohokam pottery]
E1-64 Salado Polychrome: Red, white and black painted pottery of the Classic period southern Southwest (c. 1275–1450): Pinto Polychrome, Gila Polychrome, Tonto Polychrome. [aka Roosevelt Red Ware;Salado Red Ware]
E1-65 Casas Grandes Pottery: Polychrome pottery of the Casas Grandes (Paquimé) culture of Chihuahua in its Medio period (c. 1200–1450), with macaws, plumed serpents and effigy forms: Ramos Polychrome, Babícora Polychrome. [aka Paquimé pottery;Ramos Polychrome;Chihuahuan polychromes]
E1-66 Pueblo Pottery: Hand-coiled, slipped and painted pottery of the Pueblo peoples of New Mexico and Arizona from the Pueblo IV period to the present, made by women potters of each pueblo in its own ware: Zuni owls and water jars, Zia and Santo Domingo polychromes, Taos micaceous ware, Tesuque rain gods. [aka Pueblo ceramics]
E1-67 Acoma Pottery: Thin-walled white-slipped pottery of Acoma Pueblo painted in black and orange, from historic water jars with parrots and rainbows to the fine-line revival of Lucy Lewis and Marie Chino. [aka Acoma Pueblo pottery]
E1-68 Hopi Pottery: Yellow-firing pottery of the Hopi mesas, from Jeddito and Sikyátki Polychrome (c. 1400–1625) to the Sikyátki Revival begun by Nampeyo of Hano at First Mesa around 1895 and carried on by Hopi-Tewa potters. [aka Hopi-Tewa pottery;Sikyatki Revival]
E1-69 Black-on-Black Ware: Burnished reduction-fired blackware with matte black designs developed by Maria and Julian Martinez at San Ildefonso Pueblo around 1918, and the polished and carved blackware of Santa Clara Pueblo. [aka San Ildefonso blackware;Santa Clara blackware]
E1-70 Mississippian Pottery: Shell-tempered pottery of the Mississippian cultures of the Eastern Woodlands (c. 800–1600): Cahokia and Moundville engraved bottles, head pots and effigy vessels, Caddo engraved bottles, Lamar complicated-stamped jars. [aka Mississippian culture pottery]
E1-71 Swift Creek Pottery: Woodland-period pottery of Georgia and northern Florida (c. 100–800) paddle-stamped with curvilinear 'complicated stamped' designs carved on wooden paddles. [aka Swift Creek Complicated Stamped]
E1-72 Weeden Island Pottery: Pottery of the Weeden Island culture of Florida and the Gulf coast (c. 200–900): incised and punctated wares and the pierced animal-effigy vessels made for burial mounds (McKeithen effigy vessels). [aka Weeden Island ceramics]
E1-73 Catawba Pottery: Coil-built, burnished and pit-fired earthenware of the Catawba Nation of South Carolina, the oldest continuously practised Native pottery tradition east of the Mississippi. [aka Catawba Indian pottery]
E1-74 Hermannsburg Potters: The hand-built, painted terracotta of the Western Arrernte women of Hermannsburg (Ntaria) since 1990, with modelled animal lids and landscapes painted in underglaze: lidded pots. [aka Ntaria pottery]
E1-75 Maiolica: Tin-glazed earthenware of Renaissance Italy, painted in high-temperature colours, from Florentine and Faentine wares to the istoriato of Urbino and the lustre of Deruta and Gubbio: Deruta lustre dishes, Urbino istoriato plates, albarelli. [aka Italian maiolica]
E1-76 Faience: Tin-glazed earthenware of France and its neighbours from the 16th century, made in named centres: Nevers blue-ground wares, Rouen lambrequin dishes, Moustiers grotesques. [aka French faience;fayence]
E1-77 Delftware: Tin-glazed earthenware of Delft and the Netherlands, 17th–18th century, painted in blue in imitation of Chinese porcelain, and its English counterpart: tulip vases, tile pictures, blue-and-white chargers. [aka Delft Blue;Delft pottery]
E1-78 Saint-Porchaire Ware: Rare French lead-glazed white earthenware of the mid-16th century, inlaid with coloured clays and modelled in Mannerist forms: salts, ewers, candlesticks. [aka Henri II ware]
E1-79 Staffordshire Pottery: The pottery of the Staffordshire Potteries, 17th–19th century: slipware, salt-glazed and creamware tablewares, Wedgwood's jasper and basalt, transfer-printed pearlware and Staffordshire figures: Toft slipware chargers, Wedgwood jasper vases, flatback figures. [aka Staffordshire ware]
E1-80 Pennsylvania German Redware: Lead-glazed red earthenware of the Pennsylvania Germans, 18th–19th century, slip-trailed or sgraffito-decorated with tulips and birds: sgraffito plates, slip-decorated pie dishes. [aka sgraffito redware]
E1-81 Talavera de la Reina Pottery: Tin-glazed earthenware of Talavera de la Reina and El Puente del Arzobispo in Castile from the 16th century, in blue-and-white and polychrome, for tableware, apothecary jars and tile panels (UNESCO 2019, with Puebla): serie tricolor, Puente del Arzobispo green-and-ochre ware. [aka cerámica de Talavera;Talavera ware]
E1-82 Bisalhães Black Pottery: Black earthenware of Bisalhães near Vila Real, burnished and fired in a smothered pit kiln (soenga), for cooking and decoration (UNESCO urgent safeguarding 2016). [aka louça preta de Bisalhães]
E1-83 Gmundner Keramik: Earthenware of Gmunden on the Traunsee, made since the 15th century, best known for its 'flamed' (geflammt) slip decoration in green, blue or brown. [aka Gmunden ceramics;Gmundner Geschirr]
E1-84 Émaux de Longwy: Faience of Longwy in Lorraine decorated since the 1870s with coloured enamels filled into printed outlines in the manner of cloisonné. [aka Longwy enamels;Longwy faience]
E1-85 Izraztsy: Russian glazed and relief stove and wall tiles from the 16th to the 19th century — terracotta, polychrome 'peacock-eye' relief tiles and painted Dutch-style tiles — for tiled stoves and church façades. [aka Russian stove tiles;Russian tiled stoves]
E1-86 Skopin Ceramics: Glazed earthenware of Skopin (Ryazan region) since the 19th century, with fantastic sculptural vessels in the form of birds, beasts and dragons under green and brown glazes. [aka Skopin pottery]
E1-87 Balkhar Pottery: Hand-built earthenware of the Lak village of Balkhar (Dagestan), made by women and painted in white engobe with free linear ornament. [aka Balkhar ceramics]
E1-88 Opishnia Ceramics: The pottery of Opishnia (Poltava Oblast, Ukraine) with painted engobe ornament and sculptural vessels and toys (barany, lions). [aka Opishnia decorative ceramics;Opishne pottery]
E1-89 Kosiv Painted Ceramics: Engobe-painted earthenware of Kosiv and the Hutsul region (Ukraine), with green, yellow and brown sgraffito-painted scenes of Hutsul life on a white ground (UNESCO 2019). [aka Kosiv ceramics]
E1-90 Sokal Ceramics: Traditional painted ceramics of Sokal in Lviv Oblast (Ukraine).
E1-91 Bubnivka Ceramics: Painted earthenware of Bubnivka (Vinnytsia Oblast, Ukraine), with dense floral ornament. [aka Bubnivka painted ceramics]
E1-92 Oleshnia Pottery: Traditional pottery of Oleshnia in Chernihiv Oblast (Ukraine).
E1-93 Horezu Ceramics: Slip-painted earthenware of Horezu in Oltenia (Romania), decorated with the cock, star and spiral in brown, red, green and blue on cream (UNESCO 2012). [aka Horezu pottery]
E1-94 Zlakusa Pottery: Unglazed hand-wheel pottery of the village of Zlakusa in western Serbia, of clay mixed with calcite, for cooking pots (UNESCO 2020). [aka Zlakusa pottery making]
### E2 Fritware (Stonepaste) — Axis: named ware.
E2-1 Seljuk Pottery: fritware of Seljuq Iran and its neighbours (11th–13th century) other than its named lustre, mina'i and lajvardina wares: monochrome and carved, silhouette, laqabi and underglaze-painted wares. [aka Seljuq pottery; monochrome ware; silhouette ware; laqabi ware]
E2-2 Kashan Lustreware: lustre-painted fritware of Kashan (late 12th–14th century) — vessels and the star, cross and mihrab tiles of Iranian shrines — signed by potter families such as Abu Tahir and Abu Zayd. [aka Kashan ware; Persian lustreware]
E2-3 Mina'i Ware: overglaze-painted fritware of Kashan (late 12th–early 13th century), with figural scenes in seven colours. [aka haft-rangi ware]
E2-4 Lajvardina: Ilkhanid fritware with a cobalt-blue (lajvard) or turquoise glaze overpainted in white, red and gold leaf (late 13th–14th century). [aka Lajvard ware]
E2-5 Raqqa Ware: fritware of Raqqa and Syria (12th–13th century), lustred or underglaze-painted in black under a turquoise glaze. [aka Raqqa pottery; Syrian fritware]
E2-6 Sultanabad Ware: Ilkhanid fritware of the late 13th–14th century with a grey-green slip and Chinese-inspired lotus and animal designs. [aka Sultanabad pottery]
E2-7 Iznik Pottery: the fritware of Iznik in Ottoman Anatolia (late 15th–17th century) — vessels and tiles in its successive styles: blue-and-white (Abraham of Kütahya), Golden Horn, Damascus and Rhodian with its bole red. [aka Iznik ware; Iznik tiles; Rhodian ware]
E2-8 Damascus Tiles: Ottoman-period fritware and tiles made in Damascus (16th–17th century), painted in blues, green and manganese without Iznik's red. [aka Damascus ware (Syrian); Syrian Ottoman pottery]
E2-9 Kubachi Ware: Safavid fritware of north-western Iran (16th–17th century) with crackled glaze, found in the village of Kubachi in Daghestan. [aka Kubachi pottery]
E2-10 Safavid Pottery: the fritware of Safavid Iran (16th–17th century) other than Kubachi ware: Kirman and Mashhad blue-and-white in the manner of Chinese porcelain, celadon imitations and late lustre. [aka Kirman ware; Persian blue-and-white; Gombroon ware]
E2-11 Kütahya Ceramics: the fritware of Kütahya in western Anatolia (18th–19th century), small vessels and tiles often made by Armenian potters for church and household. [aka Kutahya ware]
E2-12 Qajar Ceramics: the tiles and vessels of Qajar Iran (19th century): moulded and painted figural tiles of Tehran and Shiraz (Ali Muhammad Isfahani) and revival lustre. [aka Qajar tiles]
E2-13 Qallaline Tiles: the tin-glazed tiles of the Qallaline quarter of Tunis (17th–19th century), with Ottoman and Italian floral designs. [aka Tunisian tiles; qallalin]
E2-14 Jaipur Blue Pottery: Quartz-paste (fritware) vessels and tiles of Jaipur painted in cobalt and turquoise under a clear glaze, from the 19th century: surahis, tiles. [aka Blue Pottery of Jaipur]
E2-15 Egyptian Faience: Glazed sintered-quartz ware of ancient Egypt, blue and green, used for small sculpture, amulets, vessels and inlay: scarabs, ushabti figures, blue hippopotamus figurines, lotus chalices. [aka tjehenet;glazed composition]
### E3 Stoneware — Axis: named ware. Built at scale.
E3-1 Khmer Ceramics: The glazed stoneware of the Angkorian Khmer kilns of Cambodia and north-east Thailand (9th–14th century), in green and brown glazes: baluster jars, animal-form lime pots, roof finials. [aka Angkorian ceramics]
E3-2 Sawankhalok Ware: Stoneware of the Si Satchanalai kilns in north-central Thailand (14th–16th century), celadon, brown and underglaze-black, exported widely to the archipelago: celadon dishes, covered boxes, figurines. [aka Si Satchanalai ware]
E3-3 Vietnamese Ceramics: Glazed stoneware of Vietnam from the Lý and Trần dynasties to the Lê export trade: Lý–Trần white and brown wares, Chu Đậu blue-and-white, Bát Tràng wares. [aka Annamese ceramics]
E3-4 Yue Ware: Grey-green celadon stoneware of Zhejiang (Eastern Han to Song), centred on Shanglin Lake, the first great Chinese celadon: chicken-head ewers, Five Dynasties mise (secret colour) ware made for the Wuyue court. [aka Yue celadon;mise;secret-colour ware]
E3-5 Yaozhou Ware: Northern celadon of Tongchuan in Shaanxi (Tang to Yuan), olive-green glaze over crisply carved and moulded peonies and waves. [aka Northern celadon;Yaozhou celadon]
E3-6 Longquan Celadon: Celadon stoneware of the Longquan kilns in southern Zhejiang (Song to Ming), in thick bluish-green glazes and exported across Asia (UNESCO 2009): kinuta vases, mallet vases, large Ming dishes. [aka Longquan ware;kinuta seiji]
E3-7 Ru Ware: The rare celadon made for the Northern Song court at Qingliangsi in Henan (c. 1086–1125), with a crackled duck-egg-blue glaze; fewer than a hundred pieces survive. [aka Ru yao]
E3-8 Guan Ware: Official ware of the Southern Song court at Hangzhou (Xiuneisi and Jiaotanxia kilns), thin dark-bodied stoneware with thick crackled glaze. [aka Guan yao;official ware]
E3-9 Jun Ware: Stoneware of Henan (Song to Yuan) with an opalescent lavender-blue glaze splashed with copper purple: flowerpots and stands, bowls and dishes. [aka Jun yao;Chün ware]
E3-10 Jian Ware: Dark-glazed tea bowls of Jianyang in Fujian (Song), prized in Japan as tenmoku: hare's fur, oil-spot and yōhen bowls. [aka tenmoku;temmoku;Jian tea bowls]
E3-11 Cizhou Ware: Popular stoneware of northern China (Song to Ming) decorated through white slip by sgraffito, carving and black painting: pillows, meiping vases, jars painted with fish and peonies. [aka Cizhou yao]
E3-12 Yixing Ware: Unglazed purple-clay (zisha) stoneware of Yixing in Jiangsu (from the 16th century), above all small teapots by named potters such as Gong Chun and Shi Dabin. [aka zisha;Yixing clay;purple sand ware]
E3-13 Goryeo Celadon: The celadon stoneware of Goryeo Korea (10th–14th century) from the kilns of Gangjin and Buan, with jade-green glaze and inlaid (sanggam) decoration: maebyeong vases, kundika, incense burners. [aka Goryeo ware;Korean celadon;sanggam celadon]
E3-14 Buncheong: Grey stoneware of early Joseon Korea (15th–16th century) covered in white slip that is stamped, inlaid, incised, painted or brushed: mishima, hakeme and kohiki bowls prized in Japan. [aka punch'ong;buncheong sagi]
E3-15 Seto Ware: Glazed stoneware of Seto in Owari, Japan's only glazed medieval kiln (from the 13th century), imitating Chinese celadon and tenmoku, and the tea wares of the neighbouring Mino kilns in the Momoyama period: Ko-Seto jars, Ki-Seto, Setoguro bowls. [aka Seto-yaki;setomono]
E3-16 Shino Ware: Momoyama-period tea ware of the Mino kilns with a thick, pitted white feldspar glaze, sometimes painted in iron: e-Shino, nezumi (grey) Shino, the Unohanagaki tea bowl. [aka Shino-yaki]
E3-17 Oribe Ware: Mino stoneware of the early 17th century named for the tea master Furuta Oribe, with copper-green glaze and boldly painted iron designs in asymmetric forms: kutsu (clog) tea bowls, fan-shaped dishes. [aka Oribe-yaki]
E3-18 Bizen Ware: Unglazed, wood-fired stoneware of Imbe in Okayama, one of the Six Ancient Kilns, with fire-marks (hidasuki, goma): tea-ceremony water jars, hanaire flower vases. [aka Bizen-yaki;Imbe ware]
E3-19 Shigaraki Ware: Wood-fired stoneware of Shigaraki in Shiga, one of the Six Ancient Kilns, with feldspar inclusions and natural ash glaze: storage jars and tea wares. [aka Shigaraki-yaki]
E3-20 Iga Ware: Wood-fired stoneware of Iga in Mie, close to Shigaraki, with heavy ash glaze and distorted forms favoured for Momoyama tea vases and water jars. [aka Iga-yaki]
E3-21 Tamba Ware: Stoneware of Tachikui in Hyōgo, one of the Six Ancient Kilns: ash-glazed storage jars and, from the Edo period, slip and glaze wares. [aka Tamba-yaki;Tamba Tachikui ware]
E3-22 Tokoname Ware: Stoneware of the Chita peninsula in Aichi, the largest of the Six Ancient Kilns: large medieval storage jars and later red-clay teapots. [aka Tokoname-yaki]
E3-23 Echizen Ware: Stoneware of Fukui, one of the Six Ancient Kilns, descended from Tokoname techniques: ash-glazed storage jars and mortars. [aka Echizen-yaki]
E3-24 Karatsu Ware: Stoneware of northern Kyushu begun by Korean potters in the late 16th century, iron-painted (e-Garatsu) and glaze-patterned for tea and daily use. [aka Karatsu-yaki]
E3-25 Hagi Ware: Soft stoneware of Hagi in Yamaguchi founded by Korean potters (1604), with pale crackled glazes that change with use, chiefly tea bowls. [aka Hagi-yaki]
E3-26 Takatori Ware: Stoneware of Fukuoka founded by the Korean potter Hachizō (early 17th century), refined tea caddies and bowls with flowing glazes for the Enshū taste. [aka Takatori-yaki]
E3-27 Agano Ware: Stoneware of Fukuchi in Fukuoka founded by Korean potters in the early 17th century, with green copper and iron glazes; one of Kobori Enshū's seven kilns. [aka Agano-yaki]
E3-28 Kyō Ware: The pottery of Kyoto from the 17th century, overglaze-enamelled and painterly, by named potters and kilns: Nonomura Ninsei's enamelled tea jars, Ogata Kenzan's painted dishes, Awata and Kiyomizu wares. [aka Kyō-yaki;Kyoto ware;Kyō-Kiyomizu ware]
E3-29 Onta Ware: Folk stoneware of a hamlet in Hita, Ōita, made by a few families since 1705 with slip, combed (tobiganna) and poured decoration; championed by the Mingei movement. [aka Onta-yaki]
E3-30 Koishiwara Ware: Folk stoneware of Tōhō, Fukuoka (from the 17th century), the parent kiln of Onta, decorated with chattering (tobiganna) and brushed slip. [aka Koishiwara-yaki]
E3-31 Mumyōi Ware: High-fired red stoneware of Sado island made from iron-rich mumyōi clay, burnished and fired to a deep red-to-black (from the 19th century). [aka Sado Mumyōi ware]
E3-32 Rhenish Stoneware: Salt-glazed stoneware of the Rhineland centres (Siegburg, Raeren, Frechen, Cologne, Westerwald), 15th–19th century, exported across Europe and its colonies: Bartmann (Bellarmine) jugs, Westerwald blue-and-grey tankards. [aka German stoneware;Rhenish salt-glazed stoneware]
### E4 Porcelain — Axis: named ware. Built at scale.
E4-1 Bencharong: Porcelain made in China to Thai order and enamelled in five colours on a black or coloured ground for the Ayutthaya and Bangkok courts: covered bowls, thepanom-figure jars. [aka benjarong]
E4-2 Ding Ware: Thin ivory-white porcelain of Quyang in Hebei (Tang to Yuan), fired upside-down with unglazed rims bound in metal, with carved and moulded lotus designs. [aka Ding yao]
E4-3 Xing Ware: White porcelain of Hebei (Tang), the first fine Chinese white ware, paired in Tang texts with Yue celadon. [aka Xing yao]
E4-4 Qingbai Ware: Bluish-white porcelain of Jingdezhen and southern China (Song and Yuan), translucent with a pale blue glaze pooling in carved designs. [aka yingqing;shadow blue]
E4-5 Chinese Blue-and-White Porcelain: Porcelain painted in cobalt under the glaze, made at Jingdezhen from the Yuan dynasty: Yuan large dishes, Ming imperial Xuande and Chenghua wares, Transitional and Kangxi porcelain. [aka qinghua;Jingdezhen blue-and-white]
E4-6 Doucai: 'Contending colours': underglaze-blue outlines filled with overglaze enamels, perfected at Jingdezhen under the Chenghua emperor (1465–87): chicken cups, Yongzheng doucai dishes. [aka joined colours]
E4-7 Wucai: 'Five-colour' porcelain of Jingdezhen (Jiajing and Wanli reigns) combining underglaze blue with red, green and yellow overglaze enamels, the precursor of famille verte. [aka five-colour porcelain]
E4-8 Famille Verte: Kangxi-period (1662–1722) Jingdezhen porcelain painted in overglaze enamels dominated by greens, with yellow-ground (famille jaune) and black-ground (famille noire) variants. [aka kangxi wucai]
E4-9 Famille Rose: Porcelain painted in opaque pink and other overglaze enamels developed from European enamel in the 1720s: falangcai palace wares, yangcai, and export Rose Medallion and Rose Mandarin. [aka fencai;yangcai]
E4-10 Jingdezhen Porcelain: The porcelain of Jingdezhen's imperial and private kilns other than its named decorative wares: monochromes (peachbloom, robin's-egg, tea-dust), anhua hidden decoration, underglaze red and the imperial reign-marked wares of the Ming and Qing. [aka Jingdezhen ware;Chinese porcelain;Chinese vase]
E4-11 Kraak Ware: Thin Jingdezhen blue-and-white export porcelain of the Wanli reign and after (c. 1570–1650), with panelled borders, shipped in quantity by the Portuguese and Dutch. [aka kraak porcelain;carrack porcelain]
E4-12 Swatow Ware: Coarse export porcelain of the Zhangzhou kilns in Fujian (late 16th–17th century), blue-and-white and enamelled, traded to Southeast Asia and Japan. [aka Zhangzhou ware]
E4-13 Chinese Export Porcelain: Porcelain made in China for European and American markets (16th–19th century) to order or in export styles: armorial services, Jesuit ware, Canton famille rose (Rose Medallion), Chine de commande. [aka China trade porcelain;Chine de commande]
E4-14 Dehua Porcelain: White porcelain of Dehua in Fujian (Ming and Qing), with an ivory glaze, known in Europe as blanc de Chine: Guanyin figures by He Chaozong, libation cups. [aka blanc de Chine;Dehua ware]
E4-15 Joseon White Porcelain: The white porcelain (baekja) of the Joseon court kilns at Gwangju (15th–19th century), plain or painted in underglaze cobalt, iron and copper: moon jars, blue-and-white dragon jars. [aka baekja;Korean white porcelain]
E4-16 Arita Ware: Porcelain of Arita in Hizen (Kyushu) from about 1616, shipped from Imari: shoki-Imari blue-and-white, Ko-Imari gold-and-red export ware, Dutch East India Company commissions. [aka Imari ware;Hizen porcelain]
E4-17 Kakiemon: Arita porcelain enamelled in the style of the Sakaida Kakiemon family (from the 1640s), sparse asymmetric designs on a milk-white body (nigoshide), copied at Meissen and Chantilly. [aka Kakiemon ware]
E4-18 Nabeshima Ware: Porcelain of the Nabeshima clan kiln at Ōkawachi (late 17th–19th century), made for gifts to the shogun: footed dishes with underglaze blue and enamel designs. [aka Nabeshima-yaki;Iro-Nabeshima]
E4-19 Hirado Ware: Fine white porcelain of Mikawachi near Hirado (18th–19th century), painted in soft blue and finely modelled, often with Chinese boys under pines. [aka Mikawachi ware]
E4-20 Kutani Ware: Overglaze-enamelled porcelain associated with Kutani in Kaga: Ko-Kutani (17th century) in bold green, yellow and purple, and the 19th-century revival kilns (Yoshidaya, Eiraku). [aka Kutani-yaki;Ko-Kutani]
E4-21 Limoges Porcelain: Hard-paste porcelain made at Limoges from the discovery of kaolin nearby (1768) to the industrial factories of the 19th–20th century (Haviland).
E4-22 Gzhel: Ceramics of the Gzhel district near Moscow, from 18th-century majolica and 19th-century semi-faience to the cobalt blue-and-white porcelain of the 20th century. [aka Gzhel ceramics;Gzhel porcelain]

## F Glass — test: made of glass, placed by how it is formed and decorated.
  key: 1. Decorated by cutting or carving? → F2. 2. Painted with enamel or gilding? → F3. 3. Lustre-painted? → F4. 4. Window or stained glass? → F5. 5. Otherwise blown or moulded glass → F1.
### F1 Blown & Moulded Glass — Axis: workshop tradition.
F1-1 Beykoz Glass: Ottoman glass of the Beykoz and Istanbul workshops (late 18th–19th century), above all the çeşm-i bülbül ('nightingale's eye') ware with spiral white and coloured threads. [aka çeşm-i bülbül]
F1-2 Syrian Glassblowing: the free-blown glass of the Damascus and Hebron workshops, inscribed by UNESCO in 2023 as a heritage in need of urgent safeguarding. [aka Damascus glass; Hebron glass]
F1-3 Roman Glass: Blown, mould-blown, cast and cut glass of the Roman empire, 1st century BC – 5th century AD, made in Syria, Italy, Egypt and the Rhineland: Ennion's mould-blown cups, Portland Vase, Lycurgus cage cup.
F1-4 Hellenistic Glass: Core-formed, cast and mosaic glass of the Greek world and the Eastern Mediterranean, 6th–1st century BC: core-formed alabastra and amphoriskoi, cast bowls, gold-sandwich glass. [aka core-formed glass;Eastern Mediterranean glass]
F1-5 Venetian Glass: The glass of Venice and Murano from the 15th century: clear cristallo, enamelled and gilded wares, filigrana (latticino), ice glass and chandeliers: Barovier wedding cup, filigrana goblets, façon de Venise. [aka Murano glass;façon de Venise]
F1-6 Art of Glass Beads: Venetian and French lampwork and drawn glass beadmaking (perle veneziane), inscribed by UNESCO in 2020 for Italy and France. [aka perle di vetro;perle veneziane;Murano beads]
F1-7 Czech Blown Glass Bead Christmas Decorations: Christmas tree ornaments made by hand from blown glass beads in the Poniklá region of the Czech Republic, silvered, coloured and threaded (UNESCO 2020). [aka Rautis decorations;handmade production of Christmas tree decorations from blown glass beads]
F1-8 Līvāni Glassblowing: The glassblowing craft of Līvāni in Latvia, centre of Latvian glassmaking since 1887. [aka glassblowing craft skills in Līvāni]
### F2 Cut & Carved Glass — Axis: workshop tradition.
F2-1 Islamic Cut Glass: glass decorated by wheel-cutting and relief-carving in Iran, Iraq and Egypt (9th–11th century), from facet-cut bowls to relief-cut cameo glass and the Hedwig glasses. [aka relief-cut glass; Hedwig glasses]
F2-2 Bohemian Glass: Glass of Bohemia from the late 17th century, potash-lime crystal decorated by wheel-engraving and cutting, later with coloured and cased glass: engraved goblets, Zwischengoldglas, Biedermeier cased glass. [aka Bohemian crystal]
### F3 Enamelled & Gilded Glass — Axis: workshop tradition.
F3-1 Mamluk Enamelled Glass: enamelled and gilded glass of Ayyubid and Mamluk Syria and Egypt (13th–14th century): mosque lamps, beakers and bottles with blazons and inscriptions. [aka Ayyubid-Mamluk enamelled glass; mosque lamps]
### F4 Lustre-Painted Glass — Axis: workshop tradition.
F4-1 Islamic Lustre-Painted Glass: glass stained with metallic lustre in Egypt, Syria and Iraq (8th–11th century), the predecessor of lustre pottery. [aka lustre-stained glass]
### F5 Stained Glass — Axis: school. Built at scale.

## G Metalwork, Arms & Jewellery — test: made of metal: vessels and objects, instruments, arms and armour, and jewellery.
  key: 1. Worn as ornament? → G3. 2. A weapon or armour? → G2. 3. Otherwise vessels, objects and instruments of base or precious metal, cast, raised, engraved or inlaid → G1.
### G1 Metalwork — Axis: workshop tradition (named school or technique).
G1-1 Khurasan Metalwork: cast and inlaid brass of eastern Iran and Herat (12th–13th century), with silver and copper inlay and figural bands (the Bobrinsky Bucket, 1163). [aka Herat metalwork; Seljuq inlaid metalwork]
G1-2 Mosul School of Metalwork: inlaid brass made by craftsmen with the nisba al-Mawsili in Mosul, Syria and Egypt (13th century), the model for Ayyubid and Mamluk inlay. [aka Mosul metalwork; al-Mawsili inlay]
G1-3 Mamluk Metalwork: inlaid brass and bronze of Mamluk Egypt and Syria (1250–1517) — basins, candlesticks, Qur'an boxes — with epigraphic blazons (the Baptistère de Saint Louis by Muhammad ibn al-Zayn). [aka Mamluk inlaid brass]
G1-4 Veneto-Saracenic Metalwork: inlaid brass made in the Islamic manner in the 15th–16th century and long thought Venetian, now mostly given to Mamluk and Iranian workshops. [aka Veneto-Saracenic metalwork]
G1-5 Ottoman Tombak: fire-gilded copper of the Ottoman court (17th–19th century): ewers, basins, censers and mosque fittings. [aka tombak; gilt copper]
G1-6 Bidri Ware: zinc-alloy objects inlaid with silver and blackened, from Bidar in the Deccan (from the 14th century). [aka Bidriware]
G1-7 Koftgari: damascening of steel with gold and silver wire, practised in Mughal and Rajput India and in Iran for arms and objects. [aka damascening; tah-nishan]
G1-8 Islamic Astrolabes: brass astrolabes and celestial globes of the Islamic world, engraved instruments made by named makers in Baghdad, Isfahan, Lahore and al-Andalus. [aka astrolabes; celestial globes]
G1-9 Firoozeh Koobi: Iranian metal objects set with turquoise chips in a mosaic over the surface (Isfahan, Mashhad). [aka turquoise inlay]
G1-10 Persian Metalwork: inlaid and engraved brass and steel of Iran after the Mongols: Inju Fars inlay (14th century), Timurid and Safavid tinned-brass jugs, candlesticks and inkwells with poetic inscriptions, and Qajar cut steel (Hajji 'Abbas). [aka Timurid metalwork; Safavid metalwork; Qajar steel]
G1-11 Sogdian Silver: Chased, engraved and gilded silver vessels of the Sogdian city-states (6th–9th century), many found in the Urals and Siberia: cups with roundels of ibexes, bowls with banquet and hunting scenes. [aka Sogdian silverware;Sogdian metalwork]
G1-12 Animal Style: The art of the Eurasian steppe nomads (Scythian, Saka, Sarmatian, Ordos, Xiongnu), 9th century BCE–3rd century CE, in which stylised and contorted animals cover gold and bronze plaques, harness and weapons: curled-feline plaques, Arzhan deer, Ordos belt buckles. [aka Scythian animal style;steppe art;Scytho-Siberian art]
G1-13 Dong Son Drums: Bronze kettle drums of the Đông Sơn culture of northern Vietnam (c. 500 BCE–300 CE), cast with star-centred tympana, frogs and processions, traded across the archipelago: Ngọc Lũ drum, Hoàng Hạ drum, Makalamau of Sangeang. [aka Đông Sơn drums;Heger type I drums]
G1-14 Burmese Silver: Repoussé and chased silver of Burma, 19th–early 20th century, with crowded narrative and zodiac friezes: offering bowls, betel boxes, presentation cups. [aka Burmese silverwork]
G1-15 Chinese Ritual Bronzes: Bronze vessels and bells cast in piece-moulds for ancestral rites from the Erligang and Shang to the Han: ding cauldrons, gu and jue wine vessels, taotie masks, inscribed Western Zhou gui. [aka Shang bronzes;Zhou bronzes]
G1-16 Ordos Bronzes: Cast bronze belt plaques, knives and fittings in the steppe animal style from the Ordos and northern frontier of China (6th–1st century BCE). [aka Ordos culture bronzes]
G1-17 Yugi: Korean bronze tableware forged or cast from high-tin bronze (bangjja yugi), of Anseong and Napcheong; makers designated yugijang. [aka bangjja yugi;Korean brassware;yugijang]
G1-18 Ipsa: Korean inlay of silver and gold wire into iron and bronze (Goryeo to Joseon): incense burners, kundika and furniture fittings; makers designated ipsajang. [aka ipsajang;Korean metal inlay]
G1-19 Ethiopian Crosses: Processional, hand and pectoral crosses of the Ethiopian Orthodox Church in cast brass, silver and carved wood, with openwork lattices: Lalibela processional crosses, Gondar crosses, hand crosses. [aka Ethiopian processional crosses]
