G1-20 Akan Goldweights: Small brass weights cast by lost wax by the Akan for weighing gold dust, geometric and figurative (proverbs): abrammuo, with kuduo vessels and forowa boxes of the same casters. [aka abrammuo;mrammou]
G1-21 Igbo-Ukwu Bronzes: Leaded-bronze vessels and regalia cast by lost wax at Igbo-Ukwu (9th–10th century), with dense granular surfaces: roped pot, shell-shaped bowl, pendant ornaments.
G1-22 Luristan Bronzes: Cast bronze horse-bits, finials, standards, pins and weapons from the Zagros mountains of western Iran, early 1st millennium BC: master-of-animals finials, cheekpieces with winged creatures, votive pins. [aka Lorestan bronzes]
G1-23 Achaemenid Metalwork: Gold and silver vessels and ornaments of the Persian empire, 6th–4th century BC: gold fluted bowls and rhyta, Oxus Treasure, griffin-handled amphorae. [aka Achaemenid goldwork]
G1-24 Sasanian Silver: Silver and silver-gilt vessels of Sasanian Iran, 3rd–7th century, hammered and chased with royal hunts and Dionysiac scenes: royal hunting plates, ewers with dancing figures, high-footed bowls. [aka Sasanian metalwork]
G1-25 Etruscan Mirrors: Bronze hand mirrors of Etruria and Latium with mythological scenes engraved on the back, 6th–2nd century BC, together with the engraved cistae of Praeneste: mirror with Minerva and Hercules, Ficoroni Cista. [aka Etruscan mirror;Praenestine cistae]
G1-26 Moche Metalwork: Gold, silver and gilded-copper objects of the Moche, worked by depletion gilding and inlaid with turquoise and shell: Sipán ear ornaments, backflaps, nose ornaments.
G1-27 Sicán Metalwork: Gold and tumbaga objects of the Sicán (Lambayeque) culture of Peru's north coast (c. 900–1100 CE): Sicán funerary masks, tumi knives, beakers. [aka Lambayeque metalwork]
G1-28 Chimú Metalwork: Hammered and repoussé silver and gold of the Chimú kingdom (c. 900–1470 CE): beakers, ear spools, feathered tunics with plaques. [aka Chimú silver]
G1-29 Inca Metalwork: Gold and silver of the Inca empire (c. 1400–1532): cast and hammered figurines from capacocha offerings, keros and ornaments: silver llamas, gold female figurines.
G1-30 Muisca Goldwork: Lost-wax tumbaga castings of the Muisca of the Colombian highlands (c. 600–1600 CE), above all the votive tunjos: Muisca raft, tunjo figures. [aka tunjos]
G1-31 Colonial Andean Silverwork: The silver of the Viceroyalty of Peru (16th–19th century) from Potosí, Cusco and Lima: church plate, sahumadores, and the domestic and equestrian silver that continued in the Río de la Plata: incense burners in animal form, mate cups, altar frontals. [aka Peruvian viceregal silver]
G1-32 La Tène Art: The curvilinear art of the Iron Age Celts, c. 450 BC to the Roman conquest, chiefly in bronze and gold metalwork: Basse-Yutz flagons, Waldalgesheim style torcs, Battersea Shield. [aka La Tène style;early Celtic art]
G1-33 Migration Period Art: The metalwork of the Germanic peoples, c. 300–800, in polychrome garnet cloisonné and the interlaced animal styles I and II: brooches and fibulae, Lombard gold-foil crosses, Visigothic votive crowns of Guarrazar. [aka Germanic animal style;Migration art]
G1-34 Anglo-Saxon Metalwork: Metalwork of Anglo-Saxon England, 5th–11th century, from garnet-cloisonné and filigree gold to Trewhiddle-style silver: Sutton Hoo great buckle, Staffordshire Hoard, Fuller Brooch.
G1-35 Viking Art: The animal-ornament art of Scandinavia and its settlements, c. 750–1100, in its succession of styles named after finds (Oseberg, Borre, Jelling, Mammen, Ringerike, Urnes), chiefly in metal and wood: Oseberg ship carvings, Mammen axe, Urnes stave church portal. [aka Norse art]
G1-36 South Staffordshire Enamels: English painted enamel on copper of c. 1750–1800 from Bilston, Wednesbury, Birmingham and London (popularly 'Battersea enamels'): snuffboxes, étuis, patch boxes, cassolettes. [aka Battersea enamels;Bilston enamels]
G1-37 Ormolu: Fire-gilt bronze of 17th–19th-century France and its imitators, cast and chased for furniture mounts, clocks and candelabra, and for mounting Chinese and Japanese porcelain: mounted porcelain vases, Gouthière candelabra. [aka gilt-bronze mounts;bronze doré]
G1-38 Kubachi Silver: Silver and metalwork of the Dargin village of Kubachi in Dagestan: engraved, niello and filigree vessels, arms and jewellery with dense floral ornament. [aka Kubachi metalwork]
G1-39 Kasli Iron Casting: Cast-iron sculpture and openwork made at the Kasli works in the Urals since the 19th century: Kasli cast-iron pavilion (Paris 1900), statuettes of horsemen and animals. [aka Kasli casting]
G1-40 Finift: Painted enamel on copper of Russia, above all of Rostov the Great from the 18th century: icons, medallions and jewellery painted in enamel colours. [aka Rostov finift;Rostov enamel]
G1-41 Hutsul Metalwork: The brass casting and metal inlay of the Hutsuls of the Carpathians: cast crosses, jewellery, pipes and toporets axes, and brass-inlaid wood (UNESCO-listed in the Ukrainian National Inventory). [aka metalworking traditions of the Hutsul region]
G1-42 Drotárstvo: Slovak wire craft developed by itinerant tinkers who mended pottery with wire and later made wire objects and sculpture (UNESCO 2023). [aka wire craft and art;tinkering]
G1-43 Mosan Art: Romanesque goldsmiths' work and enamel of the Meuse valley (11th–13th century): Nicholas of Verdun's Klosterneuburg Altar, the Stavelot Triptych.
### G2 Arms & Armour — Axis: tradition.
G2-1 Islamic Arms and Armour: the arms and armour of the Islamic courts: Mamluk and Ottoman helmets and mail, Persian shamshirs and watered-steel (wootz) blades, Ottoman yatağans and Mughal talwars, katars and jade-hilted daggers. [aka shamshir; yatağan; talwar; wootz steel; Damascus steel]
G2-2 Kris: The asymmetrical pattern-welded (pamor) dagger of Java and the Malay world (UNESCO 2005), with carved hilts and sheaths, in regional forms from Sumatra to Mindanao: Javanese keris with naga blade, Balinese figural hilts, Bugis sheaths, Moro kalis. [aka keris;kalis]
G2-3 Japanese Swords: Blades forged and polished by Japanese swordsmiths in named traditions and schools: tachi, katana, tantō of the five traditions (Yamashiro, Yamato, Bizen, Sōshū, Mino). [aka nihontō]
G2-4 Japanese Sword Mountings: Metal sword fittings (tosogu) made by schools of metalworkers from the Muromachi to the Meiji: tsuba guards, menuki, kozuka, fuchi-kashira, in shakudō, shibuichi and mokume-gane. [aka tosogu;sword furniture]
G2-5 Milanese Armour: Plate armour of Milan, the leading European centre from c. 1400 to the 16th century (the Missaglia and Negroli families), from smooth white harness to embossed parade armour all'antica: Avant armour, Negroli burgonets. [aka Milanese armor]
G2-6 Puukko: The traditional Finnish belt knife with a wooden or birch-bark handle and leather sheath, made by named smiths (Kauhava, Fiskars). [aka Finnish knife]
### G3 Jewellery — Axis: tradition.
G3-1 Fatimid Jewellery: gold jewellery of Fatimid Egypt and Syria (10th–12th century) in filigree and granulation, often enamelled. [aka Fatimid gold filigree]
G3-2 Mughal Jewellery: jewellery and jewelled objects of the Mughal court: kundan setting, enamelled (minakari) backs, carved emeralds and jade inlaid with gems. [aka kundan; minakari; meenakari]
G3-3 Bedouin Jewellery: the silver jewellery of the Bedouin and settled peoples of Arabia, Yemen and Oman, worked in filigree, granulation and with coral and carnelian. [aka Yemeni silver; Omani silver]
G3-4 Berber Jewellery: the silver jewellery of the Amazigh peoples of Morocco, Algeria and Tunisia: fibulae, headdresses and the cloisonné enamel of Kabylia. [aka Amazigh jewellery; Kabyle jewellery]
G3-5 Javanese Gold Jewellery: Gold ornaments of Hindu-Buddhist Java (8th–15th century), cast, repoussé and set with stones: sacred-thread clasps, ear ornaments, rings with Sri inscriptions, Wonoboyo hoard. [aka Old Javanese gold;Indonesian gold]
G3-6 Miao Silver Ornaments: Silver headdresses, neck-rings and breastplates hammered and filigreed by Miao smiths of Guizhou for festival dress. [aka Miao silverwork]
G3-7 Jangdo: Korean ornamental knives in silver, horn and wood worn on the hanbok as accessory and symbol of fidelity; makers designated jangdojang. [aka jangdojang]
G3-8 Tairona Goldwork: Cast gold and tumbaga ornaments of the Tairona of the Sierra Nevada de Santa Marta (c. 900–1600 CE): bat-man and eagle pendants, nose ornaments, labrets.
G3-9 Quimbaya Goldwork: The gold of the Quimbaya of the Middle Cauca valley, with its ceramics: Quimbaya Treasure poporos, seated figures, helmets. [aka Quimbaya artifacts]
G3-10 Calima Goldwork: Hammered and cast gold of the Calima region of south-western Colombia in its Ilama, Yotoco and Malagana phases: diadems, nose rings, pectorals with faces.
G3-11 Tolima Goldwork: Flat cast gold pendants of the Tolima region of the Magdalena valley, with anchor-shaped human-bat figures.
G3-12 Isthmian Goldwork: Cast and hammered gold and tumbaga ornaments of the chiefdoms of Panama and Costa Rica (c. 200–1500 CE) in the Coclé, Veraguas, Chiriquí and Diquís styles: Sitio Conte plaques, eagle pendants, crocodile deity figures. [aka Gold of ancient Panama;Coclé gold]
G3-13 Mixtec Goldwork: Lost-wax cast gold ornaments of the Postclassic Mixtec of Oaxaca, with filigree-effect wax threads: Monte Albán Tomb 7 pectorals, labrets, bell pendants.
G3-14 Mapuche Silverwork: The silver jewellery of Mapuche women in Chile and Argentina, flourishing from the 18th century: trapelacucha pectorals, tupu pins, trarilonco headbands. [aka rütran]
G3-15 Navajo Silverwork: Silver jewellery and horse gear of Diné smiths since the 1850s, hammered, cast in tufa and stamped, set with turquoise from the 1880s: concho belts, squash blossom necklaces, ketohs, cuff bracelets. [aka Navajo jewelry;Navajo silversmithing]
G3-16 Zuni Jewelry: Lapidary jewellery of Zuni Pueblo, silver set with small cut stones since the 1920s: needlepoint and petit point, channel inlay, mosaic inlay. [aka Zuni silverwork;Zuni inlay]
G3-17 Heishe: Fine disc beads ground from shell, turquoise and stone and strung in necklaces, above all at Kewa (Santo Domingo) Pueblo. [aka heishi]
G3-18 Trade Silver: Silver and later German-silver ornaments of the fur trade and of Native smiths of the Northeast, Great Lakes and Prairies (18th–19th century): ring and luckenbooth brooches, gorgets, armbands and hair plates. [aka German silver;nickel silver ornaments]
G3-19 Riji: Pearl-shell pendants and pubic coverings of the Kimberley, engraved with interlocking meanders and filled with ochre, worn in ceremony and traded far inland: riji, jakoli, longkalongka. [aka jakoli;jakuli;longkalongka;Kimberley pearl shell]
G3-20 Fabergé: The jewellery and objets de luxe of the House of Fabergé in St Petersburg and Moscow, 1842–1918, in guilloché enamel, gold and hardstone: Imperial Easter eggs, cigarette cases, hardstone animals. [aka House of Fabergé]
G3-21 Hairwork: Jewellery and pictures worked from human hair, a 19th-century fashion carried on as a village craft in Vamhus, Dalarna. [aka hair jewellery;hårarbete]

## H Textiles, Carpets & Dress — test: made of fibre: carpets and rugs, woven, embroidered, dyed and felted textiles, and dress.
  key: 1. Knotted pile? → H1. 2. A flatweave (tapestry-woven kilim, soumak, warp-faced weaving)? → H2.
  key: 3. A figured or patterned woven textile (silk, brocade, lampas, velvet, tapestry, shawl)? → H3.
  key: 4. Decorated with the needle (embroidery, appliqué, needle lace)? → H4. 5. Patterned by resist-dyeing or printing (ikat, block print)? → H5. 6. Felt? → H6.
  key: 7. A dress tradition curated as such (a named costume ensemble)? → H7. A garment type alone is not an art tradition (P10).
  key: 8. Basketry, matting or barkcloth? → H8. Beadwork, porcupine quillwork or featherwork as the work? → H9.
### H1 Pile Carpets & Rugs — Axis: weaving tradition; centres, tribes and design types are its kinds.
H1-1 Persian Carpet: knotted-pile carpets of Iran, from the Safavid court carpets (the Ardabil carpet, hunting and vase carpets, Polonaise carpets) to the city, village and tribal rugs named by their centres and peoples (Tabriz, Kashan, Isfahan, Kerman, Heriz, Qashqai, Bakhtiari). [aka Persian rug; Iranian carpet; farsh]
H1-2 Anatolian Rug: knotted-pile carpets of Anatolia from the Seljuq Konya carpets onward: animal carpets, the 'Holbein' and 'Lotto' carpets known from European paintings, Ushak star and medallion carpets, and the village rugs of Bergama, Konya and Ghiordes. [aka Turkish carpet; Ushak carpet; Holbein carpet; Lotto carpet]
H1-3 Mamluk Carpet: carpets woven in Cairo under the Mamluks (late 15th–early 16th century) in red, green and blue with kaleidoscopic geometric fields. [aka Cairene carpet]
H1-4 Caucasian Carpet: knotted-pile rugs of the Caucasus — Kazak, Karabagh, Shirvan, Kuba and the dragon carpets — and of the Azerbaijani schools. [aka Caucasian rug; Azerbaijani carpet]
H1-5 Turkmen Carpet: knotted-pile carpets and tent furnishings of the Turkmen tribes (Tekke, Yomut, Salor, Saryk, Ersari), with their tribal güls. [aka Turkoman carpet; Bukhara rug]
H1-6 Baluch Rug: dark-toned knotted rugs of the Baluch peoples of eastern Iran, Afghanistan and Pakistan. [aka Baluchi rug; Belouch]
H1-7 Afghan Rug: knotted rugs of Afghanistan, including the war rugs woven since 1979 with weapons and maps. [aka war rugs; chobi rugs]
H1-8 Mughal Carpet: knotted carpets of the Mughal workshops of Lahore, Agra and Kashmir (late 16th–17th century): pictorial and flowering-plant carpets of very fine weave. [aka Indo-Persian carpet; Indo-Isfahan carpet]
H1-9 Moroccan Rug: the pile and flatwoven rugs of the Amazigh tribes of Morocco (Beni Ouarain, Azilal, Boucherouite) and of the urban workshops of Rabat. [aka Berber carpet; Amazigh rug]
H1-10 Freest Fisherman's Carpets: Knotted carpets designed and made since the 1920s on the coast of the Bay of Greifswald (Freest, Western Pomerania), with motifs of fish, birds and sea. [aka Freester Fischerteppiche;Pomeranian fisherman's carpets]
H1-11 Ryijy: Finnish knotted-pile wall hangings and bed covers with a long shaggy pile, from 18th-century folk ryijys to modern artist-designed rugs. [aka rya;ryijy rug]
### H2 Flatweaves — Axis: weaving technique and tradition.
H2-1 Kilim: tapestry-woven flatweaves of Anatolia, Iran, the Caucasus, Central Asia and North Africa, from slit-tapestry Anatolian kilims to Persian gelims, gabbeh-like tribal weaves and Tunisian mergoum. [aka kelim; gelim; mergoum]
H2-2 Soumak: weft-wrapped flatweaves of the Caucasus and north-west Iran, with a herringbone surface. [aka sumak]
H2-3 Al Sadu: the warp-faced weaving of the Bedouin of Kuwait, Saudi Arabia, the UAE and Qatar, for tents and camel trappings (UNESCO 2011). [aka Sadu weaving; Bedouin weaving]
H2-4 Inca Textiles: The tapestry-woven cumbi cloth of the Inca state, woven by aqllakuna and cumbicamayoc, with tocapu tunics as the highest rank: Dumbarton Oaks tocapu tunic, checkerboard military tunics. [aka cumbi;tocapu]
H2-5 Wari Textiles: The interlocking tapestry tunics of the Wari state (c. 600–1000 CE) with abstracted staff-god profiles expanded and compressed across the field. [aka Huari tapestry]
H2-6 Colonial Andean Tapestry: Tapestry-woven textiles of the Viceroyalty of Peru (16th–19th century) in which cumbi weavers combined Inca, Spanish and Asian designs: colonial uncus with tocapu, tapices with coats of arms. [aka tapices;colonial uncu]
H2-7 Andean Textiles: The living warp-faced and complementary-warp weaving of Quechua and Aymara communities of Peru, Bolivia and Ecuador, with named regional styles: Jalq'a and Tarabuco axsus, Chinchero lliclla, Otavalo weaving. [aka Andean weaving]
H2-8 Taquile Textile Art: The weaving and knitting of Taquile Island in Lake Titicaca, where men knit chullos and women weave belts (chumpi) recording the agricultural calendar, inscribed by UNESCO in 2008. [aka Taquile textiles]
H2-9 Saltillo Serape: Tapestry-woven wool blankets of Saltillo and northern Mexico (18th–19th century) with a central diamond medallion and serrated fields. [aka sarape;Saltillo sarape]
H2-10 Navajo Weaving: Tapestry-woven blankets and rugs of Diné women on the upright loom: chief's blankets (first to third phase), eye-dazzlers, Two Grey Hills and Ganado rugs, Yeibichai rugs. [aka Navajo rugs;Navajo blankets;diyogí]
H2-11 Chilkat Weaving: Twined mountain-goat-wool and cedar-bark robes, tunics and aprons of the Tlingit and Haida, woven from painted pattern boards in formline designs. [aka Chilkat blanket;naaxein]
H2-12 Coast Salish Weaving: Twill and twined blankets of mountain-goat and dog wool woven by Coast Salish women on two-bar looms, revived since the 1960s. [aka Salish blankets]
H2-13 Chimayó Weaving: Hispano wool weaving of the upper Rio Grande valley, New Mexico, from colonial Rio Grande blankets to the Chimayó blankets and vests woven by families such as the Ortegas and Trujillos. [aka Rio Grande weaving;Chimayo blankets]
H2-14 Māori Weaving: The fibre arts of the Māori in harakeke (New Zealand flax) and muka (its fibre): finger-twined (whatu) cloaks with tāniko borders and plaited (raranga) baskets and mats: kaitaka and korowai cloaks, piupiu, kete, whāriki. [aka raranga;whatu;Māori traditional textiles;kākahu]
H2-15 Ceinture Fléchée: The finger-woven wool sash of French Canada and the Métis, with its arrow (flèche) and lightning patterns, an element of Quebec's intangible heritage: Assomption sashes. [aka fléché;arrow sash]
H2-16 Raanu: Finnish weft-faced flatwoven wool coverlet and wall hanging in stripes and geometric patterns. [aka Finnish flatweave]
H2-17 Chiprovtsi Kilim: Tapestry-woven kilims of Chiprovtsi in north-western Bulgaria since the 17th century, with geometric and later floral designs (UNESCO 2014). [aka Chiprovtsi carpet;Chiprovtsi rugs]
H2-18 Pirot Kilim: Double-sided tapestry-woven kilims of Pirot in south-eastern Serbia, with geometric motifs such as the turtle and the vine. [aka Pirot carpet;pirotski ćilim]
H2-19 Sjenica-Pešter Kilim: Kilims woven on the Pešter plateau around Sjenica in south-western Serbia. [aka Sjenica kilim]
H2-20 Stapar Rugs: Rug-making in the village of Stapar in Vojvodina (Serbia), with floral rugs on dark grounds. [aka rug-making in Stapar]
H2-21 Kotsy: Ukrainian woollen flatwoven carpets (kots, kylym) of the Left-Bank and Polissia regions. [aka kots;kotsy;Ukrainian kilim]
H2-22 Reshetylivka Carpets: Handwoven floral carpets of Reshetylivka in Poltava Oblast (Ukraine). [aka Reshetylivka carpet weaving]
H2-23 Romanian and Moldovan Wall Carpets: Woollen wall carpets (scoarțe, covoare) woven in Romania and the Republic of Moldova and hung in houses as dowry and decoration (UNESCO 2016). [aka scoarță;scoarțe;traditional wall-carpet craftsmanship]
### H3 Woven Silks & Figured Textiles — Axis: workshop tradition.
H3-1 Tiraz: inscribed textiles from the caliphal workshops (tiraz) of the Umayyads, Abbasids and Fatimids, with bands of embroidered or tapestry-woven Arabic inscriptions. [aka tiraz textiles; tiraz bands]
H3-2 Safavid Silk Textiles: the figured silks, lampas, brocades and velvets of Safavid Iran (Ghiyath al-Din 'Ali-yi Naqshband), with figural scenes and floral fields. [aka Persian velvet; Safavid brocade]
H3-3 Ottoman Silk Textiles: the silk lampas (kemha) and velvets (çatma) of Bursa and Istanbul (15th–17th century), with ogival lattices, tulips and çintamani. [aka kemha; çatma; Bursa silk]
H3-4 Nasrid Silk: silks woven in Nasrid Granada and Almería (13th–15th century), with interlace, inscriptions and the 'Alhambra silks'. [aka Alhambra silks; Hispano-Islamic silk]
H3-5 Termeh: Persian tapestry-twill woven with the boteh, made in Yazd and Kerman. [aka Persian termeh; Yazd brocade]
H3-6 Kashmir Shawl: twill-tapestry shawls woven from pashmina in Kashmir (17th–19th century), whose boteh became the paisley of European imitations. [aka Kani shawl; pashmina shawl]
H3-7 Banarasi Brocade: Silk brocades woven with gold and silver zari in Varanasi, from Mughal kinkhab to the Banarasi sari: kinkhab, jangla and butidar saris. [aka kinkhab;kincob;Benares brocade]
H3-8 Jamdani: Supplementary-weft figured muslin of Dhaka and Bengal (UNESCO 2013), with motifs inlaid by hand on the loom: jamdani saris, Dhaka muslin. [aka Dhakai jamdani]
H3-9 Kanchipuram Silk: Heavy mulberry-silk saris of Kanchipuram with contrast korvai borders and temple motifs. [aka Kanjeevaram]
H3-10 Paithani: Silk and zari saris of Paithan and Yeola in Maharashtra with tapestry-woven peacock and lotus pallus.
H3-11 Songket: Silk or cotton brocade with supplementary weft of gold and silver thread, woven in the Malay courts of Sumatra, the Peninsula and Brunei: Palembang songket, Minangkabau songket. [aka kain songket]
H3-12 Lampung Ship Cloths: Supplementary-weft ceremonial cloths of the Lampung of south Sumatra depicting ships of the soul: the large aristocratic palepai banners and the ubiquitous tampan. [aka ship cloth;palepai;tampan]
H3-13 Piña: Sheer cloth woven from pineapple-leaf fibre in Aklan and Manila, often embroidered (calado), made into barong and pañuelo. [aka piña cloth]
H3-14 Inabel: The cotton handloom weaving of the Ilocos region of Luzon, in binakol whirlpool and other patterns. [aka abel Iloko]
H3-15 Naga Motif Weaving in Lao Communities: Handwoven Lao silks with naga serpent motifs in supplementary weft and ikat (UNESCO 2023): sinh skirts, pha biang shoulder cloths. [aka Lao naga textiles]
H3-16 Kesi: Silk tapestry woven with discontinuous wefts in China from the Tang, reproducing paintings and calligraphy in the Song and dressing the Qing court: Shen Zifan's kesi landscapes, dragon robes. [aka k'o-ssu;silk tapestry]
H3-17 Zhuang Brocade: Weft-patterned cotton-and-silk brocade of the Zhuang of Guangxi, one of China's four famous brocades. [aka zhuangjin]
H3-18 Li Textiles: The spinning, ikat-dyeing, weaving and embroidery of the Li (Hlai) women of Hainan (UNESCO urgent safeguarding 2009): tube skirts, dragon quilts. [aka traditional Li textile techniques;Li brocade]
H3-19 Shuri Weaving: The court textiles of Shuri, the Ryukyu capital: hanaori and rōton-ori figured weaves and kasuri silks. [aka Shuri-ori;traditional Shuri weaving]
H3-20 Bashōfu: Cloth woven from Japanese banana fibre in Okinawa and Amami, plain or kasuri-patterned, centred on Kijoka. [aka basho-fu;banana-fibre cloth]
H3-21 Echigo-jōfu and Ojiya-chijimi: Fine ramie cloth of the Uonuma region of Niigata, snow-bleached and often kasuri-patterned (UNESCO 2009). [aka Echigo-jofu;Ojiya-chijimi]
H3-22 Hansan Mosi: Fine ramie cloth woven by women of Hansan in South Chungcheong (UNESCO 2011), for summer hanbok. [aka Hansan mosi jjagi;mosi]
H3-23 Kente Cloth: Strip-woven silk and cotton cloth of the Asante and Ewe of Ghana and Togo, narrow strips with named warp and weft-float patterns sewn edge to edge: adwini asa, oyokoman, Ewe kente. [aka nwentoma;Ewe kente]
H3-24 Aso Oke: Prestige strip-woven cloth of the Yoruba of Nigeria: sanyan, alaari, etu. [aka aso-oke]
H3-25 Akwete Cloth: Cloth woven by Ndoki Igbo women of Akwete on the vertical loom, with the ikaki (tortoise) motif. [aka Akwete]
H3-26 Ivorian Loincloth Weaving: The traditional loincloth (pagne) weaving of Côte d'Ivoire, strip-woven on narrow looms (Baule, Senufo, Dioula, Dan), inscribed by UNESCO in 2023. [aka pagne weaving]
H3-27 Coptic Textiles: Tapestry-woven linen and wool textiles of late antique and early Islamic Egypt, 3rd–8th century, from burial grounds: tunic clavi and roundels, hangings with Dionysiac and Nilotic scenes, weft-loop wall hangings. [aka late antique Egyptian textiles]
H3-28 Maya Textiles: The backstrap-woven and brocaded cotton cloth of the Maya of Guatemala and Chiapas, whose huipiles, sashes and headcloths are distinct by town and community: Chichicastenango huipiles, Santiago Atitlán tzutes, Chamula wool. [aka huipil weaving;traje]
H3-29 Chancay Textiles: Cotton and camelid cloth of the Chancay culture of Peru's central coast (c. 1000–1470 CE): gauze and openwork, painted cloth and woven dolls: gauze panels, painted cloths.
H3-30 Flemish Tapestry: Tapestry woven in the Southern Netherlands, 14th–17th century, at Arras, Tournai, Brussels, Oudenaarde and Antwerp, after cartoons by leading painters: Apocalypse of Angers, Unicorn tapestries, Acts of the Apostles after Raphael. [aka Netherlandish tapestry;Brussels tapestry]
H3-31 Aubusson Tapestry: Tapestry and carpet weaving of Aubusson and Felletin in the Creuse, from the 16th century to its 20th-century revival (Lurçat), inscribed by UNESCO in 2009.
H3-32 Täkänä: Finnish double-weave (Finn-pick) technique giving reversible two-colour cloth, used for coverlets and hangings. [aka Finn-pick double weave]
H3-33 Northern Vidzeme Double-Weave Blankets: Double-weave colour-gradient blanket weaving of Northern Vidzeme in Latvia. [aka doubleweave colour gradient blanket weaving]
H3-34 Sigulda Belts: Patterned belts woven in Sigulda municipality (Latvia).
H3-35 Bezdan Damask: Damask linen woven in Bezdan (Vojvodina, Serbia) since the 1880s, with pictorial and floral designs. [aka Bezdan damask weaving]
H3-36 Serpanok Weaving: Weaving of serpanok, a fine gauze-like cloth, in Dubrovytsia hromada (Rivne Oblast, Ukraine). [aka serpanok]
H3-37 Hlyniany Textile: Patterned woven textiles of Hlyniany (Lviv Oblast, Ukraine), long known for its kilims. [aka Hlyniany kilims]
H3-38 Obukhovychi Weaving: Traditional patterned weaving of the village of Obukhovychi (Kyiv Oblast, Ukraine).
### H4 Embroidery & Needlework — Axis: tradition.
H4-1 Suzani: large embroidered hangings and covers of Uzbekistan and Tajikistan (Bukhara, Nurata, Samarkand, Shakhrisabz), worked in silk chain and couching stitch. [aka suzani embroidery]
H4-2 Ottoman Embroidery: the silk and metal-thread embroidery of Ottoman households and court: towels, napkins, covers and garments, worked in double running and satin stitch. [aka Turkish embroidery]
H4-3 Palestinian Embroidery: tatreez: the cross-stitch embroidery of Palestinian women's dress by village and region (UNESCO 2021). [aka tatreez]
H4-4 Pateh: the wool embroidery of Kerman in Iran, densely covering woollen cloth with boteh and floral designs. [aka Kerman embroidery]
H4-5 Rasht Embroidery: felt appliqué and chain-stitch embroidery of Rasht in Gilan (Iran). [aka Rashti-duzi]
H4-6 Oya: Turkish needle lace: knotted borders of flowers for headscarves. [aka oya lace; Turkish needle lace]
H4-7 Khayamiya: the appliqué tent panels of the Cairo tentmakers' street, Sharia al-Khayamiya. [aka Egyptian appliqué]
H4-8 Balochi Needlework: the dense geometric embroidery of Baloch women's dress (Iran, Pakistan, Afghanistan). [aka Baloch embroidery]
H4-9 Phulkari: Darn-stitch silk embroidery on khaddar of the women of Punjab, in sparse phulkari and fully covered bagh: bagh, chope. [aka bagh]
H4-10 Chikankari: White-on-white shadow and knot-stitch embroidery of Lucknow on fine cotton. [aka chikan work]
H4-11 Kantha: Running-stitch embroidery on layered old cloth by the women of Bengal and Bangladesh: nakshi kantha, lep kantha. [aka nakshi kantha]
H4-12 Sujani: Running-stitch embroidered quilts of Bhusura in Muzaffarpur, Bihar, with narrative figures. [aka sujni;Sujini]
H4-13 Ralli Quilt: Patchwork, applique and embroidered quilts of Sindh, Cholistan, Kutch and Rajasthan. [aka rilli;rilly]
H4-14 Chakan: The silk-on-cotton floral embroidery of Tajikistan for garments, coverlets and wall hangings (UNESCO 2018): chakan dresses, wall hangings. [aka Chakan embroidery]
H4-15 Khamak: The fine geometric satin-stitch embroidery of Kandahar and of Hazara women in Afghanistan, worked from the back on fine cotton: shirt panels, pillow covers, shawls. [aka Kandahari embroidery;khamak embroidery]
H4-16 Turkmen Needlework: The embroidery applied to Turkmen dress of all ages and sexes in Turkmenistan and Iran, in chain, satin and stitch-band techniques (UNESCO 2022): chyrpy mantles, tahya caps. [aka Turkmen-style needlework art;Turkmen embroidery]
H4-17 Tapis: The ceremonial tube skirts of Lampung women, warp-ikat and woven grounds couched with gold thread, mica and embroidery. [aka kain tapis]
H4-18 Kalaga: Burmese padded appliqué hangings embroidered with sequins and metal thread in scenes from the Jatakas and court life. [aka shwe chi doe]
H4-19 Tekat: Malay raised gold-thread embroidery couched over card or padding, for cushions, betel covers and wedding furnishings. [aka tekat timbul]
H4-20 Suzhou Embroidery: Silk embroidery of Suzhou in Jiangsu, one of China's four famous embroideries, fine-split thread and double-sided pieces: cats, goldfish, painting copies. [aka Su xiu;Su embroidery]
H4-21 Xiang Embroidery: Silk embroidery of Changsha in Hunan, one of the four famous embroideries, famed for tigers and lions in shaded stitches. [aka Xiang xiu;Hunan embroidery]
H4-22 Shu Embroidery: Silk embroidery of Chengdu in Sichuan, one of the four famous embroideries: pandas, carp and hibiscus on satin. [aka Shu xiu;Sichuan embroidery]
H4-23 Cantonese Embroidery: Embroidery of the Pearl River Delta (Guangzhou and Chaozhou), dense and brilliant, with raised gold-thread work; one of the four famous embroideries. [aka Yue xiu;Guangxiu]
H4-24 Gu Embroidery: Painting-embroidery of the Gu family of Shanghai (late Ming), copying Song and Yuan paintings in silk thread: Han Ximeng's albums. [aka Gu xiu;Shanghai embroidery]
H4-25 Korean Embroidery: Silk embroidery of Joseon court and household (jasu): embroidered screens, rank badges (hyungbae), pillow ends, wedding robes (hwarot); masters designated jasujang. [aka jasu;jasujang]
H4-26 Bojagi: Korean wrapping cloths, above all jogakbo patchwork of leftover ramie and silk, and embroidered subo. [aka pojagi;jogakbo]
H4-27 Sashiko: Japanese running-stitch quilting and darning of indigo cloth, in regional forms: kogin of Tsugaru, hishizashi, mended boro. [aka sashiko stitching;kogin;boro]
H4-28 Hmong Textile Art: Embroidery, batik, appliqué and pleating of the Miao (Hmong) of Guizhou and the Hmong diaspora: pleated skirts, baby carriers, story cloths (paj ntaub). [aka Miao embroidery;paj ntaub]
H4-29 Ainu Textiles: Ainu robes of elm-bark (attus) and nettle cloth and of imported cotton, with appliqué and chain-stitch embroidery of curving designs: ruunpe, chikarkarpe, kaparamip. [aka attus;attush;ruunpe]
H4-30 Kuba Raffia Textiles: Raffia cloth of the Kuba peoples of the DR Congo, woven by men and embroidered by women: Shoowa cut-pile ('Kasai velvet') panels, appliqué overskirts, Bushoong ceremonial skirts. [aka Kasai velvet;Shoowa;ncak;mapel]
H4-31 Asafo Flags: Appliqué regimental flags (frankaa) of the Fante Asafo companies of coastal Ghana, with proverb designs and a corner Union Jack or Ghanaian flag. [aka frankaa;Fante flags]
H4-32 Paracas Textiles: Camelid-wool embroidered mantles from the Paracas Necropolis on Peru's south coast (c. 300 BCE–200 CE), stem-stitched with repeating flying figures: Paracas Necropolis mantles. [aka Paracas Necropolis textiles]
H4-33 Mola: Reverse-appliqué cotton panels made by Guna women of Panama and Colombia for the front and back of their blouses. [aka mola panels]
H4-34 Arpillera: Appliqué and embroidered burlap-backed pictures made by women's workshops in Chile under the Pinochet dictatorship and later in Peru. [aka arpilleras]
H4-35 Drapo Vodou: Sequinned and beaded ritual flags of Haitian Vodou, carried to salute the lwa: vèvè flags, Myrlande Constant tapestries. [aka Vodou flag;sequin flags]
H4-36 Ñandutí: Paraguayan needle lace of radiating spider-web suns, made in Itauguá. [aka ñandutí lace;ao po'i]
H4-37 Divina Pastora Irish Lace: The needle lace (renda irlandesa) made with cord and buttonhole stitches in Divina Pastora, Sergipe, registered by IPHAN. [aka renda irlandesa]
H4-38 Seminole Patchwork: Machine-pieced bands of cotton strips sewn into skirts, jackets and shirts by Seminole and Miccosukee women of Florida since the early 20th century. [aka Seminole piecework]
H4-39 Ribbonwork: Silk-ribbon appliqué cut and layered in negative and positive designs on skirts, leggings and blankets by Great Lakes and Prairie peoples (Menominee, Ho-Chunk, Meskwaki, Potawatomi, Osage) since the late 18th century. [aka ribbon work;ribbon appliqué]
H4-40 Moosehair Embroidery: Embroidery in dyed moosehair on birchbark and black cloth by Wendat (Huron) women of Wendake (Lorette) and other Northeastern peoples, learned from Ursuline nuns: floral moccasins, calling-card boxes, cigar cases. [aka moose-hair embroidery;Huron embroidery]
H4-41 Star Quilts: Pieced quilts centred on a single eight-pointed star of diamond patches, made by Lakota, Dakota and other Plains women since the reservation era and given at honouring and memorial giveaways. [aka Lakota star quilt;wičháȟpi owíŋža]
H4-42 Tivaevae: The appliqué and piecework quilts of the Cook Islands (and tīfaifai of Tahiti), sewn communally by women's groups (vainetini) and given at weddings, birthdays and funerals: tivaevae manu, tivaevae taorei, tivaevae tataura. [aka tivaivai;tīfaifai]
H4-43 Opus Anglicanum: English medieval embroidery in silk and gold thread, c. 1200–1400, prized across Europe for vestments: Syon Cope, Butler-Bowdon Cope, Steeple Aston Cope. [aka English work]
H4-44 Bucks Point Lace: English bobbin lace of Buckinghamshire and the East Midlands, 18th–19th century, a point-ground lace in the manner of Lille and Mechlin. [aka Buckinghamshire lace;English Lille]
H4-45 Bedfordshire Lace: English bobbin lace of the Midlands from the mid-19th century, a guipure lace of plaits and leaves influenced by Maltese lace. [aka Bedfordshire Maltese]
H4-46 Amish Quilts: Pieced quilts of the Amish of Pennsylvania and the Midwest, c. 1870–1950, in solid dark colours and bold geometric fields with fine quilting: Center Diamond, Bars, Sunshine and Shadow. [aka Mennonite quilts]
H4-47 Hooked Rugs: Rugs and mats made by hooking strips of cloth or yarn through a backing, in New England and the Canadian Maritimes from the 19th century, including the Grenfell mats of Labrador and Newfoundland. [aka rug hooking;Grenfell mats]
H4-48 Greek Island Embroidery: The silk-on-linen embroidery of the Aegean and Ionian islands and Epirus (17th–19th century), each island with its own stitches and designs, used for bed curtains, cushions and dress: Cretan skirt borders, Rhodian bed tents, Naxos and Dodecanese hangings. [aka Greek embroidery;Aegean embroidery]
H4-49 Hardanger Embroidery: Norwegian whitework of counted satin-stitch blocks (kloster) with cut and drawn threads, from the Hardanger district, used on bunad aprons and linens. [aka hardangersøm]
H4-50 Mountmellick Embroidery: Irish white embroidery of raised floral designs in thick cotton on white cotton satin, begun in Mountmellick, County Laois, about 1825 by Johanna Carter. [aka Mountmellick work]
H4-51 Arraiolos Rug: Portuguese wool rugs embroidered in Slav (long-armed cross) stitch on linen or jute canvas, made in Arraiolos in the Alentejo since the 17th century. [aka tapetes de Arraiolos]
H4-52 Bordados da Glória do Ribatejo: The embroidery of Glória do Ribatejo (Salvaterra de Magos), worked mainly in cross stitch on household linens and local dress. [aka Glória do Ribatejo embroidery]
H4-53 Alençon Lace: French needle lace of Alençon in Normandy, developed in the 1660s under royal patronage and made into the 19th century, with fine réseau ground and corded outlines: flounces, lappets. [aka point d'Alençon]
H4-54 Burano Lace: Venetian needle lace of the island of Burano, revived in 1872 by the Burano lace school on the model of the old Venetian points. [aka merletto di Burano;punto Burano]
H4-55 Lefkara Lace: The drawn-thread and needle-lace embroidery of Pano Lefkara in Cyprus, worked on linen in geometric designs (UNESCO 2009). [aka lefkaritika]
H4-56 Brussels Lace: Pillow lace of Brussels and Brabant, its motifs made separately in bobbin or needle lace and joined to a droschel or bride ground (17th–19th century), including point de gaze and Brussels appliqué. [aka point d'Angleterre;Brussels point]
H4-57 Mechlin Lace: Flemish bobbin lace of Mechelen with a hexagonal ground and a flat thread outlining the motifs, the 18th century's favourite lace for ruffles. [aka Malines lace;Mechelen lace]
H4-58 Quill Embroidery: Embroidery of leather with split quills of peacock tail feathers on belts, braces and shoes of Alpine dress, practised in Tyrol (Sarntal) and Austria. [aka Federkielstickerei]
H4-59 Honiton Lace: English bobbin lace of east Devon whose motifs are made separately and joined, famed for Queen Victoria's wedding lace (1840).
H4-60 Le Puy Lace: Bobbin lace of Le Puy-en-Velay in the Auvergne, in linen, silk and wool guipures and Cluny-style laces made by cottage lacemakers. [aka dentelle du Puy]
H4-61 Maltese Lace: Bobbin lace of Malta and Gozo, above all the 19th-century silk guipure with the Maltese cross and wheatears. [aka bizzilla]
H4-62 Headford Lace: The bobbin lace of Headford, County Galway, a cottage industry of the 18th–19th centuries revived today. [aka Headford lacemaking]
H4-63 Carrickmacross Lace: Irish appliqué lace of County Monaghan, cambric shapes cut and sewn on machine net, introduced about 1820 by Mrs Grey Porter.
H4-64 Limerick Lace: Irish embroidered net lace of Limerick (from 1829), worked in tambour (chain stitch) or run (darning) stitch on machine net.
H4-65 Irish Crochet Lace: Crochet made in imitation of Venetian needle lace, spread as famine relief from the 1840s; motifs are worked separately and joined with a crocheted mesh: Clones lace, Youghal-area crochet. [aka Irish lace crochet]
H4-66 Ukrainian Embroidery: The regional embroidery of Ukraine on shirts (vyshyvanka), rushnyky and household linen in cross, satin and counted stitches, distinct by region and village: Reshetylivka white-on-white, Borshchiv black shirts, Podillia nyz. [aka vyshyvka;vyshyvanka embroidery]
H4-67 Rushnyk: East Slavic ritual cloths of linen embroidered or woven with symbolic red and black ornament, used at weddings, funerals and to dress icons: Krolevets woven rushnyky. [aka rushnyky;ruchnik]
H4-68 Belarusian Embroidery: Embroidery of Belarus on ritual towels, shirts and aprons in counted red geometric ornament, including the Vetka negliubka tradition.
H4-69 Chuvash Embroidery: Counted-thread embroidery of the Chuvash of the Volga in red, black and green on white linen, for headdresses, shirts and ritual cloths.
H4-70 Mari Embroidery: Embroidery of the Mari of the Volga-Kama region, dense red-and-black geometric patterns on shirts and headdresses (shurka, shymaksh).
H4-71 Karelian Embroidery: Embroidery of the Karelians of Finland and Russia, in red counted and drawn work on towels and shirts. [aka Karjalainen kirjonta]
H4-72 Krestets Stitch: Drawn-thread whitework embroidery of Krestsy (Novgorod region) on linen, with geometric openwork grounds. [aka Krestsy stitching;Krestetskaya strochka]
H4-73 Mstyora Embroidery: White satin-stitch and openwork embroidery of Mstyora (Vladimir region) on batiste and linen. [aka Mstyora white satin stitch]
H4-74 Muhu Embroidery: Embroidery of the Estonian island of Muhu, with bold floral motifs and birds in bright pinks, greens and oranges on black wool. [aka Muhu tikand]
H4-75 Matyó Embroidery: The embroidery of the Matyó community of Mezőkövesd in Hungary, with dense roses and peonies in bright satin stitch (UNESCO 2012). [aka Folk art of the Matyó;Mezőkövesd embroidery]
H4-76 Zmijanje Embroidery: Dark-blue wool embroidery on women's dress and household linen of the Zmijanje villages near Banja Luka (UNESCO 2014).
H4-77 Macedonian Embroidery: Embroidery of North Macedonia on women's shirts and sleeves in dense red, black and gold geometric motifs.
H4-78 Kosovo-Style Embroidery: Serbian embroidery in the Kosovo style, inscribed in the Serbian ICH inventory.
H4-79 Altiță: Embroidery on the shoulder band (altiță) of the Romanian and Moldovan traditional blouse (ie), the defining ornament of the costume (UNESCO 2022). [aka ie;Romanian blouse;altita]
H4-80 Syrian-Armenian Embroidery: The embroidery culture of the Syrian-Armenian community of Armenia (Marash and Aintab traditions), inscribed in Armenia's ICH list. [aka Embroidery culture of the Syrian-Armenian community]
H4-81 Armenian Needlelace: Knotted needle lace of Armenia and the Armenian diaspora, made with a needle in rows of knots for collars and covers. [aka Armenian lace;Bebilla;Armenian lace making]
H4-82 Tatar Leather Mosaic: The Kazan Tatar technique (kayuly kün) of stitching coloured morocco pieces into mosaic patterns with gold thread, for boots (ichigi) and slippers. [aka kayuly kün;ichigi;chitek]
H4-83 Orenburg Shawl: Knitted lace shawls of goat down from the Orenburg region, including the fine gossamer 'web' shawl that passes through a ring. [aka Orenburg lace;pautinka]
H4-84 Vologda Lace: Bobbin tape lace of Vologda with continuous tape patterns on a net ground (from the 19th century).
H4-85 Yelets Lace: Fine bobbin tape lace of Yelets (Lipetsk region), with small motifs on delicate grounds.
H4-86 Rauma Lace: Bobbin lace of the town of Rauma in Finland, made since the 18th century with fine linen thread. [aka Rauma bobbin lace]
H4-87 Lace-Making in Heinämaa: The bobbin lace tradition of the village of Heinämaa in Finland.
H4-88 Knipelēšana: Latvian bobbin lace-making tradition. [aka Latvian bobbin lace]
H4-89 Idrija Lace: Bobbin tape lace of Idrija (Slovenia), made since the 17th century with a continuous tape (UNESCO 2018).
H4-90 Lacemaking in Croatia: The lace traditions of Croatia — needle lace of Pag, bobbin lace of Lepoglava and Sveta Marija, aloe-thread lace of Hvar (UNESCO 2009). [aka Croatian lace]
H4-91 Quilts of Gee's Bend: Quilts made by the women of Gee's Bend, Alabama, since the 19th century: Annie Mae Young's work-clothes quilts, Mary Lee Bendolph's quilts.
### H5 Dyed & Printed Textiles — Axis: technique and tradition.
H5-1 Central Asian Ikat: warp-ikat silks and silk-cotton (adras, atlas) of Bukhara, Samarkand and the Fergana Valley (abrband, 'cloud-binding'). [aka abrband; adras; atlas]
H5-2 Qalamkar: block-printed and painted cotton of Isfahan. [aka ghalamkar; Persian qalamkari]
H5-3 Indian Chintz: Mordant-painted and resist-dyed cottons made on the Coromandel Coast and in Gujarat for export to Europe and Asia, 17th–18th century: palampores, Golconda floorspreads, petticoat panels for the Dutch market. [aka chintz;painted and dyed cotton]
H5-4 Kalamkari: Pen-drawn and block-printed mordant-dyed cotton of Andhra Pradesh, in the Srikalahasti temple-cloth and Machilipatnam block-print styles: temple hangings, Persian-market prayer cloths. [aka qalamkari]
H5-5 Ajrakh: Indigo and madder resist block-printed cotton of Sindh and Kutch, printed on both faces. [aka ajrak]
H5-6 Bandhani: Tie-dyed textiles of Gujarat and Rajasthan made by binding thousands of tiny points: gharchola, odhni. [aka bandhej;bandhana]
H5-7 Leheriya: Diagonally tied and dyed wave-patterned turbans and saris of Rajasthan. [aka lahariya]
H5-8 Patola: Double-ikat silk saris of Patan in Gujarat, traded to Southeast Asia as heirloom cloths. [aka patolu;Patan patola]
H5-9 Rogan Art: Cloth painted with a thickened castor-oil paste drawn from a stylus, practised at Nirona in Kutch. [aka roghan]
H5-10 Indonesian Batik: Wax-resist dyed cloth of Java (UNESCO 2009), drawn by canting (tulis) or stamped (cap), in the court styles of Yogyakarta and Surakarta and the coastal pesisir styles of Pekalongan, Lasem and Cirebon: parang rusak kain, batik Belanda, Hokokai batik. [aka batik;Javanese batik;batik tulis;batik cap]
H5-11 Malaysian Batik: Block-printed and hand-drawn batik of the east-coast Malay states, Kelantan and Terengganu, with large floral designs. [aka batik Malaysia]
H5-12 Indonesian Ikat: Warp-ikat cotton cloths of the eastern islands and of Sumatra, Borneo and Sulawesi, tied and dyed in morinda and indigo: Sumba hinggi mantles, Toraja sacred cloths, Savu and Flores sarongs. [aka tenun ikat;kain kebat;hinggi]
H5-13 Geringsing: The double-ikat cotton cloths of Tenganan Pegeringsingan in Bali, worn for protection in rites. [aka gringsing]
H5-14 Tais: Handwoven textiles of Timor-Leste (UNESCO urgent safeguarding 2021), in ikat, supplementary weft and tapestry. [aka kain tais]
H5-15 Pua Kumbu: The ceremonial warp-ikat blankets of the Iban of Sarawak and Kalimantan, woven by women with spirit and ancestor figures. [aka pua]
H5-16 T'nalak: Abaca warp-ikat cloth of the T'boli of South Cotabato, its designs dreamed by the weaver. [aka tinalak]
H5-17 Telepuk: Malay cloth stamped with gold leaf from carved wooden blocks for court and wedding dress. [aka kain telepuk]
H5-18 Yūzen: Paste-resist dyeing with a fine rice-paste line and painted dyes, attributed to Miyazaki Yūzensai of Kyoto (late 17th century): Kyō-yūzen and Kaga-yūzen kimono. [aka yūzen-zome]
H5-19 Katazome: Japanese stencil dyeing with paste applied through cut paper stencils (katagami), and the stencils themselves as collected objects: Ise-katagami, komon small-pattern kimono. [aka katagami;Ise-katagami]
H5-20 Bingata: Okinawan stencil- and freehand paste-resist cloth dyed in bright pigments, made for the Ryukyu court and gentry. [aka Ryukyuan stencil dyeing]
H5-21 Tsutsugaki: Freehand paste-resist dyeing with a cone (tsutsu), mostly indigo, for festive covers, futon covers and banners (Edo to early 20th century).
H5-22 Shibori: Japanese shaped-resist dyeing by binding, stitching, folding and capping cloth: kanoko dots, arimatsu-narumi shibori, the Momoyama tsujigahana robes. [aka tsujigahana]
H5-23 Kasuri: Japanese ikat: cotton, ramie and silk cloth woven from resist-dyed threads, in regional centres: Kurume, Iyo, Bingo, Miyako-jōfu. [aka Japanese ikat;kasuri-ori]
H5-24 Bai Tie-Dye: Indigo tie-dyed cotton (zharan) of the Bai of Dali and Zhoucheng in Yunnan. [aka zharan;Dali tie-dye]
H5-25 Geumbak: Korean gold-leaf stamping of textiles with carved woodblocks, for royal and bridal dress; makers designated geumbakjang. [aka geumbakjang]
H5-26 Bògòlanfini: Mud-dyed cotton cloth of the Bamana of Mali, strip-woven by men and painted by women with fermented mud on a leaf-dye ground. [aka bogolan;bogolanfini;mud cloth]
H5-27 Adire: Indigo resist-dyed cloth of Yoruba women: adire eleko (starch resist), adire oniko (tied), adire alabere (stitched). [aka adire eleko;adire oniko]
H5-28 Adinkra Cloth: Asante funerary and prestige cloth stamped with calabash stamps of named adinkra symbols, centred on Ntonso. [aka adinkra]
H5-29 Ndop Cloth: Indigo stitch-resist royal display cloth of the Cameroon Grassfields (Bamileke, Bamum), strip-woven and patterned in white. [aka ndop]
H5-30 Kanga: Printed cotton cloths of the Swahili coast worn in pairs, with a border, central field and a printed Swahili proverb (jina): kanga, kitenge. [aka khanga;leso;kitenge]
H5-31 Rebozo: The Mexican woman's shawl, at its finest in warp-ikat (jaspe) cotton and silk with knotted fringe from Santa María del Río and Tenancingo. [aka rebozo de bolita]
H5-32 Blaudruck: Resist block printing and indigo dyeing of linen and cotton, carried on by family dye-works of Central Europe (UNESCO 2018, Austria, Czechia, Germany, Hungary, Slovakia): Burgenland blueprint, Mühlviertel indigo printing. [aka blueprint;resist block printing and indigo dyeing;Modrotisk;Kékfestés;Modrotlač;blue-dyeing]
H5-33 Staphorster Stipwerk: Dot printing of Staphorst in Overijssel: coloured patterns stamped with pin-headed stamps on cloth for the village dress (kraplap, caps). [aka Staphorst dot work]
H5-34 Pavlovo Posad Shawl: Printed woollen shawls of Pavlovo Posad (Moscow region) since the 1860s, with dense roses and borders, first block-printed then screen-printed. [aka Pavlovo Posad scarf]
H5-35 Kelaghayi: Silk headscarves of Azerbaijan (Sheki, Basqal) printed by wooden stamps with resist and natural dyes, with buta motifs (UNESCO 2014). [aka kalagai]
### H6 Felt — Axis: tradition.
H6-1 Kyrgyz and Kazakh Felt: the mosaic felt carpets (shyrdak), pressed felt (ala-kiyiz, tus kiiz) and embroidered felt of the Kyrgyz and Kazakh (UNESCO 2012). [aka shyrdak; ala-kiyiz; tus kiiz]
### H7 Dress & Costume — Axis: tradition.
H7-1 Palestinian Costume: the embroidered thobes and headdresses of Palestinian women, distinct by village and region. [aka thobe; Palestinian dress]
H7-2 Ottoman Court Dress: the kaftans and garments of the Ottoman sultans and court, kept in the Topkapı Palace treasury. [aka Ottoman kaftan]
H7-3 Possum-Skin Cloak: Cloaks of sewn possum pelts incised on the skin side with clan and Country designs, worn by the Aboriginal peoples of south-eastern Australia and revived since the 1990s: Lake Condah and Maiden's Punt cloaks, revival cloaks. [aka possum skin cloak]
H7-4 Gákti: The dress of the Sámi, a tunic of wool or reindeer skin trimmed with woven bands and coloured cloth whose cut and colours mark region and family: Kautokeino gákti, four-winds hat. [aka kolt;kofte;Sami costume]
H7-5 Bunad: The regional folk costumes of Norway, revived and standardised from local dress since the late 19th century, with embroidered bodices, aprons and silver brooches (sølje): Vest-Telemark bunad, Hardanger bunad. [aka bunader]
H7-6 Swedish Folk Costume: The parish costumes of Sweden, worn and revived as signs of local and national identity, with embroidered bodices, pockets and headdresses: Dalarna costumes, Leksand and Rättvik dress. [aka folkdräkt;sockendräkt;bygdedräkt]
H7-7 Tracht: The regional folk dress of the German-speaking Alps and southern Germany, made and worn in local forms with their own headdresses, bodices and embroidery: Bregenzerwald Juppe, Lake Constance Radhaube, Gailtal costume. [aka Trachten;Volkstracht]
H7-8 Traditional Albanian Clothing: The regional folk costume of the Albanians, with distinct ensembles by region and community: xhubleta bell-shaped skirt, fustanella, xhamadan vests. [aka Albanian folk costume]
H7-9 Croatian National Costume: The regional folk costumes of Croatia, from Slavonian gold-embroidered dress to Dalmatian and Zagorje ensembles. [aka Croatian folk costume]
H7-10 Macedonian Traditional Clothing: The regional folk costumes of North Macedonia, with heavily embroidered women's shirts and sleeveless coats. [aka Macedonian folk costume]
H7-11 Erzya Costume: The traditional dress of the Erzya (Mordvin) people, with embroidered white shirts (panar), pulai hip ornament and headdresses. [aka Erzyan national costume]
### H8 Basketry, Barkcloth & Fibre Arts — Axis: tradition. Coiled, twined and plaited basketry, matting and barkcloth (tapa).
H8-1 Dumbara Mats: Hana-fibre mats with symbolic motifs woven by the kinnara community of the Dumbara valley in Sri Lanka (UNESCO 2021). [aka Dumbara rata kalala]
H8-2 Noken: Knotted and woven string bags of bark fibre and orchid stem made by women of Papua (UNESCO urgent safeguarding 2012).
H8-3 Daluang: Javanese beaten-bark paper (dluwang) of paper mulberry, made in Ponorogo and Garut and used for pesantren and wayang beber manuscripts. [aka dluwang;gedog paper]
H8-4 Kumihimo: Japanese braided silk cords made on stands (marudai, takadai), for sword mounts, armour lacing and obijime.
H8-5 Maedeup: Korean decorative knotting in silk cord, with tassels for norigae pendants and ritual objects; makers designated maedeupjang. [aka maedeupjang;Korean knotwork]
H8-6 Chaesang: Korean boxes of dyed bamboo strips woven in patterns; makers designated chaesangjang. [aka chaesangjang;bamboo case weaving]
H8-7 Wanchojang: Korean weaving of sedge (wanchoe) into patterned mats (hwamunseok), baskets and cushions, especially of Ganghwa island. [aka sedge weaving;hwamunseok]
H8-8 Ugandan Barkcloth: Barkcloth beaten from the inner bark of the mutuba fig by Baganda craftsmen, terracotta-coloured, for royal and funerary use (UNESCO 2008). [aka olubugo]
H8-9 Mbuti Barkcloth: Barkcloth of the Mbuti of the Ituri forest, prepared by men and painted by women with linear and dotted designs in gardenia and charcoal. [aka Mbuti barkcloth paintings]
H8-10 Agaseke: Coiled lidded baskets of Rwanda with conical lids and geometric patterns, made by women. [aka peace basket]
H8-11 Panama Hat: Hats plaited from toquilla palm straw in Ecuador (Montecristi, Cuenca), inscribed by UNESCO in 2012. [aka toquilla straw hat;jipijapa]
H8-12 Pomo Basketry: Coiled and twined baskets of the Pomo of northern California, among them the feathered and beaded gift baskets and the art baskets made for sale by named weavers. [aka Pomo baskets;feathered baskets]
H8-13 California Indian Basketry: Twined and coiled basketry of the Native peoples of California and the adjoining Great Basin edge other than the Pomo: Hupa, Karuk and Yurok overlay-twined caps, Yokuts bottleneck baskets, Cahuilla coiled baskets, Washoe degikup. [aka California baskets]
H8-14 Spruce-Root Basketry: Fine twined baskets and hats of split spruce root with false-embroidery grass designs, made by Tlingit and Haida women of the northern Northwest Coast. [aka Tlingit basketry;Haida basketry]
H8-15 Unangan Grass Basketry: Very finely twined wild-rye-grass baskets, wallets and lidded jars of the Unangan (Aleut), above all the Attu baskets with silk false embroidery made for sale after 1867. [aka Aleut basketry;Attu basketry]
H8-16 Plateau Cornhusk Bags: Flat twined bags of Indian hemp with cornhusk false embroidery made by Nez Perce, Yakama, Umatilla and other Columbia Plateau women for root storage and exchange. [aka cornhusk bags;Plateau flat twined bags]
H8-17 Plateau Imbricated Basketry: Coiled cedar-root baskets imbricated with bear grass and cherry bark made by Klickitat, Nlaka'pamux and other Interior Salish weavers: berry baskets, lidded trunks. [aka Klickitat basketry;Nlaka'pamux coiled basketry]
H8-18 Apache Basketry: Coiled willow and devil's-claw ollas and trays and pitched twined water jars of the Western, Jicarilla and Mescalero Apache, with figures of people and animals. [aka Western Apache basketry;Apache ollas]
H8-19 Pima Basketry: Coiled willow, cattail and devil's-claw baskets of the Akimel O'odham (Pima) and Tohono O'odham (Papago), the latter also in yucca and bear grass, with fret and squash-blossom designs. [aka O'odham basketry;Papago basketry;Tohono O'odham basketry]
H8-20 Hopi Basketry: Basketry of the Hopi mesas: coiled galleta-grass and yucca plaques of Second Mesa and wicker plaques of rabbitbrush of Third Mesa, often with katsina figures. [aka Hopi plaques;poota;yungyapu]
H8-21 Rivercane Basketry: Plaited baskets of split rivercane dyed with walnut and bloodroot, made by Cherokee, Chitimacha, Choctaw and Koasati weavers of the Southeast, including the double-woven baskets. [aka Southeastern cane basketry;double-weave basketry]
H8-22 Wabanaki Basketry: Woven brown-ash splint and braided sweetgrass baskets of the Wabanaki (Passamaquoddy, Penobscot, Maliseet, Mi'kmaq) of Maine and the Maritimes, from work baskets to fancy 'curly' baskets. [aka ash splint basketry;sweetgrass baskets]
H8-23 Birchbark Biting: Designs bitten into thin folded sheets of birchbark by Anishinaabe, Cree and Algonquin women, used as patterns for quill and bead embroidery and as works in themselves. [aka birchbark bitings;mazinibaganjigan]
H8-24 Tapa Cloth: Barkcloth beaten from paper-mulberry and other inner bark across the Pacific and painted, stencilled, rubbed or printed with designs: Samoan siapo, Tongan ngatu, Fijian masi, Hawaiian kapa, Niuean hiapo, Omie and Maisin barkcloth. [aka tapa;barkcloth;siapo;ngatu;masi;kapa]
H8-25 ʻIe Tōga: The finely plaited pandanus fine mats of Samoa, and the fine mats of Tonga and Tuvalu, exchanged as the highest valuables at life-crisis ceremonies: ʻie tōga, Tongan kie and taʻovala. [aka fine mat;ie toga;kiekie;fala]
H8-26 Aboriginal Fibre Art: The coiled, looped, twined and plaited fibre work of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander women and men, from string bags and baskets to contemporary fibre sculpture: dillybags, Arnhem Land pandanus baskets, rainforest bicornual baskets, Tjanpi grass sculpture, ghost-net sculpture. [aka Aboriginal weaving;dilly bag]
H8-27 Lalava: Decorative coconut-fibre (sennit, magimagi) lashing of Tonga, Fiji and Samoa, binding the beams of houses and the hulls of canoes in red and black geometric patterns, now also made as freestanding works. [aka sennit lashing;magimagi]
H8-28 Sweetgrass Baskets: Coiled baskets of sweetgrass, bulrush and pine needle sewn with palmetto by the Gullah Geechee of the South Carolina Lowcountry, a West African coiling tradition: fanner baskets, sewing baskets. [aka Gullah sweetgrass baskets]
H8-29 Panier de Mayun: The willow and bramble baskets of Mayun in the Brière marshes (Loire-Atlantique). [aka Mayun basket]
H8-30 Bastabinne: Swedish baskets plaited from strips of spruce bast. [aka spruce-bast basket]
H8-31 Birch-Bark Crafts: Containers, boxes, footwear and objects of folded, woven, sewn and carved birch bark made across Finland, Karelia and Russia: tuohikontti backpacks, tuesok boxes, woven bast shoes. [aka tuohityö;beresta work]
H8-32 Shemogodsk Carving: Openwork carving of birch bark from the Shemoksa river near Veliky Ustyug since the early 19th century, called 'northern lace', on boxes and tuesoks. [aka Shemogodsk carved birch bark;Shemogodskaya carving]
H8-33 Pärekori: Finnish baskets plaited from pulled wood shingles (päre). [aka shingle basket;making of shingle baskets from pulled shingles]
H8-34 Clubrush Work of Kruunupyy: Weaving of clubrush (bulrush) into mats and objects in Kruunupyy (Kronoby), Finland. [aka clubrus work tradition in Kruunupyy]
H8-35 Himmeli: Geometric hanging mobiles of straw tubes threaded together, made at Christmas in Finland and the Baltic (Lithuanian sodai). [aka straw mobile;sodai]
H8-36 Straw Weaving in Belarus: Belarusian straw weaving of ritual and decorative objects — figures, baskets, spiders (paŭki) and boxes (UNESCO 2025). [aka Belarusian straw craft]
H8-37 Turiisk Straw Plaiting: Straw plaiting tradition of Turiisk hromada in Volyn Oblast (Ukraine).
H8-38 Iza Vine Weaving: Willow-vine basket and furniture weaving of the village of Iza in Zakarpattia (Ukraine). [aka vine weaving in Iza village]
H8-39 Shchitky Reed Weaving: Reed weaving tradition of Shchitky and Pysarivka (Vinnytsia Oblast, Ukraine).
### H9 Beadwork, Quillwork & Featherwork — Axis: tradition.
H9-1 Zulu Beadwork: Glass-bead regalia of the Zulu of KwaZulu-Natal in brick stitch, with colour codes by region and age: ucu necklaces, love-letter panels (incwadi), beaded aprons and skirts. [aka Zulu beadwork]
H9-2 Maasai Beadwork: Beaded collars, earrings and headdresses of the Maasai of Kenya and Tanzania: flat disc collars, wedding necklaces.
H9-3 Yoruba Beadwork: Beaded regalia of Yoruba kings and Ifa priests: beaded crowns with veils (ade), diviners' bags, beaded staffs.
H9-4 Mexican Featherwork: Feather mosaic of Mesoamerica and colonial New Spain (amanteca), from Aztec shields and headdresses to Christian feather paintings: Moctezuma's headdress, Mass of Saint Gregory (1539). [aka arte plumario;plumería]
H9-5 Porcupine Quillwork: Embroidery with flattened dyed porcupine quills on hide and birchbark across the Plains, Subarctic and Northeast: Lakota quilled robes and shirts, Mi'kmaq and Odawa quilled birchbark boxes, Dene quilled bands. [aka quillwork]
H9-6 Plains Beadwork: Glass-bead embroidery of the peoples of the Great Plains and Plateau in lane-stitch and overlay techniques, in tribal styles: Lakota fully beaded vests and cradleboards, Crow (Apsáalooke) horse gear, Blackfeet and Cree floral beadwork. [aka Plains Indian beadwork]
H9-7 Haudenosaunee Raised Beadwork: Raised (embossed) glass beadwork on velvet by Mohawk, Tuscarora and Seneca women, above all the 'whimsies' (pincushions, frames, purses) sold at Niagara Falls from the mid-19th century. [aka Iroquois beadwork;beaded whimsies]
H9-8 Wampum: Tubular beads of white whelk and purple quahog shell strung and woven into belts and strings by the Haudenosaunee and Algonquian peoples of the Northeast to record treaties and council business: Hiawatha Belt, Two Row Wampum. [aka wampum belts]
H9-9 Hawaiian Featherwork: The featherwork of the Hawaiian chiefs: red and yellow honeyeater and ʻiʻiwi feathers knotted to olonā netting as emblems of rank and divinity: ʻahu ʻula cloaks and capes, mahiole helmets, lei hulu, feathered god images. [aka ʻahu ʻula;Hawaiian feather cloaks]
H9-10 Kahu Huruhuru: Māori feather cloaks: muka cloaks finger-woven (whatu) with feathers of kiwi, kererū, kākā and other birds set in the weft, the highest-ranking cloaks of the 19th century onward: kahu kiwi, kahu kererū. [aka feather cloak;kahu kiwi]

## I Woodwork, Lacquer & Furniture — test: made of wood, or of lacquer and papier-mâché: carved and inlaid woodwork, lacquerware and furniture.
  key: 1. Lacquer or lacquered papier-mâché? → I2. 2. Furniture? → I3. 3. Otherwise carved, turned or inlaid wood → I1.
  key: 4. A musical instrument? → I4.
### I1 Carved & Inlaid Woodwork — Axis: tradition and technique.
I1-1 Mashrabiya: screens of turned wooden spindles in latticework, for windows and balconies of Cairo and the Arab world. [aka mashrabiyya; shanashil; rawshan]
I1-2 Khatam: Persian micro-marquetry of wood, bone and metal assembled in geometric patterns (Shiraz, Isfahan). [aka khatam-kari; Persian marquetry]
I1-3 Ottoman Mother-of-Pearl Inlay: Ottoman woodwork inlaid with mother-of-pearl, tortoiseshell and ivory (sedefkârlık): Qur'an stands, chests and thrones. [aka sedef kakma; sedefkârlık]
I1-4 Palestinian Olive-Wood Carving: carved olive wood of Bethlehem and Jerusalem, devotional and souvenir objects since the 16th century. [aka Bethlehem olive wood]
I1-5 Islamic Woodwork: carved and joined woodwork of the Islamic world outside the named leaves: minbars, Qur'an stands and cenotaphs carved in bevelled and geometric strapwork (the Minbar of Nur al-Din, Aleppo 1168). [aka Islamic woodcarving; minbar carving]
I1-6 ʿAjami: painted, gessoed and gilded wooden panelling of Ottoman Syrian reception rooms (the Aleppo Room, 1600–03; the Damascus Room, 1707). [aka ajami; Damascene painted wood]
I1-7 Kondapalli Toys: Carved and painted softwood (tella poniki) figures of Kondapalli in Andhra Pradesh: Dasavatara sets, ambari elephant. [aka Kondapalli bommalu]
I1-8 Nirmal Toys: Painted softwood toys and lacquered paintings of the Naqqash families of Nirmal in Telangana. [aka Nirmal paintings]
I1-9 Malay Woodcarving: The carved wood of the Malay Peninsula, above all of Kelantan and Terengganu, for palaces, houses, boats and weapon hilts, in pierced floral (awan larat) designs. [aka ukiran Melayu]
I1-10 Yosegi-zaiku: Wood mosaic of Hakone: blocks of differently coloured woods glued into patterns and planed into veneers for boxes and puzzle boxes. [aka yosegi;Hakone marquetry]
I1-11 Ainu Woodcarving: Ainu carving of wood in curving and scale designs: prayer sticks (ikupasuy), trays, knife hilts (makiri) and ritual objects, and the carved trays of Nibutani. [aka ikupasuy;Nibutani-bori]
I1-12 Hwagak: Korean painted ox-horn veneer: thin translucent horn sheets painted on the back in bright colours and laid on boxes and furniture; makers designated hwagakjang. [aka hwagakjang;painted ox horn]
I1-13 Mate Burilado: Engraved and pyrographed gourds of the central Peruvian Andes (Huanta, Cochas Chico), with dense narrative scenes. [aka mates burilados;Peruvian carved gourds]
I1-14 Bentwood Boxes: Kerfed, steamed and bent cedar boxes and chests of the Northwest Coast, carved and painted in formline: storage and grease boxes, bentwood chests, bent-corner bowls. [aka bent-corner boxes;tawt'a gaay]
I1-15 Fijian Clubs: The carved hardwood war clubs (i-wau) of Fiji, made by specialist carvers in named forms and often finely incised: totokia (pineapple club), gata (gunstock club), i-ula throwing clubs, kinikini paddle clubs. [aka i-wau;i-ravu;totokia]
I1-16 Tramp Art: American and European woodwork of c. 1870–1940 built up from notch-carved layers of cigar-box and crate wood: boxes, frames, miniature furniture.
I1-17 Abramtsevo-Kudrino Carving: Flat relief wood carving of curling floral ornament on boxes, ladles and furniture, created at the Abramtsevo estate workshop by Vasily Vornoskov of Kudrino (late 19th century) and continued at Khotkovo. [aka Kudrino carving]
I1-18 Untsukul Inlay: Woodwork of the Avar village of Untsukul (Dagestan) inlaid with fine silver and cupronickel wire and plates in geometric patterns: walking sticks, boxes, vases. [aka Untsukul notch;Untsukul wood inlay]
I1-19 Hutsul Wood Carving: Carving and inlay of the Hutsuls of the Ukrainian Carpathians — chip carving, beadwork inlay and brass inlay on boxes, plates and crosses (Shkriblyak family). [aka Hutsul artistic wood carving]
I1-20 Chernihiv Wood Carving: Traditional artistic wood carving of Chernihiv Oblast (Ukraine). [aka artistic wood carving of Chernihiv Oblast]
I1-21 Konjic Woodcarving: Woodcarving of Konjic in Bosnia and Herzegovina, with dense floral and geometric relief on furniture and interiors (UNESCO 2017).
I1-22 Kuksa: Carved drinking cups of birch burl made by the Sámi and Finns. [aka guksi;kåsa]
### I2 Lacquer — Axis: tradition.
I2-1 Persian Lacquer: painted and varnished papier-mâché and wood of Iran — pen boxes, mirror cases, bindings and caskets — from the Timurid and Safavid periods to its Zand and Qajar flowering (Muhammad Zaman, 'Ali Ashraf, Muhammad Isma'il). [aka Persian lacquer; lacquer painting]
I2-2 Kashmiri Papier-Mâché: painted and varnished papier-mâché of Kashmir (boxes, vases), with floral and hunting designs. [aka kar-e-kalamdani]
I2-3 Edirnekâri: Ottoman lacquer of Edirne (17th–19th century): writing boxes, bookbindings and furniture painted with flowers under varnish. [aka Edirne work; Ottoman lacquer]
I2-4 Channapatna Toys: Lathe-turned wooden toys of Channapatna in Karnataka finished in coloured lac.
I2-5 Etikoppaka Toys: Lac-turned wooden toys of Etikoppaka in Andhra Pradesh coloured with vegetable dyes.
I2-6 Burmese Lacquerware: Coiled-bamboo and horsehair vessels coated in thitsi lacquer, engraved (yun) or gilded and glass-inlaid, made above all in Bagan: offering vessels (hsun-ok), betel boxes, manuscript chests. [aka yun;Burmese lacquer]
I2-7 Lai Rot Nam: Thai gold-and-black lacquer decoration on wood, made by gold leaf on a lacquer ground with a water-washed resist: manuscript cabinets, temple doors and window shutters. [aka Thai gold and black lacquer]
I2-8 Carved Lacquer: Chinese lacquer built up in many layers and carved in relief, from the Song: tihong cinnabar lacquer, tixi guri scrolls, Yongle and Qianlong imperial boxes. [aka diaoqi;qidiao;cinnabar lacquer]
I2-9 Maki-e: Japanese lacquer decorated with sprinkled gold and silver powder, from the Heian period: Kōdaiji maki-e, Hon'ami Kōetsu's writing boxes, inrō, Nanban export lacquer. [aka makie]
I2-10 Japanese Lacquerware: Japanese lacquer other than maki-e, made in regional centres named for their wares: Wajima-nuri, Tsugaru-nuri, Kamakura-bori, Negoro, and raden mother-of-pearl inlay. [aka urushi;shikki]
I2-11 Ryukyuan Lacquerware: Lacquer of the Ryukyu kingdom (15th–19th century) with red grounds, mother-of-pearl inlay, gold-leaf (chinkin) and relief (tsuikin) decoration. [aka Ryukyu shikki]
I2-12 Najeonchilgi: Korean lacquer inlaid with mother-of-pearl, from Goryeo sutra boxes to Joseon clothing chests; makers designated najeonjang and chiljang. [aka najeon;Korean mother-of-pearl lacquer]
I2-13 Yi Lacquerware: Red, yellow and black painted lacquer of the Yi (Nuosu) of Liangshan in Sichuan: wooden bowls, cups and armour. [aka Nuosu lacquer;Yi lacquer]
I2-14 Kintsugi: Japanese repair of broken ceramics with lacquer dusted with gold, valued as an aesthetic in its own right since the 16th century. [aka kintsukuroi]
I2-15 Mexican Lacquerware: Lacquer (maque) of Mexico made with insect and seed-oil lacquers on gourds and wood, by inlay or painting, in its regional centres: Olinalá boxes, Pátzcuaro gilded trays, Chiapa de Corzo gourds. [aka maque;laca]
I2-16 Barniz de Pasto: Decoration of wood with sheets of mopa-mopa resin cut and applied in colour, practised in Pasto (Nariño) since the colonial period (UNESCO urgent safeguarding 2020). [aka mopa-mopa]
I2-17 Khokhloma: Painted and varnished turned woodenware of the Nizhny Novgorod region (Semyonov, Khokhloma) since the 17th century, with red and black foliage over a gold-like ground produced by tin powder and oven-hardened varnish: bowls, spoons, ladles. [aka Khokhloma painting;Khokhloma lacquerware]
I2-18 Palekh Miniature: Lacquer miniature painting in egg tempera on black papier-mâché boxes from Palekh, created by former icon painters in 1923 (Golikov), with fairy-tale scenes in gold. [aka Palekh lacquer]
I2-19 Fedoskino Miniature: Lacquer miniature painting in oils on papier-mâché from Fedoskino near Moscow since 1795 (Lukutin factory), with genre scenes over mother-of-pearl and metal-leaf grounds. [aka Fedoskino lacquer]
I2-20 Mstyora Miniature: Lacquer miniature painting of Mstyora (Vladimir region) from 1923, with landscapes in warm tones and gold ornament on papier-mâché. [aka Mstyora lacquer]
I2-21 Kholuy Miniature: Lacquer miniature painting of Kholuy (Ivanovo region) from 1934, by former icon painters, with dense narrative scenes in blue and green. [aka Kholuy lacquer]
### I3 Furniture & Decorative Arts Styles — Axis: period style of furniture and the decorative arts (Louis XIV to Mid-Century Modern); designers and makers are kinds.
I3-1 Soban: Korean small portable dining tables of wood in regional styles (Naju, Haeju, Tongyeong); makers designated sobanjang. [aka sobanjang]
I3-2 International Gothic (decorative arts): Courtly late Gothic goldsmiths' work, tapestry and glass (c. 1250–1450): the Goldenes Rössl, the Apocalypse Tapestry of Angers.
I3-3 Mannerism (decorative arts): Renaissance and Mannerist decorative arts and ornament (c. 1450–1600): Cellini's Salt Cellar, Jamnitzer's goldsmiths' work, Pannemaker's tapestries.
I3-4 Louis XIV Style: French court decorative arts c. 1660–1715 under Le Brun: Boulle marquetry, Gobelins tapestries, Versailles silver furniture. [aka Louis Quatorze]
I3-5 Louis XV Style: The Rococo decorative arts of France c. 1715–1760: Cressent's commodes, Meissonnier's ornament, Vincennes-Sèvres porcelain. [aka Rocaille;Louis Quinze]
I3-6 Louis XVI Style: Early neoclassical decorative arts in France c. 1760–1790: Riesener's furniture, Marie-Antoinette's boudoir at Fontainebleau. [aka Louis Seize]
I3-7 Empire Style: Napoleonic neoclassical design c. 1800–1830: Percier and Fontaine's Malmaison, Jacob-Desmalter's furniture.
I3-8 Biedermeier: Central European design and domestic culture 1815–1848, simple and comfortable, and its painting of bourgeois life: Danhauser furniture, Waldmüller's genre scenes.
I3-9 Victorian Decorative Arts: British decorative arts 1837–1901, historicist and industrial: Pugin's Gothic Revival furniture, Minton majolica.
I3-10 Arts and Crafts Movement: The reform of design and craft after Ruskin and Morris (1860s–1920s): Morris & Co. wallpapers, Ashbee's silver, Stickley's Craftsman furniture.
I3-11 Art Nouveau: The international style of organic line and ornament c. 1890–1910 across design, architecture and painting: Gallé's glass, Mucha's posters, Horta's Hôtel Tassel, Klimt's Beethoven Frieze. [aka Jugendstil;Secession style;Modernisme;Stile Liberty]
I3-12 Art Deco: The streamlined geometric luxury style of the 1920s–1930s: Ruhlmann's furniture, Lalique glass, Cassandre's posters, the Chrysler Building.
I3-13 Bauhaus: The German school of art and design (1919–1933) and its functional modernism: Breuer's Wassily Chair, Brandt's tea infuser, Albers's weavings.
I3-14 Mingei: The Japanese folk-craft movement (1926) of Yanagi Sōetsu, Hamada and Kawai: Hamada Shōji's stoneware, Serizawa Keisuke's stencil dyeing. [aka Folk craft movement]
I3-15 Mid-Century Modern: Modern design c. 1933–1965: the Eames Lounge Chair, Arne Jacobsen's Egg chair, Castiglioni lamps.
I3-16 Contemporary Design: Design by makers born from 1945: product, furniture, fashion and graphic design.
### I4 Musical Instruments — Axis: tradition. Instruments studied as decorated objects, whatever their material.
I4-1 Vanuatu Slit Gongs: The upright slit gongs of northern Vanuatu (Ambrym, Malakula), breadfruit trunks hollowed and carved with ancestral faces and raised in dancing grounds of the grade societies: atingting kon (Ambrym), Malakula slit gongs; tree-fern grade figures as a kind. [aka atingting kon;tamtam;slit drum]

## J Photography — test: an image made with a camera. Photographers and artists are kinds of their school or movement.
### J1 Photographic Schools & Movements — Axis: period style and movement (pictorialism, straight photography, documentary, contemporary).
J1-1 Early Photography: Photography of the daguerreotype, calotype and wet-collodion decades (1839–c. 1880): Talbot's The Open Door, Nadar's portraits, Cameron's portraits. [aka Daguerreian era]
J1-2 Pictorialism: The first international art-photography movement (1880s–1920s), soft-focus and hand-worked: Steichen's The Pond—Moonlight, Käsebier's Blessed Art Thou among Women.
J1-3 Straight Photography: Sharp-focus, unmanipulated modernist photography from the 1910s (Group f/64): Strand's Wall Street, Weston's Pepper No. 30, Adams's Moonrise, Hernandez. [aka Pure photography]
J1-4 New Vision: The experimental photography of the 1920s–1930s (Neues Sehen) of angles, close-ups and photograms: Moholy-Nagy's photograms, Rodchenko's Pioneer Girl. [aka Neues Sehen]
J1-5 Humanistic Photography: French post-war photography of everyday life and its dignity: Doisneau's Kiss by the Hôtel de Ville, Willy Ronis's Le Petit Parisien. [aka Photographie humaniste;street photography]
J1-6 Documentary Photography: Photography that records people, places and events for social or historical purpose, including photojournalism: Lange's Migrant Mother, Evans's Let Us Now Praise Famous Men, Salgado's Workers. [aka Social documentary photography;Photojournalism]
J1-7 Contemporary Photography: Photography by artists born from 1945, staged, documentary and conceptual.

## K Architecture — test: a building or designed built whole, placed by its style; buildings are representative works and architects are kinds.
  key: 1. A revival built outside the tradition it revives? → K2 (P8).
  key: 2. A style of the Islamic world? → K1.
  key: 3. Otherwise the style's own tradition: ancient Mediterranean and Near East → K3; medieval Europe → K4; Europe and its colonies 1400–1850 → K5; modern and contemporary movements → K6; South Asia → K7; East Asia → K8; Southeast Asia → K9; Africa → K10; the Indigenous Americas → K11; named vernacular and Oceanic traditions → K12.
### K1 Islamic Architecture — Axis: the dynastic and regional style named by architectural history; sub-styles and architects are its kinds.
K1-1 Umayyad Architecture: the architecture of the Umayyad caliphate (661–750): the Dome of the Rock, the Great Mosque of Damascus and the desert palaces (Qusayr 'Amra, Khirbat al-Mafjar, Mshatta).
K1-2 Abbasid Architecture: the architecture of the Abbasid caliphate (750–1258): Samarra's mosques and palaces, the round city of Baghdad and the Tulunid mosque of Cairo.
K1-3 Fatimid Architecture: the architecture of the Fatimid caliphate in Ifriqiya and Egypt (909–1171): al-Azhar, al-Hakim and the gates of Cairo.
K1-4 Moorish Architecture: the Islamic architecture of al-Andalus and the Maghreb: the caliphal Great Mosque of Córdoba and Madinat al-Zahra, the taifa Aljafería, Almoravid and Almohad mosques and the Nasrid Alhambra. [aka Andalusian architecture; Hispano-Moorish architecture]
K1-5 Mudéjar Architecture: the Islamic-derived architecture built under Christian rule in Iberia (12th–16th century), in brick, tile and carpentry (artesonado ceilings). [aka Mudejar style]
K1-6 Seljuk Architecture: the architecture of the Great Seljuqs in Iran and Central Asia (11th–12th century): four-iwan mosques, domed chambers, brick tomb towers and minarets. [aka Great Seljuk architecture]
K1-7 Anatolian Seljuk Architecture: the architecture of the Seljuqs of Rum and the beyliks in Anatolia (12th–14th century): caravanserais, madrasas with carved stone portals, and tomb towers. [aka Rum Seljuk architecture; beylik architecture]
K1-8 Mamluk Architecture: the architecture of the Mamluk sultanate in Egypt and Syria (1250–1517): funerary complexes with domes and minarets, ablaq masonry and carved stone domes.
K1-9 Ilkhanid Architecture: the architecture of the Mongol Ilkhanate in Iran (1256–1335): the Mausoleum of Öljeitü at Soltaniyeh and great mosques with tiled portals.
K1-10 Timurid Architecture: the architecture of Timur and his successors in Central Asia and Iran (1370–1507): Samarkand, Herat and Mashhad, with bulbous ribbed domes and tile-mosaic revetment.
K1-11 Safavid Architecture: the architecture of Safavid Iran (1501–1736), above all Isfahan's Naqsh-e Jahan square, its mosques, palaces and bridges. [aka Isfahani style]
K1-12 Ottoman Architecture: the architecture of the Ottoman empire from the early Bursa and Edirne mosques through the classical domed mosques of Mimar Sinan to Ottoman Baroque. [aka classical Ottoman architecture; Ottoman Baroque]
K1-13 Qajar Architecture: the architecture of Qajar Iran (1789–1925): palaces and mosques with polychrome figural tiles (Golestan Palace).
K1-14 Indo-Islamic Architecture: the Islamic architecture of the South Asian sultanates before and beside the Mughals: Delhi Sultanate (Qutb Minar), Gujarat, Bengal, Malwa and the Deccan sultanates. [aka Indo-Saracenic (pre-colonial); Sultanate architecture]
K1-15 Mughal Architecture: the architecture of the Mughal empire (1526–1857): Humayun's tomb, Fatehpur Sikri, the Taj Mahal and the Mughal gardens. [aka Mughal gardens]
K1-16 Sudano-Sahelian Architecture: the earthen architecture of the West African Sahel: mud-brick mosques with projecting torons (Djenné, Timbuktu, Agadez). [aka Sudanese style]
K1-17 Swahili Architecture: the coral-stone architecture of the Swahili coast (Kilwa, Lamu, Zanzibar): mosques, pillar tombs and carved doors.
K1-18 Chinese Islamic Architecture: mosques of China in the Chinese timber-frame idiom (Xi'an, Niujie) and the brick and tiled mosques of Xinjiang. [aka Sino-Islamic architecture]
K1-19 Javanese Mosque Architecture: the mosques of Java and the Malay world built in the regional timber idiom with stacked pyramidal (tajug) roofs instead of domes (Demak, Kudus, Menara Kudus). [aka masjid Jawa; Indonesian vernacular mosque]
### K2 Historicist & National Revivals — Axis: the style revived. Built at scale; the pilot holds the revivals of Islamic styles.
K2-1 Moorish Revival Architecture: the 19th–20th-century revival of Moorish and Islamic forms in Europe and the Americas — synagogues, theatres, baths and palaces. [aka Neo-Moorish; Islamic Revival]
K2-2 Neo-Mudéjar: the Spanish revival of Mudéjar brick and tile (late 19th–early 20th century): bullrings, stations and schools. [aka Neomudéjar]
K2-3 Neo-Mamluk Architecture: the revival of Mamluk forms in Egypt and beyond (late 19th–20th century). [aka Mamluk Revival]
K2-4 First National Architectural Movement: the Turkish national style of 1908–1930 reviving Ottoman and Seljuq forms (Vedat Tek, Mimar Kemaleddin). [aka Turkish Neoclassicism; Neo-Ottoman]
K2-5 Indo-Saracenic Architecture: the British colonial revival style in India (from the 1870s) combining Indo-Islamic and Mughal forms with Gothic and Neoclassical planning: railway stations, courts and palaces (Robert Fellowes Chisholm, Charles Mant). [aka Indo-Gothic; Hindoo style]
