K2-6 Gothic Revival Architecture: The revival of medieval Gothic forms from the mid-18th century to the early 20th, in churches, colleges, parliaments and houses: the Palace of Westminster, Cologne Cathedral's completion, St Patrick's Cathedral, New York. [aka Neo-Gothic; Victorian Gothic; Gothick]
K2-7 Tudor Revival Architecture: The revival of Tudor and Elizabethan domestic forms (half-timbering, tall chimneys, mullioned windows) in late 19th- and 20th-century houses: Jacobethan country houses, mock-Tudor suburban villas. [aka Mock Tudor; Tudorbethan]
K2-8 Romanesque Revival Architecture: The 19th-century revival of round-arched Romanesque forms, from the German Rundbogenstil to Richardsonian Romanesque in America: Trinity Church, Boston, the Natural History Museum, London. [aka Neo-Romanesque; Norman Revival]
K2-9 Neo-Byzantine Architecture: The 19th–20th-century revival of Byzantine domes, arcades and banded masonry, in Orthodox and Catholic churches and public buildings: Sacré-Cœur, Paris, Westminster Cathedral, Saint Sava, Belgrade. [aka Byzantine Revival architecture]
K2-10 Renaissance Revival Architecture: The 19th-century revival of Italian, French and Northern Renaissance forms for palaces, banks, museums and civic buildings: the Vienna State Opera, the Reform Club, the Rijksmuseum. [aka Neo-Renaissance]
K2-11 Italianate Architecture: The 19th-century picturesque revival of Italian villa and palazzo forms, with bracketed eaves and towers, popular in Britain and North America: Osborne House, Italianate row houses of San Francisco. [aka Italianate style]
K2-12 Second Empire Style: The Napoleon III-era style of mansard roofs and rich Baroque-Renaissance ornament, spread from Paris to the Americas: the Palais Garnier, Philadelphia City Hall, the Eisenhower Executive Office Building. [aka Napoleon III style; Second Empire architecture]
K2-13 Baroque Revival Architecture: The late 19th- and early 20th-century revival of Baroque massing and ornament for public buildings and theatres: the Berlin Cathedral, Edwardian Baroque town halls. [aka Neo-Baroque; Edwardian Baroque]
K2-14 Greek Revival Architecture: The late 18th- and 19th-century revival of Greek temple forms and orders, dominant in the United States and northern Europe: the Second Bank of the United States, the Altes Museum, St George's Hall, Liverpool.
K2-15 Egyptian Revival Architecture: The revival of ancient Egyptian pylons, obelisks and lotus columns after Napoleon's Egyptian campaign, in cemeteries, prisons, synagogues and cinemas: the Egyptian Hall, Piccadilly, the Tombs, New York. [aka Egyptomania (architecture)]
K2-16 Beaux-Arts Architecture: The academic classical architecture taught at the École des Beaux-Arts in Paris (c. 1830–1920), symmetrical and richly sculpted, carried to the Americas: the Petit Palais, New York Public Library, Grand Central Terminal. [aka Beaux-Arts style]
K2-17 Eclecticism in Architecture: Nineteenth- and early 20th-century architecture freely combining historical styles in one building: Gründerzeit apartment blocks, Stile Umbertino palaces of Rome, Russian eclectic mansions. [aka Eclectic architecture; historicism (eclectic)]
K2-18 Victorian Architecture: The succession of revival and picturesque styles built in Britain and its empire and in North America during Victoria's reign (1837–1901): Stick style houses, Eastlake villas, Australian Victorian terraces. [aka Victorian house]
K2-19 Queen Anne Style Architecture: The late 19th-century British and American domestic style of red brick or shingle, asymmetrical towers, bays and turrets: Norman Shaw's Swan House, the Carson Mansion. [aka Queen Anne Revival]
K2-20 Shingle Style Architecture: The American seaside and suburban house style of the 1880s wrapped in continuous wood shingles: the Isaac Bell House, the William Watts Sherman House. [aka Shingle style]
K2-21 Colonial Revival Architecture: The American (and Brazilian) revival from the 1876 Centennial of colonial Georgian and Dutch forms: Georgian Revival houses, Dutch Colonial Revival gambrel houses. [aka Neo-Colonial]
K2-22 Spanish Colonial Revival Architecture: The early 20th-century revival of Spanish colonial forms in California, Florida and the American Southwest, after the 1915 Panama–California Exposition: Santa Barbara County Courthouse, the California Building, San Diego. [aka Spanish Revival]
K2-23 Mission Revival Architecture: The Californian revival (1890–1920) of the Spanish missions' arcades, curved parapets and bell towers: the Mission Inn, Riverside, the Santa Fe Depot, San Diego. [aka California Mission style]
K2-24 Pueblo Revival Architecture: The 20th-century revival in New Mexico and Arizona of Pueblo and Spanish adobe forms: the New Mexico Museum of Art, La Fonda on the Plaza. [aka Pueblo style; Santa Fe style]
K2-25 Mayan Revival Architecture: The 1920s–1930s American revival of Maya and Mesoamerican ornament: Frank Lloyd Wright's Hollyhock House, the Mayan Theater, Los Angeles. [aka Maya Revival]
K2-26 Neo-Manueline: The Portuguese and Brazilian revival (from the 1840s) of Manueline ornament: Pena Palace, Rossio Station, the Royal Portuguese Cabinet of Reading, Rio. [aka Neomanuelino]
K2-27 Russian Revival Architecture: The 19th-century revival of medieval Muscovite and Byzantine-Russian forms in the Russian Empire: the State Historical Museum, the Church of the Savior on Blood, the Upper Trading Rows. [aka Pseudo-Russian style; Neo-Russian style]
K2-28 Russian Neoclassical Revival: The revival of Russian Neoclassicism and Empire style in the Russian Empire c. 1900–1917 (retrospectivism): Fomin's Polovtsov Dacha, Zholtovsky's Tarasov House. [aka Retrospectivism]
K2-29 Romanian Revival Architecture: The Romanian national style (from the 1880s) reviving Brâncovenesc and Moldavian forms: Ion Mincu's Lahovari House, Mincu's Bufetul de la Șosea. [aka Neo-Romanian; Neo-Brâncovenesc]
K2-30 National Romantic Style: The Nordic and Baltic national architecture of c. 1890–1920 drawing on vernacular and medieval forms: Helsinki Central Station, Stockholm City Hall, Norwegian dragestil. [aka National Romanticism; Jugend national romanticism]
K2-31 Zakopane Style: The Polish national style created by Stanisław Witkiewicz (1890s) from Podhale highland timber building: Villa Koliba, the Chapel in Jaszczurówka. [aka Witkiewicz style]
K2-32 Swiss Chalet Style: The 19th-century picturesque revival of the Alpine timber chalet, built across Europe and Scandinavia: Norwegian sveitserstil villas, spa pavilions. [aka Sveitserstil; chalet style]
K2-33 Federation Architecture: The Australian architecture of c. 1890–1915, combining Queen Anne, Arts and Crafts and Romanesque elements: Federation Queen Anne houses, the Queen Victoria Building. [aka Federation style]
K2-34 Rustic Architecture: The North American log-and-stone style of the Adirondack Great Camps and the national parks (1870s–1940s): Old Faithful Inn, Timberline Lodge. [aka National Park Service rustic; Parkitecture]
### K3 Ancient Mediterranean & Near Eastern Architecture — Axis: the civilisation's style (Egyptian, Mesopotamian, Greek orders, Roman).
K3-1 Ancient Egyptian Architecture: The stone architecture of pharaonic Egypt (c. 3000 BC – AD 300): pyramids, temples and rock-cut tombs: the Pyramids of Giza, Karnak, Abu Simbel. [aka Egyptian architecture]
K3-2 Architecture of Mesopotamia: The mud-brick architecture of Sumer, Babylonia and Assyria: ziggurats, temples, palaces and city walls: the Ziggurat of Ur, the Ishtar Gate, the palaces of Nimrud. [aka Mesopotamian architecture; Assyrian architecture; Babylonian architecture]
K3-3 Achaemenid Architecture: The palace and tomb architecture of the Persian empire (550–330 BC): Persepolis, Pasargadae, Naqsh-e Rostam. [aka Persian imperial architecture]
K3-4 Parthian Architecture: The architecture of the Arsacid empire (247 BC – AD 224), with the first monumental iwans and Hellenistic ornament: Hatra, Nisa, Ashur's Parthian palace. [aka Arsacid architecture]
K3-5 Sasanian Architecture: The architecture of the Sasanian empire (224–651): vaulted iwans, squinch domes and fire temples: Taq Kasra, Palace of Ardashir, Takht-e Soleyman. [aka Sassanid architecture]
K3-6 Minoan Architecture: The Bronze Age palace architecture of Crete (c. 2000–1450 BC): Knossos, Phaistos, Malia.
K3-7 Mycenaean Architecture: The Late Bronze Age citadels, megara and tholos tombs of mainland Greece: the Lion Gate, Mycenae, the Treasury of Atreus, Tiryns. [aka Mycenaean Greek architecture]
K3-8 Ancient Greek Architecture: The architecture of the Greek world from the Archaic to the Hellenistic period, defined by the temple and the orders: the Parthenon, the Temple of Hephaestus, the Theatre of Epidaurus. [aka Classical Greek architecture]
K3-9 Etruscan Architecture: The architecture of the Etruscans (c. 900–27 BC): timber-and-terracotta temples, arched gates and rock-cut tombs: the Banditaccia necropolis, the Arch of Augustus, Perugia.
K3-10 Ancient Roman Architecture: The architecture of Rome and its empire (c. 500 BC – AD 400), of the arch, vault, dome and concrete: the Pantheon, the Colosseum, the Pont du Gard. [aka Roman architecture]
K3-11 Nabataean Architecture: The rock-cut tomb and temple façades of the Nabataeans (1st century BC – 2nd century AD): Al-Khazneh, Ad Deir at Petra, Hegra.
### K4 Medieval European Architecture — Axis: period style (Byzantine, Romanesque, Gothic and their regional schools).
K4-1 Early Christian Architecture: The church architecture of the late Roman empire (4th–6th century): basilicas, baptisteries and martyria: Old St Peter's, Santa Sabina, the Mausoleum of Galla Placidia. [aka Early Christian art and architecture; Paleochristian architecture]
K4-2 Byzantine Architecture: The architecture of the Eastern Roman empire (c. 330–1453): domed churches on pendentives and the cross-in-square plan: Hagia Sophia, San Vitale, Ravenna, Hosios Loukas. [aka Byzantine style]
K4-3 Serbo-Byzantine Architecture: The medieval church architecture of Serbia (12th–15th century) in its Raška, Vardar and Morava schools: Studenica, Gračanica, Ravanica. [aka Serbian medieval architecture]
K4-4 Architecture of Kievan Rus': The church architecture of Kievan Rus' and its successor principalities (10th–13th century): Saint Sophia Cathedral, Kyiv, Saint Sophia, Novgorod, the Church of the Intercession on the Nerl. [aka Old Russian architecture]
K4-5 Armenian Architecture: The stone church architecture of Armenia from the 4th century, with domed central plans and carved khachkars: Etchmiadzin Cathedral, Zvartnots, the Cathedral of the Holy Cross, Aghtamar. [aka Architecture of Armenia; Armenian church architecture]
K4-6 Architecture of Georgia: The medieval church and fortress architecture of Georgia (Caucasus): Jvari, Svetitskhoveli, Bagrati Cathedral. [aka Georgian church architecture]
K4-7 Pre-Romanesque Architecture: The architecture of western Europe between the end of Rome and c. 1000: Visigothic, Asturian, Anglo-Saxon, Lombard and Mozarabic churches: Santa María del Naranco, San Juan de Baños, Escomb Church. [aka Pre-Romanesque art and architecture]
K4-8 Carolingian Architecture: The architecture of the Carolingian empire (c. 780–900), reviving Roman forms: the Palatine Chapel, Aachen, the Lorsch gatehouse, Germigny-des-Prés.
K4-9 Ottonian Architecture: The architecture of the Ottonian empire (c. 936–1056) leading into Romanesque: St. Michael's Church, Hildesheim, St. Cyriakus, Gernrode.
K4-10 Romanesque Architecture: The round-arched, massive-walled architecture of Europe c. 1000–1200, with its regional schools: Speyer Cathedral, Sainte-Foy, Conques, Durham Cathedral, Pisa Cathedral. [aka Romanesque style]
K4-11 Arab-Norman Architecture: The architecture of Norman Sicily (1091–1194) combining Latin, Byzantine and Islamic forms: the Palatine Chapel, Palermo, Monreale Cathedral, the Zisa. [aka Norman-Arab-Byzantine architecture; Siculo-Norman architecture]
K4-12 Moldavian Style: The church architecture of medieval Moldavia (15th–16th century), Gothic-Byzantine in plan with painted exteriors: Voroneț, Sucevița, Putna Monastery. [aka Moldavian architectural style]
K4-13 Gothic Architecture: Medieval European architecture of the pointed arch, rib vault and flying buttress (c. 1140–1520): Chartres Cathedral, Sainte-Chapelle, Cologne Cathedral.
### K5 Early Modern European & Colonial Architecture — Axis: period style (Renaissance, Baroque, Rococo, Neoclassicism, Palladianism) and its colonial forms.
K5-1 Tudor Architecture: The English architecture of 1485–1603, late Perpendicular Gothic turning to Renaissance: Hampton Court Palace, King's College Chapel, Hardwick Hall. [aka Tudor style]
K5-2 Plateresque: The Spanish style of c. 1490–1560 covering façades in fine silversmith-like Renaissance ornament: the University of Salamanca façade, San Esteban, Salamanca, Acolman. [aka Plateresco]
K5-3 Manueline: The Portuguese late Gothic style of the reign of Manuel I (1495–1521), with maritime and twisted-rope ornament: Jerónimos Monastery, Belém Tower, the Convent of Christ, Tomar. [aka Portuguese late Gothic]
K5-4 Herrerian Style: The austere classical style of Juan de Herrera for Philip II (from 1567): El Escorial, Valladolid Cathedral, the Archivo de Indias. [aka Herrerian architecture; estilo herreriano]
K5-5 Churrigueresque: The late Baroque style of Spain and New Spain (c. 1700–1760) with estípite pilasters and dense ornament: the Sagrario Metropolitano, Mexico City, the Transparente, Toledo. [aka Ultrabarroco]
K5-6 Rococo Architecture: The late Baroque architecture and interior style of c. 1730–1770, light, asymmetrical and ornamental: Sanssouci, the Wieskirche, the Amalienburg. [aka Rococo; Louis XV style]
K5-7 Uzorochye: The ornate Muscovite church and palace architecture of the 17th century, with tented roofs, kokoshniki and tilework: the Church of the Nativity in Putinki, the churches of Yaroslavl. [aka Russian patterned style; Moscow Baroque (early)]
K5-8 Brâncovenesc Style: The Wallachian style of Constantin Brâncoveanu's reign (1688–1714) combining Renaissance, Baroque and Ottoman elements: Horezu Monastery, Mogoșoaia Palace. [aka Brâncovenesc art; Brâncovean style]
K5-9 Palladian Architecture: The architecture derived from Andrea Palladio's villas and treatise, revived in 18th-century Britain, Ireland and America: Villa Rotonda, Chiswick House, Monticello. [aka Palladianism; Neo-Palladianism]
K5-10 Georgian Architecture: The British and colonial American classical architecture of 1714–1830, in brick and stone terraces, houses and churches: the Royal Crescent, Bath, Merrion Square, colonial Williamsburg.
K5-11 Empire Style: The Napoleonic phase of Neoclassicism (c. 1800–1830), spread across Europe and Russia: the Arc de Triomphe du Carrousel, La Madeleine, the Russian Empire buildings of St Petersburg. [aka Style Empire]
K5-12 Federal Architecture: The American classical architecture of c. 1780–1830, after Adam and Palladio: the White House, Massachusetts State House, the Gardner–Pingree House. [aka Federal style]
K5-13 Pombaline Style: The rational, earthquake-resistant style of the rebuilding of Lisbon after 1755: the Baixa Pombalina, Praça do Comércio. [aka Pombaline architecture]
K5-14 Architecture of the Bulgarian Revival: The Bulgarian urban houses, churches and schools of the National Revival (18th–19th century): Plovdiv Old Town, Koprivshtitsa, the Rila Monastery's residential wings. [aka Bulgarian National Revival architecture]
K5-15 American Colonial Architecture: The architecture of the English colonies of North America (17th–18th century): First Period timber houses, saltbox and Cape Cod types, Georgian brick: the Paul Revere House, Westover Plantation. [aka Colonial architecture (United States)]
K5-16 Spanish Colonial Architecture: The architecture of the Spanish empire in the Americas and the Philippines (16th–19th century): missions, cathedrals and plazas: the Mission San Xavier del Bac, Cusco Cathedral, the Baroque churches of the Philippines. [aka Spanish colonial style]
K5-17 Andean Baroque: The mestizo Baroque of the colonial Andes (c. 1680–1780) with flat carved Indigenous-inflected ornament: San Lorenzo, Potosí, the Jesuit Church of Arequipa. [aka Mestizo Baroque; Andean Hybrid Baroque]
K5-18 Portuguese Colonial Architecture: The architecture of the Portuguese empire in Brazil, Africa and Asia (16th–19th century): the historic centre of Ouro Preto, Salvador's Pelourinho, the churches of Old Goa. [aka Portuguese colonial style]
K5-19 French Colonial Architecture: The architecture of the French colonies in North America and the Caribbean: raised galleried Creole houses and Quebec stone houses: the Bolduc House, the French Quarter, New Orleans. [aka Creole architecture; French colonial style]
K5-20 Dutch Colonial Architecture: The architecture of the Dutch colonies (New Netherland, the East Indies, Suriname), with gambrel and stepped-gable roofs: the Jan Martense Schenck House, Dutch colonial houses of Batavia. [aka Dutch colonial style]
K5-21 Cape Dutch Architecture: The whitewashed, thatched, ornate-gabled farmhouses of the Dutch Cape Colony (17th–18th century): Groot Constantia, Vergelegen. [aka Cape Dutch style]
K5-22 Renaissance Architecture: European architecture c. 1400–1600 reviving classical orders: Brunelleschi's Duomo dome, Bramante's Tempietto, Palladio's Villa Rotonda.
K5-23 Baroque Architecture: European and colonial architecture c. 1600–1750 of movement and spatial drama: Borromini's San Carlo, Versailles, Melk Abbey.
K5-24 Neoclassical Architecture: Architecture c. 1750–1850 on Greek and Roman models: the Panthéon, Paris, the British Museum, the Brandenburg Gate.
### K6 Modern & Contemporary Architecture — Axis: movement (Art Nouveau, Modernism, Art Deco, Brutalism, Postmodernism, Deconstructivism).
K6-1 Arts and Crafts Architecture: The domestic architecture of the Arts and Crafts movement (c. 1860–1920), vernacular materials and honest craftsmanship: the Red House, Voysey's Broadleys, the Gamble House. [aka Arts and Crafts movement; Domestic Revival]
K6-2 Chicago School: The steel-frame commercial architecture of Chicago (1880s–1910s), the first skyscrapers: the Home Insurance Building, the Monadnock Building, the Carson Pirie Scott Building. [aka Commercial style]
K6-3 Prairie School: The Midwestern American architecture of Frank Lloyd Wright and his circle (c. 1900–1917), low and horizontal: Robie House, Unity Temple, the Darwin D. Martin House. [aka Prairie style]
K6-4 Czech Cubism: The Cubist architecture of Bohemia (1910–1925), with faceted crystalline façades and its later rondocubist phase: the House of the Black Madonna, the Kovařovic Villa, the Adria Palace. [aka Cubist architecture]
K6-5 Expressionist Architecture: The early 20th-century European architecture of distorted, sculptural and crystalline form, in brick and concrete: the Einstein Tower, the Chilehaus, Het Schip.
K6-6 Futurist Architecture: The architecture of speed and the machine announced by Sant'Elia (1914) and its later heirs, from Googie to neo-futurism: Sant'Elia's Città Nuova, the Theme Building, LAX.
K6-7 Art Deco Architecture: The architecture of Art Deco (c. 1920–1940), geometric, stepped and ornamented, with its Streamline Moderne phase: the Chrysler Building, Miami Beach's Art Deco District, Napier. [aka Art Deco]
K6-8 Bauhaus Architecture: The architecture of the Bauhaus school and its masters (1919–1933) and its diaspora: the Bauhaus Dessau, the Masters' Houses, Tel Aviv's White City. [aka Bauhaus style]
K6-9 International Style: The modern architecture named by Hitchcock and Johnson (1932): volume rather than mass, regularity, no applied ornament: Villa Savoye, the Barcelona Pavilion, the Seagram Building. [aka International modernism]
K6-10 Constructivist Architecture: The Soviet avant-garde architecture of the 1920s–early 1930s: the Shukhov Tower, the Narkomfin Building, the Rusakov Workers' Club. [aka Soviet avant-garde architecture]
K6-11 Reform Architecture: The German-speaking reform movement of c. 1900–1930 that rejected historicism for simple, regional building (Heimatschutz, Um 1800): Paul Schultze-Naumburg's Cecilienhof, Hellerau. [aka Reformarchitektur]
K6-12 Traditionalist School: The Dutch traditionalist architecture of c. 1900–1960 in brick and tile, with its Delft and Bossche schools: Granpré Molière's Vreewijk, Dom Hans van der Laan's Roosenberg Abbey. [aka traditionalism (Dutch architecture)]
K6-13 Nordic Classicism: The pared-down classical architecture of Scandinavia and Finland c. 1910–1930: Asplund's Stockholm City Library, the Woodland Chapel, Aalto's early Jyväskylä buildings. [aka Swedish Grace]
K6-14 Stripped Classicism: The simplified, unornamented classicism of the 1920s–1940s for government buildings in democracies and dictatorships alike: the Federal Reserve Board Building, the Palais de Chaillot. [aka Starved Classicism]
K6-15 Stalinist Architecture: The monumental socialist-realist architecture of the USSR and its satellites (1933–1955): the Seven Sisters, the Palace of Culture and Science, Karl-Marx-Allee. [aka Socialist Classicism; socialist realism (architecture)]
K6-16 Fascist Architecture: The architecture of Fascist Italy (1922–1943), from rationalism to monumental stripped classicism: EUR, Rome, the Casa del Fascio, Como, the Palazzo della Civiltà Italiana. [aka Stile Littorio; architecture of the Fascist period]
K6-17 Nazi Architecture: The architecture of the Third Reich (1933–1945), monumental neoclassical for the state and Heimatstil for housing: the Nuremberg Rally Grounds, Tempelhof Airport, the Reich Chancellery. [aka Architecture of the Third Reich]
K6-18 Mid-Century Modern Architecture: The American-led domestic and commercial modernism of c. 1945–1970: post-and-beam houses, glass pavilions and regional variants: the Eames House, the Stahl House, Miami Modern hotels. [aka Mid-century modern]
K6-19 High-tech Architecture: Late-modern architecture from the 1970s that expresses structure and services as industrial technology: the Centre Pompidou, the Lloyd's Building, HSBC Main Building, Hong Kong. [aka Structural expressionism]
K6-20 New Classical Architecture: Contemporary classical and traditional architecture since the 1980s: Poundbury, Robert Adam's Sackler Library, the Schermerhorn Symphony Center. [aka New Classicism; Neo-historism]
K6-21 Art Nouveau Architecture: Architecture of the Art Nouveau c. 1890–1914: Horta's Hôtel Tassel, Guimard's Métro entrances, Gaudí's Casa Batlló.
K6-22 Modern Architecture: Architecture of the modern movement (c. 1900–1975): functionalism, the International Style and their regional forms: Villa Savoye, the Barcelona Pavilion, Fallingwater. [aka Modernist architecture;International Style;Functionalism]
K6-23 Brutalist Architecture: Modernist architecture of the 1950s–1970s in exposed concrete and massive forms: Unité d'Habitation, the Barbican Estate, Boston City Hall. [aka Brutalism]
K6-24 Postmodern Architecture: Architecture from the 1960s–1990s returning to ornament, history and irony: Venturi's Vanna Venturi House, Graves's Portland Building, Stirling's Neue Staatsgalerie.
K6-25 Deconstructivism: Architecture from the 1980s of fragmented, non-rectilinear forms: Gehry's Guggenheim Bilbao, Libeskind's Jewish Museum Berlin, Hadid's Vitra Fire Station.
K6-26 Contemporary Architecture: Architecture by architects born from 1945, in plural idioms from parametric design to vernacular-sustainable building: Kéré's Gando school, SANAA's Rolex Learning Center.
### K7 South Asian Architecture — Axis: temple and building tradition (Nagara, Dravidian, Vesara, Buddhist, Indo-Islamic outside K1).
K7-1 Nagara Architecture: The northern Hindu and Jain temple tradition whose sanctum rises in a curvilinear shikhara of stacked courses (latina, shekhari and bhumija forms): Kandariya Mahadeva at Khajuraho, Mahabodhi Temple, Kopeshwar temple, Khidrapur. [aka Nagara style;North Indian temple architecture;Indo-Aryan temple style]
K7-2 Kalinga Architecture: The Odishan temple tradition of the rekha, pidha and khakhara deula with jagamohana halls (7th–13th century): Lingaraja Temple, Konark Sun Temple, Jagannath Temple, Puri. [aka Odishan temple architecture;Orissan style;deula]
K7-3 Maru-Gurjara Architecture: The temple tradition of Gujarat and Rajasthan (11th–13th century and its revival) with richly carved marble interiors, especially for Jain patrons: Dilwara temples, Mount Abu, Modhera Sun Temple, Ranakpur. [aka Māru-Gurjara architecture;Solanki style;Chaulukya style]
K7-4 Dravidian Architecture: The southern Hindu temple tradition of stepped pyramidal vimanas, pillared mandapas and monumental gopurams in walled precincts: Shore Temple, Mahabalipuram, Brihadisvara Temple, Thanjavur, Meenakshi Temple, Madurai, Virupaksha Temple, Hampi. [aka Dravida style;South Indian temple architecture;Tamil architecture;kovil]
K7-5 Vesara: The Deccan temple tradition combining Nagara and Dravidian forms, developed under the Chalukyas and Hoysalas: Pattadakal, Mahadeva Temple, Itagi, Chennakesava Temple, Belur, Hoysaleswara Temple, Halebidu. [aka Karnata Dravida;Chalukyan style;Deccan temple architecture]
K7-6 Kerala Architecture: The timber-and-laterite building tradition of Kerala with steep tiled and copper roofs: circular and rectangular sreekovil temples, koothambalam theatres, nalukettu houses and palaces: Vadakkunnathan Temple, Padmanabhapuram Palace, Mattancherry Palace. [aka architecture of Kerala;Kerala temple architecture]
K7-7 Bengal Temple Architecture: The brick and terracotta temples of Bengal (16th–19th century) whose curved roofs translate the thatched hut: the chala types (do-chala, char-chala, at-chala), jor-bangla and the ratna (pinnacled) types: Jor Bangla Temple, Bishnupur, Rasmancha, Dakshineswar Kali Temple. [aka Bengal temple style;terracotta temples of Bengal]
K7-8 Buddhist Architecture: The architecture of Buddhism in its South Asian homeland: stupas, chaitya halls and vihara monasteries from the Mauryas to the Palas, and the monasteries of Gandhara: Great Stupa, Sanchi, Dhamek Stupa, Nalanda Mahavihara, Takht-i-Bahi. [aka stupa;chaitya;vihara;early Buddhist architecture]
K7-9 Indian Rock-cut Architecture: Caves, halls and monolithic temples excavated from living rock (3rd century BCE–10th century): Ajanta Caves, Karla chaitya, Kailasa Temple, Ellora, Elephanta Caves. [aka rock-cut architecture in India;cave temples]
K7-10 Sinhalese Architecture: The Buddhist and royal architecture of Sri Lanka: dagobas, vatadages, image houses and monasteries of Anuradhapura and Polonnaruwa, and the timber halls of the Kandyan kingdom: Ruwanweli Maha Seya, Jetavanaramaya, Gal Vihara, Temple of the Tooth. [aka architecture of Sri Lanka;Sri Lankan Buddhist architecture]
K7-11 Newar Architecture: The brick-and-timber architecture of the Newars of the Kathmandu valley: multi-roofed pagoda temples, shikhara temples, stupas, bahal courtyards and palace squares with carved struts and windows: Pashupatinath, Nyatapola, Changu Narayan, Swayambhunath. [aka Newa architecture;Nepalese pagoda style]
K7-12 Tibetan Buddhist Architecture: The monastic and palace architecture of Tibet and the Tibetan Buddhist world: battered stone-and-earth walls, flat roofs, gilded roofs, chortens and kumbums: Potala Palace, Jokhang, Drepung, Gyantse Kumbum. [aka Tibetan architecture;gompa]
K7-13 Dzong Architecture: The fortress-monasteries of Bhutan and Tibet housing civil and monastic administration around a central utse tower: Punakha Dzong, Paro Rinpung Dzong, Trongsa Dzong. [aka dzong]
K7-14 Rajput Architecture: The forts, palaces, havelis and cenotaphs of the Rajput kingdoms of Rajasthan and Central India (8th–19th century), with jharokhas and chhatris: Amber Fort, Chittorgarh Fort, Kumbhalgarh, Jaisalmer Fort. [aka Rajasthani architecture;Hill Forts of Rajasthan]
K7-15 Stepwell: The stepped subterranean wells of western India built as civic and devotional monuments, Hindu and Islamic (vav, baoli): Rani ki vav, Adalaj Stepwell, Chand Baori. [aka vav;baoli;bawdi]
K7-16 Sikh Architecture: The gurdwaras and memorials of the Sikh tradition (17th–19th century) drawing on Mughal and Rajput forms, with fluted domes and gilded upper storeys: Harmandir Sahib, Takht Sri Patna Sahib, Gurdwara Baba Atal. [aka gurdwara architecture]
K7-17 Harappan Architecture: The planned fired-brick cities of the Indus Valley Civilisation (c. 2600–1900 BCE): grid streets, drains, granaries and citadels: Great Bath, Mohenjo-daro, Harappa, Dholavira. [aka Indus Valley Civilisation architecture]
### K8 East Asian Architecture — Axis: building tradition (Chinese, Japanese, Korean).
K8-1 Chinese Architecture: The classical timber-frame tradition of China, with bracket sets (dougong), raised platforms and sweeping tiled roofs, used for palaces, altars, temples, pagodas and gateways and codified in official manuals (Yingzao Fashi): Forbidden City, Temple of Heaven, Foguang Temple, Giant Wild Goose Pagoda. [aka Chinese imperial architecture;Chinese temple architecture]
K8-2 Siheyuan: The north-Chinese courtyard house of four halls round a central yard, the model of the Beijing hutong: Prince Gong Mansion, Qiao Family Compound. [aka quadrangle;courtyard house]
K8-3 Yaodong: The earth-sheltered cave dwellings of the Loess Plateau, cut into cliffs, sunk round pit courtyards or built as vaults. [aka cave dwelling;loess cave house]
K8-4 Hakka Walled Village: The fortified communal dwellings of the Hakka and Minnan of southern China: the earthen tulou of Fujian, weilongwu and walled villages: Tianluokeng tulou cluster, Chengqi Lou. [aka Fujian tulou;weilongwu]
K8-5 Hui-style Architecture: The merchant houses, ancestral halls and memorial arches of Huizhou (southern Anhui), with whitewashed walls, stepped horse-head gables and carved brick, wood and stone: Xidi, Hongcun. [aka Huizhou architecture;Huipai architecture]
K8-6 Lingnan Architecture: The building tradition of Guangdong and Guangxi: wok-ear gables, ceramic ridge figures, arcaded qilou shophouses and Lingnan gardens: Chen Clan Ancestral Hall, Foshan Ancestral Temple. [aka Cantonese architecture]
K8-7 Hokkien Architecture: The Minnan building tradition of southern Fujian, Taiwan and the Hokkien diaspora: swallowtail ridges, red brick and cut-porcelain (jian nian) roof decoration on temples and houses: Bangka Lungshan Temple, Kaiyuan Temple, Quanzhou. [aka Minnan architecture;Taiwanese temple architecture]
K8-8 Sichuanese Architecture: The timber and stilted vernacular of Sichuan and Chongqing (chuan-dou framing, diaojiaolou), with its Song–Yuan temple halls. [aka Bashu architecture]
K8-9 Shikumen: The Shanghai lane-house (lilong) type of the 1860s–1930s joining a Chinese courtyard plan to Western terrace housing behind stone-framed gates: Xintiandi, Tianzifang. [aka lilong]
K8-10 Shinto Shrine Architecture: The architecture of Japanese Shinto shrines (jinja) in named styles (zukuri) defined by roof, entrance and plan: Ise Grand Shrine (shinmei), Izumo Taisha (taisha), Kamigamo Shrine (nagare), Nikkō Tōshō-gū (gongen). [aka Shinto architecture;jinja architecture]
K8-11 Japanese Buddhist Architecture: The temple architecture of Japanese Buddhism in its named styles: wayō, daibutsuyō, zenshūyō and the mixed setchūyō, with pagodas, halls, gates and storehouses: Hōryū-ji, Tōdai-ji, Byōdō-in, Engaku-ji Shariden. [aka Japanese Buddhist temple architecture]
K8-12 Shinden-zukuri: The Heian aristocratic residence of a main hall facing a south garden and pond, linked to wings by corridors: Kyoto Imperial Palace Shishinden, Byōdō-in (derived). [aka shinden style]
K8-13 Shoin-zukuri: The warrior-class residential style of the Muromachi to Edo periods with tatami rooms, tokonoma alcove, staggered shelves, built-in desk and sliding fusuma: Ninomaru Palace, Nijō Castle, Kojō-in, Onjō-ji. [aka shoin style]
K8-14 Sukiya-zukuri: The informal residential and tea-house style derived from the chashitsu, using natural materials and restrained finish: Katsura Imperial Villa, Tai-an, Jo-an. [aka sukiya style]
K8-15 Minka: The vernacular houses of Japan's farmers, craftsmen and merchants: farmhouses (nōka) and townhouses (machiya), in regional types such as gasshō-zukuri and magariya: Shirakawa-gō, Kyoto machiya. [aka nōka;kominka]
K8-16 Japanese Castle Architecture: The fortified residences of the daimyō (16th–17th century): stone ramparts, moats, gates and the multi-storey tenshu keep: Himeji Castle, Matsumoto Castle, Osaka Castle. [aka Japanese castle;shiro]
K8-17 Kofun: The mounded tombs of Japan's Kofun period (3rd–7th century), above all the keyhole-shaped zenpō-kōen-fun, often moated and set with haniwa: Daisen Kofun, Mozu-Furuichi kofun group, Ishibutai Kofun. [aka mounded tomb;keyhole tomb]
K8-18 Ryukyuan Architecture: The architecture of the Ryukyu kingdom (Okinawa): red-tiled gusuku castles and palaces, utaki sacred groves and turtleback and gable-roofed tombs: Shuri Castle, Tamaudun, Nakagusuku Castle. [aka Okinawan architecture;gusuku]
K8-19 Giyōfū Architecture: The pseudo-Western buildings of early Meiji Japan (1860s–1880s) made by Japanese carpenters imitating Western forms in timber and plaster: Former Kaichi School, Tsukiji Hotel, Former Saiseikan Hospital. [aka pseudo-Western style;Japanese-Western eclectic]
K8-20 Korean Architecture: The timber-frame architecture of Korea's palaces, temples, shrines and fortresses, classified by bracket system (jusimpo, dapo, ikgong) and painted in dancheong: Gyeongbokgung, Bulguksa, Jongmyo, Haeinsa Janggyeong Panjeon. [aka traditional Korean architecture]
K8-21 Hanok: The traditional Korean house with ondol-heated rooms, wooden maru floors and tiled or thatched roofs, set by geomancy: Bukchon Hanok Village, Hahoe Folk Village, Yangdong Village. [aka Korean traditional house]
### K9 Southeast Asian Architecture — Axis: building tradition (Khmer, Javanese, Burmese, Thai, Cham).
K9-1 Khmer Architecture: The temple-mountains, galleried enclosures and prasat towers of the Khmer empire and its predecessors (7th–13th century) in successive named styles (Sambor Prei Kuk, Preah Ko, Bakheng, Banteay Srei, Baphuon, Angkor Wat, Bayon): Angkor Wat, Banteay Srei, Bayon, Preah Vihear. [aka Angkorian architecture]
K9-2 Candi Architecture: The Hindu and Buddhist stone temples and stupas of classical Indonesia (7th–15th century), in the Central Javanese (Mataram) and East Javanese (Singhasari, Majapahit) styles and in Sumatra: Borobudur, Prambanan, Candi Penataran, Muara Takus. [aka candi;Javanese candi;Javanese temple]
K9-3 Balinese Architecture: The Hindu-Balinese building tradition of walled pura temples with split gates (candi bentar), meru towers and pavilions, and compound houses laid out by cosmological axes: Pura Besakih, Pura Taman Ayun, Pura Ulun Danu Bratan. [aka pura;Balinese temple]
K9-4 Javanese Traditional House: The timber houses of Java in ranked roof types (joglo, limasan, kampung) with the open pendopo pavilion in front: Kraton Yogyakarta pendopo, Mangkunegaran Palace. [aka joglo;pendopo;omah]
K9-5 Burmese Architecture: The Buddhist architecture of Myanmar: bell-shaped zedi stupas, hollow gu temples, pyatthat spires and teak monasteries, from Pagan to Mandalay: Ananda Temple, Shwezigon, Shwedagon Pagoda, Shwenandaw Monastery. [aka Myanmar architecture;Bamar architecture]
K9-6 Thai Architecture: The Buddhist and royal architecture of Thailand, in the art-historical styles of Sukhothai, Lanna, Ayutthaya and Rattanakosin: wat compounds with ubosot, vihara, chedi, prang and mondop: Wat Mahathat, Sukhothai, Wat Phra Si Sanphet, Wat Phrathat Doi Suthep, Wat Phra Kaew. [aka architecture of Thailand]
K9-7 Lao Architecture: The Buddhist architecture of Laos (Lan Xang) in the Luang Prabang, Vientiane and Xieng Khouang temple styles with sweeping multi-tiered roofs and that stupas: Wat Xieng Thong, Pha That Luang, Wat Si Saket. [aka Lan Xang architecture]
K9-8 Cham Architecture: The brick tower-temples (kalan) of the Champa kingdoms in central and southern Vietnam (7th–15th century) in successive styles (Mỹ Sơn E1, Đồng Dương, Tháp Mắm): Mỹ Sơn, Po Nagar, Po Klong Garai. [aka Champa architecture]
K9-9 Vietnamese Architecture: The timber architecture of Vietnam's pagodas (chùa), communal houses (đình), citadels and imperial tombs, from the Lý to the Nguyễn: One Pillar Pagoda, Temple of Literature, Thiên Mụ Pagoda, Imperial City of Huế. [aka architecture of Vietnam]
K9-10 Batak Architecture: The saddle-roofed timber houses (rumah adat) and granaries of the Batak peoples of North Sumatra, raised on piles and carved and painted with gorga ornament. [aka Batak house;rumah Batak]
K9-11 Bahay Kubo: The Philippine nipa hut: a stilted house of bamboo and timber with a thatched nipa roof, the base type of lowland Filipino vernacular. [aka nipa hut]
K9-12 Bahay na Bato: The Spanish-colonial Filipino town house (18th–19th century) with a stone lower storey and a timber upper storey with capiz-shell sliding windows: Vigan, Casa Manila. [aka stone house]
K9-13 Sino-Portuguese Architecture: The shophouses and mansions of Phuket, Penang and the Straits Settlements (late 19th–early 20th century) mixing Hokkien plans with European façades. [aka Straits eclectic;Peranakan shophouse]
### K10 African Architecture — Axis: building tradition (Sudano-Sahelian, Ethiopian rock-hewn, Great Zimbabwe, Swahili).
K10-1 Aksumite Architecture: The stone architecture of the Aksumite kingdom (1st–7th century) with its 'monkey-head' timber-and-rubble walls, palaces and carved monolithic stelae: Northern Stelae Field, Aksum, Dungur palace, Debre Damo. [aka Axumite architecture]
K10-2 Ethiopian Rock-hewn Churches: The monolithic and semi-monolithic churches carved from rock in Ethiopia (10th–13th century and later): Bete Giyorgis, Lalibela, Bete Medhane Alem, Abuna Yemata Guh. [aka rock-hewn churches of Lalibela;Tigray rock churches]
K10-3 Gondarine Architecture: The castle-palaces and churches of the Gondar period (17th–18th century), with battlemented towers and domes: Fasil Ghebbi, Fasilides' Bath, Debre Berhan Selassie. [aka Gondar style]
K10-4 Nubian Pyramids: The steep pyramids, temples and palaces of the Kushite kingdoms of Napata and Meroë (8th century BCE–4th century CE): Meroë, Jebel Barkal, El-Kurru, Musawwarat es-Sufra. [aka Kushite architecture;Meroitic architecture]
K10-5 Great Zimbabwe: The dry-stone architecture of the Zimbabwe culture (11th–19th century): mortarless granite enclosures, coursed walls with chevron and chequer friezes: Great Zimbabwe, Khami, Mapungubwe, Naletale. [aka Zimbabwe culture dry-stone architecture;Zimbabwe tradition]
K10-6 Ksar: The fortified earthen and stone villages, granaries (agadir, ghorfa) and kasbahs of the Amazigh and Saharan world: Aït Benhaddou, Ksar of Ouadane, Ksar Ouled Soltane, Ghardaïa (M'zab). [aka ksour;kasbah;Berber architecture]
K10-7 Hausa Architecture: The earthen architecture of the Hausa city-states of northern Nigeria and Niger: domed and vaulted mud houses and palaces with relief façades (zanko pinnacles): Gidan Rumfa, Kano, Zaria Friday Mosque. [aka Hausa traditional architecture]
### K11 Indigenous American Architecture — Axis: building tradition (Maya, Mesoamerican, Andean, Pueblo, Eastern Woodlands).
K11-1 Maya Architecture: The stone architecture of the Maya (c. 1000 BCE–1500 CE): stepped pyramid-temples, palaces with corbel vaults, ballcourts and stelae plazas, in the regional styles Petén, Usumacinta, Río Bec, Chenes and Puuc: Tikal Temple I, Temple of the Inscriptions, Palenque, Uxmal, Chichen Itza. [aka Mayan architecture]
K11-2 Mesoamerican Architecture: The monumental architecture of non-Maya Mesoamerica: Olmec mounds, the talud-tablero platforms of Teotihuacan, Zapotec and Mixtec Oaxaca, Classic Veracruz and Toltec Tula: Pyramid of the Sun, Monte Albán, Mitla, Pyramid of the Niches, El Tajín. [aka pre-Columbian Mexican architecture]
K11-3 Aztec Architecture: The architecture of the Mexica and their Late Postclassic neighbours (14th–16th century): twin-temple pyramids, palaces and causeway cities: Templo Mayor, Tenayuca, Santa Cecilia Acatitlan, Malinalco. [aka Mexica architecture]
K11-4 Inca Architecture: The fitted-masonry architecture of Tawantinsuyu (15th–16th century): ashlar and polygonal walls, trapezoidal doorways and niches, terraces, roads and tambos: Machu Picchu, Sacsayhuamán, Coricancha, Ollantaytambo. [aka Incan architecture]
K11-5 Andean Architecture: The monumental architecture of the Andes before the Inca: Caral's platform mounds, Chavín's galleried temple, Moche adobe huacas, Tiwanaku's stone platforms, Wari administrative centres, Chimú Chan Chan and Chachapoya Kuélap. [aka pre-Inca Andean architecture]
K11-6 Ancestral Puebloan Architecture: The masonry and adobe great houses, cliff dwellings, kivas and terraced pueblos of the Ancestral Puebloans and their descendants in the US Southwest: Pueblo Bonito, Chaco Canyon, Cliff Palace, Mesa Verde, Taos Pueblo. [aka Pueblo architecture;Anasazi architecture]
K11-7 Mound Builders: The earthen platform mounds, effigy mounds and geometric earthworks of the Eastern Woodlands (Archaic to Mississippian, c. 3500 BCE–1600 CE): Poverty Point, Hopewell Ceremonial Earthworks, Monks Mound, Cahokia, Serpent Mound. [aka Eastern Woodlands earthworks;Mississippian mounds]
### K12 Vernacular & Oceanic Architecture — Axis: building tradition (named vernacular styles, Pacific meeting houses).
K12-1 Wharenui: The carved Māori meeting house on the marae, its frame read as the body of an ancestor: Te Hau ki Tūranga, Te Tokanganui-a-Noho, Rauru. [aka Māori meeting house;whare whakairo]
K12-2 Fale: The Samoan open-sided house with a domed thatched roof on posts, including the round fale tele guest house. [aka Samoan house;fale tele]
K12-3 Maneaba: The great community meeting house of Kiribati and Tuvalu, a vast thatched roof on low posts. [aka mwaneaba]
K12-4 Haus Tambaran: The spirit and men's houses of the Sepik region of Papua New Guinea with soaring painted gable façades. [aka spirit house;men's house]
K12-5 Marae: The stone-paved ceremonial platforms and courts of East Polynesia, including the image ahu of Rapa Nui with their moai: Taputapuātea, Ahu Tongariki, Ahu Tahai. [aka ahu;heiau]
K12-6 Nan Madol: The basalt-prism megalithic architecture of the Saudeleur polity on Pohnpei (c. 1180–1628) and Leluh on Kosrae: artificial islets of stacked columnar basalt. [aka Saudeleur megalithic architecture]
K12-7 Chilotan Architecture: The shingled timber architecture of the Chiloé archipelago, above all the Jesuit-founded wooden churches built by local boatwrights (18th–19th century): Church of Castro, Church of Achao, palafitos of Castro. [aka Chiloé School of religious wooden architecture]
### K13 Gardens & Designed Landscapes — Axis: garden tradition (Persian chahar bagh, Chinese scholar garden, Japanese garden, Italian Renaissance, French formal, English landscape).

## L Art in Place — test: made on or into a fixed surface and studied where it stands — rock art, wall painting, in-situ mosaic and tile revetment, geoglyphs — or made on the living body.
  key: 1. Pecked, engraved or painted on rock? → L1. 2. Painted on a wall or ceiling? → L2. 3. Mosaic, tile revetment or mirror work assembled on site? → L3. 4. A ground figure? → L4. 5. On the body (tattoo, body painting, henna)? → L5.
  key: 6. An ephemeral design drawn on a floor or the ground in powder, sand or paste? → L6.
### L1 Rock Art — Axis: tradition. Built at scale.
L1-1 Bhimbetka Rock Paintings: Painted rock shelters of the Vindhyan hills from the Mesolithic to the historical period (UNESCO 2003): hunting scenes, dancers, Zoo Rock. [aka Bhimbetka rock shelters]
L1-2 Petroglyphs of the Mongolian Altai: The rock carvings and paintings of the Mongolian Altai (UNESCO 2011), from about 11,000 years ago to the Turkic period: Tsagaan Salaa, Upper Tsagaan Gol, Aral Tolgoi. [aka Petroglyphic Complexes of the Mongolian Altai]
L1-3 Tamgaly Petroglyphs: The Bronze Age to medieval rock carvings of Tamgaly and the Semirechye (UNESCO 2004), with sun-headed figures and sacrificial scenes: sun-headed deities, bull and chariot scenes. [aka Tamgaly;Semirechye petroglyphs]
L1-4 Maros-Pangkep Cave Art: Painted and stencilled rock art of the limestone karst of South Sulawesi, among the oldest figurative art known (45,000+ years): Leang Tedongnge warty pig, Leang Bulu' Sipong 4 hunting scene, hand stencils. [aka Sulawesi cave art]
L1-5 Sangkulirang-Mangkalihat Rock Art: Hand stencils and animal paintings of the limestone caves of East Kalimantan and of Niah in Sarawak: Lubang Jeriji Saléh banteng, Niah Painted Cave. [aka Borneo cave art]
L1-6 Zuojiang Huashan Rock Art: Red-ochre rock paintings of the Luo Yue people on cliffs along the Zuojiang river in Guangxi (5th c. BCE–2nd c. CE), with figures and bronze drums (UNESCO 2016). [aka Huashan rock paintings]
L1-7 Bangudae Petroglyphs: Neolithic and Bronze Age petroglyphs on the Daegok stream in Ulsan, with whales, hunters and boats, and the nearby Cheonjeon-ri carvings (UNESCO 2025). [aka Daegokcheon petroglyphs]
L1-8 Fugoppe Cave: Incised figures and signs in the Fugoppe cave in Hokkaido (Epi-Jōmon) and the related Temiya cave carvings. [aka Fugoppe Cave carvings]
L1-9 Helan Mountains Petroglyphs: Petroglyphs pecked on the cliffs of the Helan and Yin mountains of Ningxia and Inner Mongolia, from the Neolithic to the medieval period: masks, animals and hunters (Helankou, Damaidi). [aka Helankou rock art;Yinshan petroglyphs]
L1-10 Saharan Rock Art: Engraved and painted rock art of the Sahara from the Holocene to historical times, by stylistic phases: Bubaline engravings, Round Head paintings, Bovidian (pastoral) paintings, Dabous giraffes. [aka Tassili rock art;Acacus rock art]
L1-11 San Rock Art: Painted and engraved rock art of the San (Bushmen) of southern Africa, over tens of thousands of years: Drakensberg eland paintings, Twyfelfontein engravings, Tsodilo paintings. [aka Bushman rock art]
L1-12 Sasanian Rock Reliefs: Royal reliefs carved on cliff faces in Fars and western Iran by the Sasanian kings, 3rd–7th century, following Achaemenid precedent: Naqsh-e Rostam investitures, Bishapur triumph of Shapur I, Taq-e Bostan. [aka Iranian rock reliefs]
L1-13 Hittite Rock Reliefs: Reliefs of gods and kings carved on rock in Hittite Anatolia, 14th–13th century BC: Yazılıkaya, Fraktin, Hanyeri, Taşçı.
L1-14 Gobustan Rock Art: Petroglyphs of hunters, dancers, animals and reed boats pecked into the rocks of Gobustan in Azerbaijan from the late Upper Palaeolithic onward (UNESCO 2007). [aka Gobustan petroglyphs]
L1-15 Rock Art in the Hail Region: Petroglyphs of humans, animals and inscriptions at Jubbah and Shuwaymis in the Ha'il region of Saudi Arabia, spanning some 10,000 years (UNESCO 2015). [aka Hail rock art;Jubbah and Shuwaymis petroglyphs]
L1-16 Cueva de las Manos: Stencilled hands and hunting scenes of guanacos painted in the Río Pinturas caves of Patagonia from c. 9300 BP (UNESCO 1999). [aka Río Pinturas rock art]
L1-17 Serra da Capivara Rock Art: The painted rock shelters of Serra da Capivara (Piauí), with Nordeste-tradition hunting, dancing and sexual scenes (UNESCO 1991): Pedra Furada, Toca do Boqueirão. [aka Nordeste tradition]
L1-18 Toro Muerto Petroglyphs: Thousands of petroglyphs pecked on volcanic boulders at Toro Muerto (Arequipa) with dancers, camelids and birds, with related Andean sites: Toro Muerto, Toquepala Caves, Miculla.
L1-19 Chiribiquete Rock Paintings: Red-ochre paintings of jaguars, hunters and dancers on the tepuis of Chiribiquete in the Colombian Amazon (UNESCO 2018).
L1-20 Pecos River Style: Large polychrome pictographs of shamanic figures painted in the rock shelters of the Lower Pecos Canyonlands of Texas and Coahuila (c. 2700 BCE onward): White Shaman Shelter, Fate Bell Shelter, Panther Cave. [aka Lower Pecos rock art]
L1-21 Barrier Canyon Style: Archaic pictographs of tall tapering ghost-like figures on the Colorado Plateau of Utah: Great Gallery, Horseshoe Canyon, Sego Canyon. [aka Barrier Canyon rock art]
L1-22 Chumash Rock Painting: Polychrome pictographs of circles, wheels and beings painted in caves of the Chumash territory of southern California (c. 1000–1800): Painted Cave, Painted Rock (Carrizo Plain), Burro Flats. [aka Chumash rock art]
L1-23 Coso Rock Art: Petroglyphs of bighorn sheep, hunters and patterned-body figures in the Coso Range of the Mojave Desert, the largest concentration in North America. [aka Coso Range petroglyphs]
L1-24 Writing-on-Stone Rock Art: Petroglyphs and pictographs of the Plains, incised in the sandstone of the Milk River valley, Alberta, including biographic warrior scenes (Áísínai'pi, UNESCO 2019). [aka Áísínai'pi]
L1-25 Rock Paintings of the Sierra de San Francisco: The Great Murals of Baja California: life-size red and black human and animal figures painted in the rock shelters of the Sierra de San Francisco and Sierra de Guadalupe (UNESCO 1993). [aka Great Murals of Baja California]
L1-26 Arnhem Land Rock Art: The painted rock shelters of the western Arnhem Land plateau and Kakadu, from Dynamic figures to the recent x-ray and contact paintings: Ubirr, Burrungkuy (Nourlangie), Nanguluwur, Injalak Hill. [aka Kakadu rock art;x-ray style]
L1-27 Gwion Gwion Rock Paintings: The fine-line figure paintings of the north-west Kimberley, slender tasselled human figures of great age. [aka Bradshaw paintings;Gwion Gwion]
L1-28 Murujuga Rock Art: The petroglyphs of the Burrup Peninsula and Dampier Archipelago (Murujuga), over a million engravings made across tens of thousands of years. [aka Burrup Peninsula petroglyphs]
L1-29 Sydney Rock Engravings: The outline engravings on Hawkesbury sandstone in the Sydney basin: animals, ancestral beings and people: Ku-ring-gai Chase sites. [aka Sydney Basin rock engravings]
L1-30 Quinkan Rock Art: The painted shelters around Laura on Cape York with the Quinkan spirit figures. [aka Laura rock art]
L1-31 Panaramitee Style: The widespread Australian engraving style of tracks, circles and dots ('track and circle'), among the oldest rock art of the continent: Ewaninga, Panaramitee. [aka track and circle]
L1-32 Māori Rock Art: The charcoal and red-ochre drawings in limestone shelters of South Canterbury and North Otago: Takiroa, Awamoko. [aka Ngāi Tahu rock art]
L1-33 Franco-Cantabrian Cave Art: Upper Palaeolithic painted and engraved cave art of south-western France and northern Spain, c. 40,000–11,000 BP: Chauvet, Lascaux, Altamira, Font-de-Gaume. [aka Palaeolithic cave art;Franco-Cantabrian art]
L1-34 Côa Valley and Siega Verde Rock Art: Open-air Upper Palaeolithic engravings of animals on schist outcrops in the Côa valley (Portugal) and at Siega Verde (Spain), c. 25,000–10,000 BP: Canada do Inferno, Penascosa, Ribeira de Piscos. [aka Côa Valley rock art]
L1-35 Levantine Rock Art: Post-Palaeolithic naturalistic rock painting of eastern Spain, c. 8000–3500 BC, with hunting, warfare and gathering scenes in shelters: Cova dels Cavalls, Roca dels Moros (Cogul), Valltorta. [aka Rock Art of the Mediterranean Basin on the Iberian Peninsula;Levantine art]
L1-36 Iberian Schematic Art: Schematic rock painting and engraving of the Iberian peninsula, c. 5000–1000 BC, associated with the first farming and metal-using cultures: anthropomorphs and idols, Galician petroglyphs of Os Mouchos and Portela da Laxe. [aka Schematic rock art]
L1-37 Cup and Ring Marks: Neolithic and Bronze Age carvings of cups and concentric rings on outcrops and standing stones of Atlantic Europe, above all Britain and Ireland: Cochno Stone, Achnabreck, Roughting Linn. [aka Atlantic rock art;cup-and-ring marks]
L1-38 Rock Carvings in Tanum: Bronze Age rock carvings of Bohuslän in western Sweden, c. 1700–500 BC, with ships, warriors, ploughing scenes and weddings: Vitlycke panel, Aspeberget, Fossum. [aka Tanum rock carvings;hällristningar]
L1-39 Rock Carvings at Alta: Rock carvings of hunter-fisher communities at Alta in Finnmark, c. 5000 BC – AD 0, with reindeer, elk, boats and bear hunts: Hjemmeluft, Bergbukten, Amtmannsnes. [aka Rock Art of Alta]
L1-40 Petroglyphs of Lake Onega and the White Sea: Neolithic petroglyphs of Russian Karelia, c. 4000–2000 BC, on the shores of Lake Onega (swans, solar signs, the 'demon') and at Zalavruga on the White Sea (hunting and boat scenes). [aka Onega petroglyphs;Belomorsk petroglyphs]
L1-41 Ural Pictograms: Red-ochre rock paintings (pisanitsy) on river cliffs of the Urals, Neolithic to Iron Age, with animals, figures and signs: Ignatievskaya Cave, Tagil pisanitsa. [aka pisanitsy;Ural rock paintings]
L1-42 Rock Drawings in Valcamonica: Petroglyphs of the Camunni in the Val Camonica (Lombardy), from the Epipalaeolithic to the Iron Age and beyond: Bedolina Map, Camunian rose, Naquane. [aka Camunian rock art]
### L2 Wall Painting — Axis: tradition.
L2-1 Umayyad Wall Painting: the figural wall paintings of the Umayyad desert palaces, above all the bath of Qusayr 'Amra (8th century). [aka Qusayr 'Amra frescoes]
L2-2 Persian Wall Painting: the palace murals of Safavid and Qajar Iran (Chehel Sotoun, Ali Qapu, Hasht Behesht). [aka Safavid murals; Qajar murals]
L2-3 Al-Qatt Al-Asiri: the geometric wall decoration painted by women inside the houses of the Asir region of Saudi Arabia (UNESCO 2017). [aka Asiri wall painting; nagash]
L2-4 Ajanta Paintings: Buddhist cave murals of Ajanta (2nd century BCE–5th century CE), the classical model for Indian wall painting: Padmapani, Mahajanaka Jataka. [aka Ajanta murals]
L2-5 Sigiriya Frescoes: The 5th-century paintings of women on the rock face of Kashyapa's palace at Sigiriya.
L2-6 Kerala Mural Painting: Temple and palace murals of Kerala (9th–19th century) in five colours: Mattancherry Palace Ramayana, Padmanabhapuram murals.
L2-7 Kandyan Period Painting: Buddhist temple murals of the Kandyan kingdom (1592–1815) in red and yellow narrative registers: Dambulla cave paintings, Degaldoruwa jatakas. [aka Kandyan school]
L2-8 Shekhawati Frescoes: Painted havelis, temples and forts of Shekhawati in Rajasthan, 18th–20th century, mixing Rajput, colonial and popular imagery. [aka Shekhawati murals]
L2-9 Pithora Painting: Ritual wall paintings of horses and deities by the Rathwa and Bhil of Gujarat and Madhya Pradesh. [aka Pithora]
L2-10 Saura Painting: Ritual wall paintings (ittal) of the Sora of Odisha dedicated to Idital. [aka idital;ittal]
L2-11 Sohrai and Khovar Painting: Harvest (sohrai) and marriage-chamber (khovar) wall paintings of Hazaribagh, Jharkhand. [aka Sohrai Khovar]
L2-12 Rangoli: Ritual floor drawing in powder and rice paste across South Asia: kolam, alpana, kalamezhuthu. [aka kolam;alpana]
L2-13 Tibetan Buddhist Wall Paintings: The secco murals of Tibetan Buddhist monasteries and temples across the Himalaya, from the Kashmiri-style programmes of Tabo and Alchi to Shalu, the Gyantse Kumbum and Guge: Alchi Sumtsek, Tabo assembly hall, Shalu murals. [aka Tibetan murals;Himalayan Buddhist murals]
L2-14 Dunhuang Murals: The wall paintings of the Buddhist cave temples of the Mogao Caves at Dunhuang (4th–14th century): Cave 217 Pure Land, Jataka narratives, donor portraits. [aka Mogao murals;Mogao Caves wall paintings]
L2-15 Kizil Murals: The wall paintings of the Buddhist caves of the kingdom of Kucha (Kizil, Kumtura and neighbouring sites), 4th–8th century, in rhomboid narrative grids and lapis blue: Cave of the Sixteen Sword-Bearers, Buddha in Nirvana. [aka Kucha wall painting;Kizil cave paintings]
L2-16 Bezeklik Murals: The wall paintings of the Uyghur Buddhist caves of Bezeklik and the Turfan oasis (Qocho) under the Uyghur kingdom (9th–13th century): pranidhi scenes, Uyghur donor princes. [aka Bezeklik Thousand Buddha Caves murals;Uyghur Buddhist wall painting]
L2-17 Sogdian Wall Painting: The secular and religious murals of Sogdian houses, temples and palaces (5th–8th century) at Panjikent, Afrasiyab and Varakhsha: Rustam cycle, Hall of the Ambassadors, Red Hall of Varakhsha. [aka Penjikent murals;Afrasiab murals]
L2-18 Bagan Murals: The tempera wall paintings of the temples of Bagan (11th–13th century) and their Inwa and Konbaung successors: Abeyadana, Lokahteikpan Jatakas, Ananda temple. [aka Pagan murals;Burmese temple murals]
L2-19 Thai Mural Painting: Tempera wall paintings of Thai Buddhist ordination halls and viharas, late Ayutthaya to early Rattanakosin and Lanna: Wat Phra Kaew Ramakien, Wat Pho, Wat Phumin, Wat Chong Nonsi. [aka Thai temple murals]
L2-20 Yongle Palace Murals: The Daoist murals of the Yongle Palace in Shanxi (14th century), with the procession of deities in the Hall of Three Purities, and the Buddhist murals of the Fahai Temple near Beijing (1443). [aka Yongle Gong murals]
L2-21 Takamatsuzuka Tomb Murals: Painted plaster walls of the late 7th-century tombs of Asuka (Takamatsuzuka and Kitora), with court ladies, the Four Directional Animals and star charts.
L2-22 Goguryeo Tomb Murals: The wall paintings of the Goguryeo tombs near Pyongyang and Ji'an (4th–7th century): hunting scenes, dancers and the Four Guardians (UNESCO 2004). [aka Complex of Goguryeo Tombs]
L2-23 Dancheong: Korean polychrome painting of wooden buildings, temples and palaces in blue, red, yellow, white and black patterns; masters designated dancheongjang. [aka dancheongjang]
L2-24 Ethiopian Church Painting: Wall painting of Ethiopian churches, on plaster and on canvas glued to walls: Debre Berhan Selassie ceiling of winged faces, Lake Tana monastery murals, Lalibela paintings. [aka Ethiopian church murals]
L2-25 Litema: Incised and painted mud-wall decoration of Basotho homesteads in Lesotho and the Free State, renewed by women.
L2-26 Ndebele House Painting: Geometric mural painting of the Southern Ndebele of South Africa on homestead walls, by women (Esther Mahlangu).
L2-27 Imigongo: Rwandan relief panels of cow dung in black, white and red spirals and zigzags, from house walls to boards.
L2-28 Egyptian Tomb Painting: Wall painting in the tombs and temples of pharaonic Egypt, in tempera on plaster: Meidum Geese, Tomb of Nebamun, Tomb of Nefertari, Theban nobles' tombs. [aka Ancient Egyptian painting]
L2-29 Minoan Wall Painting: Fresco painting of Bronze Age Crete and the Minoanised Aegean, c. 1700–1450 BC: Bull-Leaping Fresco from Knossos, Akrotiri Spring Fresco, Fisherman of Thera. [aka Minoan frescoes;Aegean wall painting;Wall Paintings of Thera]
L2-30 Etruscan Tomb Painting: Painted chamber tombs of Etruria, 7th–2nd century BC, chiefly at Tarquinia and Cerveteri: Tomb of the Leopards, Tomb of the Triclinium, François Tomb at Vulci. [aka Etruscan painting]
L2-31 Ancient Greek Painting: Surviving Greek wall painting, mostly from tombs of the 5th–3rd century BC in Macedonia, Thrace and Magna Graecia: Tomb of Philip II at Vergina, Thracian Tomb of Kazanlak, Tomb of the Diver at Paestum. [aka Greek tomb painting;Macedonian tomb painting]
L2-32 Pompeian Wall Painting: Roman wall painting of the Vesuvian cities and Rome, classed by August Mau in four Pompeian Styles, 2nd century BC – AD 79: Villa of the Mysteries, Villa of Poppaea at Oplontis, House of the Vettii. [aka Pompeian Styles;Roman wall painting]
L2-33 Catacomb Painting: Early Christian and Jewish funerary and devotional wall painting of the 2nd–5th century, above all in the catacombs of Rome and at Dura-Europos: Catacomb of Priscilla, Via Latina catacomb, Dura-Europos synagogue. [aka Early Christian wall painting]
L2-34 Byzantine Wall Painting: Fresco programmes of Byzantine and post-Byzantine churches, 6th–15th century: Cappadocian rock-cut churches, painted churches of Troödos, Panselinos at the Protaton, Mystras. [aka Byzantine frescoes;Byzantine church painting]
L2-35 Faras Wall Paintings: Wall paintings of the cathedral of Faras in the Nubian kingdom of Makuria, 8th–14th century, removed in 1961–64 to Warsaw and Khartoum: Bishop Petros with St Peter, Saint Anne, Nativity. [aka Nubian wall painting;Makurian painting]
L2-36 Maya Mural Painting: The wall paintings of the Maya from the Preclassic to the Postclassic: San Bartolo murals, Bonampak Room 2 battle, Calakmul market murals. [aka Maya murals]
L2-37 Teotihuacan Mural Painting: The fresco painting of the apartment compounds of Teotihuacan (c. 200–650 CE): Tepantitla Great Goddess, Tetitla, Atetelco. [aka Teotihuacan murals]
L2-38 Cacaxtla Murals: The Epiclassic murals of Cacaxtla (Tlaxcala, c. 650–900 CE), mixing Maya and central Mexican styles: Battle Mural, Bird Man.
L2-39 Moche Murals: Painted and modelled mud-plaster friezes of Moche temples: Huaca de la Luna Ai Apaec friezes, El Brujo.
L2-40 Mexican Muralism: The public mural movement of post-revolutionary Mexico (1920s–1970s) led by Rivera, Orozco and Siqueiros: Rivera's National Palace murals, Orozco's Hospicio Cabañas, Siqueiros' Polyforum. [aka muralismo mexicano]
L2-41 Chilean Muralism: Chilean public mural painting from the 1940s, from Siqueiros' Chillán murals to the brigade painting of the Brigada Ramona Parra. [aka muralismo chileno]
L2-42 Murals of Revolutionary Nicaragua: Public murals painted in Sandinista Nicaragua (1979–1990) by Nicaraguan and international brigades.
L2-43 Romanesque Wall Painting: Fresco programmes of Romanesque churches, c. 1000–1200: Sant Climent de Taüll, Saint-Savin-sur-Gartempe, San Isidoro, León, Sant'Angelo in Formis. [aka Romanesque frescoes]
L2-44 Gothic Wall Painting: Wall painting in Gothic churches and palaces, 13th–15th century, north of the Alps and in the Adriatic: Albi Cathedral, Saint-Sernin, Beram and Hrastovlje Danse macabre, Albertus Pictor's Swedish churches. [aka Gothic murals]
L2-45 Painted Churches of Northern Moldavia: The 15th–16th-century churches of Bukovina painted inside and out with Byzantine-derived cycles under Stephen the Great and Petru Rareș: Voroneț Last Judgement, Humor, Sucevița, Moldovița. [aka Churches of Moldavia;Bukovina painted monasteries]
L2-46 Scottish Renaissance Painted Ceilings: Tempera-painted timber ceilings of Scottish houses and castles, c. 1550–1650, with emblems, grotesques and heraldry: Prestongrange, Aberdour Castle, Culross Palace.
L2-47 Murals in Northern Ireland: Loyalist and republican political murals painted on gable walls in Northern Ireland since the early 20th century and above all from the 1970s: Falls Road murals, Bogside Artists, King Billy murals. [aka Northern Irish murals]
L2-48 Samchyky Painting: The decorative painting of the walls and interiors of houses in Samchyky village (Khmelnytskyi Oblast, Ukraine), with unique floral patterns. [aka Samchyky decorative painting]
L2-49 Street Art: Unsanctioned and commissioned art in public space from the 1970s: graffiti writing, stencil, paste-up and mural: Banksy's Girl with Balloon, Os Gêmeos's murals, JR's Women Are Heroes. [aka Graffiti art;urban art]
### L3 Mosaic & Tile Revetment — Axis: tradition and technique.
L3-1 Umayyad Mosaics: the glass wall mosaics of the Dome of the Rock and the Great Mosque of Damascus (7th–8th century), made by Byzantine-trained craftsmen.
L3-2 Zellige: geometric mosaic of hand-cut glazed tiles set in plaster, the wall and floor revetment of Morocco and al-Andalus. [aka zillij; zellij]
L3-3 Moʿaraq (Cut-Tile Mosaic): the tile revetment of Iranian and Central Asian architecture: cut-tile mosaic (mo'arraq) of the Timurid and Safavid periods, haft-rangi (seven-colour) tiles and banna'i brick-and-tile. [aka moʿarraq; haft rangi; kashi]
L3-4 Kashi Kari: the tile-mosaic revetment of Mughal and Punjabi buildings (Lahore, Multan, Thatta). [aka kashikari]
L3-5 Aina-kari: mirror mosaic: cut mirror glass set in plaster on walls and ceilings of Iranian palaces and shrines. [aka Persian mirror work; ayeneh-kari]
L3-6 Hellenistic Mosaics: Greek floor mosaics from the 5th to the 1st century BC, from pebble mosaics to tessellated opus vermiculatum: Pella stag hunt, Alexander Mosaic, Sosos's unswept floor. [aka Greek pebble mosaics]
L3-7 Roman Mosaics: Floor and wall mosaics of the Roman world, 1st century BC – 5th century AD, with strong regional schools in North Africa, Antioch and Sicily: Nile mosaic of Palestrina, Antioch mosaics, Villa Romana del Casale, Bardo mosaics. [aka Roman mosaic]
L3-8 Byzantine Mosaics: Glass and stone wall mosaics of Byzantine churches and of the workshops they trained, 5th–14th century: San Vitale, Ravenna, Hagia Sophia, Daphni and Hosios Loukas, Monreale, San Marco.
L3-9 Azulejo: Painted tin-glazed tile panels covering walls of churches, palaces and streets in Portugal, Spain and Brazil from the 16th century: Palace of the Marquises of Fronteira, São Bento station, Valencian ceramic retables. [aka azulejos]
L3-10 Trencadís: Mosaic of broken tile and crockery shards laid on curved surfaces, the cladding of Catalan Modernisme (Gaudí, Jujol): Park Güell, Casa Batlló, Hospital de Sant Pau. [aka pique assiette]
L3-11 Portuguese Pavement: Patterned pavement of black basalt and white limestone setts laid by hand in Portugal and its former colonies since the 19th century: Rossio waves, Copacabana promenade. [aka calçada portuguesa]
L3-12 Soviet Mosaics: Monumental mosaics on public buildings, metro stations and housing of the Soviet Union and its republics, 1920s–1991, a state programme of monumental art: Kyiv and Donetsk school mosaics, Yerevan facade mosaics. [aka Soviet monumental mosaics]
### L4 Geoglyphs — Axis: tradition. Built at scale.
L4-1 Steppe Geoglyphs: The earthen and stone ground figures of the Turgai Trough in northern Kazakhstan (Neolithic–Iron Age), at least 260 squares, rings and crosses: Ushtogay Square, Turgay triradial swastika. [aka Turgai geoglyphs]
L4-2 Gozan no Okuribi: The five bonfire characters and figures (Daimonji, Myōhō, the boat and the torii) lit on the hills around Kyoto on 16 August, laid out as fixed hillside figures. [aka Daimonji]
L4-3 Nazca Lines: Geoglyphs scraped into the desert of the Nazca and Palpa pampas (c. 500 BCE–500 CE), with straight lines and animal figures (UNESCO 1994): Hummingbird, Monkey, Condor, Palpa figures. [aka Nasca Lines;Nazca and Palpa lines]
L4-4 Pintados Geoglyphs: Hillside geoglyphs of the Atacama made by removing or piling stones (c. 600–1500 CE): Cerro Pintados panels, Atacama Giant, Chug-Chug. [aka Atacama geoglyphs;Tarapacá geoglyphs]
L4-5 Acre Geoglyphs: Geometric ditched enclosures of the south-western Amazon (Acre, Brazil), c. 0–1300 CE.
L4-6 Effigy Mounds: Earthen mounds raised in the shapes of animals and serpents in the Eastern Woodlands: Serpent Mound, the bear and bird mounds of Effigy Mounds National Monument. [aka effigy mound]
L4-7 Blythe Intaglios: Giant human and animal figures scraped into the desert pavement near Blythe on the lower Colorado River. [aka desert intaglios]
L4-8 Hill Figures: Figures cut into hillsides to expose the chalk or limestone beneath, mainly in southern England, from the Bronze Age (Uffington) to the 18th–19th-century white horses: Uffington White Horse, Cerne Abbas Giant, Long Man of Wilmington. [aka chalk hill figures;chalk figures]
### L5 Body Art — Axis: tradition.
L5-1 Deq: the facial and hand tattooing of Kurdish, Arab and Amazigh women. [aka dak; Kurdish tattooing]
L5-2 Godna: Tattooing of the tribal communities of central and eastern India (Baiga, Gond, Oraon), also transferred to painting on paper. [aka khoda]
L5-3 Mehndi: Henna body painting of South Asia for weddings and festivals. [aka henna;mehendi]
L5-4 Sak Yant: Thai, Khmer, Lao and Burmese protective yantra tattooing of sacred diagrams and Khom script, applied by monks and ajarn with a steel rod. [aka yantra tattooing]
L5-5 Mentawai Tattooing: The full-body line tattooing (titi) of the Mentawai of Siberut, west of Sumatra. [aka titi;tato Mentawai]
L5-6 Batok: The traditional tattooing of the Philippines, from the Visayan pintados to the Kalinga and Bontoc warrior tattoos. [aka batek;pintados]
L5-7 Irezumi: Japanese tattooing, above all the Edo-period full-body designs inspired by ukiyo-e heroes, with the Okinawan women's hand tattoos (hajichi) and Ainu lip tattoos as kinds. [aka horimono;Japanese tattooing]
L5-8 African Scarification: Cicatrisation and keloid scarification as body art across Africa, with named regional marks: Yoruba facial marks (ila), Nuba and Dinka marks.
L5-9 Kakiniit: Inuit women's facial and body tattooing, sewn or pricked with soot-blackened sinew, revived in Nunavut and Greenland since the 2000s. [aka Inuit tattooing;tunniit]
L5-10 Tā Moko: Māori tattooing, chiselled (uhi) into the skin in curvilinear designs that mark descent and rank: moko on men's faces, moko kauae for women. [aka moko]
L5-11 Polynesian Tattooing: The tattoo traditions of Polynesia worked with toothed combs and a mallet: Samoan peʻa and malu, Marquesan full-body tattoo, Tahitian tatau, Fijian veiqia. [aka tatau;peʻa;malu]
L5-12 Sailor Tattoos: The Western tattoo tradition of sailors and its prison and street derivatives, which grew into the American traditional style of bold outlines and limited colour (Sailor Jerry): swallows, anchors, pin-ups. [aka American traditional tattooing;prison tattooing]
### L6 Floor & Sand Painting — Axis: tradition. Ephemeral designs drawn on the floor or ground in powder, sand or rice paste (rangoli, kolam, Navajo dry painting).
L6-1 Sona: Sand drawings (lusona, pl. sona) of the Chokwe and neighbouring peoples of eastern Angola: continuous lines around a grid of dots, illustrating stories. [aka lusona]
L6-2 Navajo Sandpainting: Dry paintings of coloured sands, pollen and charcoal made by Diné medicine men for healing chants and erased after use, and their permanent versions glued on board for sale since the 1930s. [aka dry painting;iikááh]
L6-3 Corpus Christi Flower Carpets: Carpets of flowers laid on the streets of Polish villages (Spycimierz and others) for the Corpus Christi processions (UNESCO 2021). [aka Flower carpets tradition for Corpus Christi processions]

## M New Media, Installation & Performance — test: time-based, installed or performed work: video, sound, installation, performance, digital and net art. Photographers and artists are kinds of their school or movement.
### M1 Installation, Conceptual & Performance Art — Axis: movement (conceptual art, Fluxus, Arte Povera, land art, performance).
M1-1 Installation Art: Art that makes or transforms a space to be entered or experienced, from the 1960s: Kabakov's The Man Who Flew into Space, Kusama's Infinity Mirror Rooms.
M1-2 Conceptual Art: Art since the mid-1960s in which the idea is the work: Kosuth's One and Three Chairs, Sol LeWitt's Wall Drawings, On Kawara's Date Paintings.
M1-3 Fluxus: The international intermedia network of the 1960s around George Maciunas: event scores, actions and multiples: Yoko Ono's Cut Piece, Fluxkits.
M1-4 Mail Art: Art sent and exchanged through the postal system from the 1960s (Ray Johnson's New York Correspondence School): Johnson's moticos, artistamps. [aka Correspondence art]
M1-5 Arte Povera: The Italian movement (1967) of "poor" materials and process: Pistoletto's Venus of the Rags, Merz's igloos, Kounellis's live horses.
M1-6 Land Art: Art made in and of the landscape from the late 1960s: Smithson's Spiral Jetty, De Maria's Lightning Field, Goldsworthy's works. [aka Earth art;Earthworks;Environmental art]
M1-7 Viennese Actionism: The radical Austrian performance movement of the 1960s: Nitsch's Orgien Mysterien Theater, Schwarzkogler's actions. [aka Wiener Aktionismus]
M1-8 Performance Art: Art made by the artist's live action before an audience, from the 1960s: Abramović's Rhythm 0, Beuys's I Like America and America Likes Me. [aka Body art]
### M2 Video & New Media Art — Axis: medium-led movement (video art, new media and net art).
M2-1 Video Art: Art made with video from the mid-1960s: Nam June Paik's TV Buddha, Bill Viola's The Crossing, Joan Jonas's Vertical Roll.
M2-2 New Media Art: Art made with digital, networked and computational technologies: Cory Arcangel's Super Mario Clouds, Rafael Lozano-Hemmer's Pulse Room. [aka Digital art;Internet art;generative art;computer art;NFT art;crypto art;AI art;pixel art]

PRECEDENCE
P1 Manuscript painting vs book arts: Pictures painted in a manuscript or for an album go to A1 Manuscript & Album Painting when the tradition is studied as painting (Persian, Ottoman, Mughal, Deccan, Arab); the book as an object — its script, illumination ornament and binding — goes to C. An illustrated manuscript is placed by what makes it notable: its pictures → A1, its script or illumination → C.
P2 Tiles: ware vs revetment: A tile is placed by its ware in E Ceramics (Iznik tiles under Iznik Pottery; Kashan lustre tiles under Kashan Lustreware). A revetment assembled on site as a named technique — cut-tile mosaic, zellige, kashi kari, mirror mosaic — is L3, because the work is the wall.
P3 Architectural carving: building vs fragment: Carved stucco, stone and wood that survive as museum fragments go to their material family (D1 carving, I1 woodwork). The building that carries them is K; a named in-situ programme of painting or mosaic is L.
P4 Lustre across bodies: Lustre is a surface technique, not a sub-family: a lustreware is placed by its body (earthenware lustre → E1; fritware lustre → E2) and then by its named ware.
P5 Carved hardstone vessels: A vessel carved from rock crystal, jade or other hardstone is D3 (the carving is the work), not glass or metalwork, even when mounted in metal.
P6 Dynasty is a tag: A dynasty, reign, empire or period (Mamluk, Safavid, Qajar, Akbar's reign) is a period tag, never a node. The node is the tradition the work belongs to in its medium: Mamluk enamelled glass (F3), Mamluk metalwork (G1), Mamluk carpets (H1), Mamluk architecture (K).
P7 Place is a tag: A city, production centre, country or culture label (Iznik, Kashan, Tabriz, 'Iranian', 'Syrian') is a place tag. A centre becomes a kind under a leaf only when the field names a distinct school or ware after it (Herat school, Kashan lustreware, Ushak carpets).
P8 Revivals: A revival built outside the original tradition (Moorish Revival synagogues, Neo-Mamluk, the First National Architectural Movement) is a leaf in K Historicist & National Revivals, never a kind of the style it revives.
P9 Makers: A named maker is a kind under the leaf of the tradition they worked in, linked to their school or atelier where one is recorded. A maker active in two traditions is placed where most of their recorded work sits, with a see-also to the other (Mir Sayyid Ali and Abd al-Samad: Persian schooling, Mughal atelier → Mughal Painting).
P10 Folk and court: A folk or vernacular craft that the field names on its own (suzani, kilim, Berber jewellery) is its own leaf in the medium family; a court or urban tradition of the same medium is a separate leaf. Garment types (abaya, shemagh, ghutra) are not art traditions: they belong to the Possession Taxonomy.
P11 Period style before region: In artist-led cells a painter, sculptor or printmaker goes to the movement or school their work is attested in; with no attested movement, to the period-style leaf of their medium (Baroque Painting, Romantic Painting ...), never to a country.
P12 Tradition per medium: A culture is never a leaf by itself: its leaves are its traditions in each medium (Yoruba sculpture, Yoruba beadwork), so one culture can hold several leaves across families. A culture known for one medium only gets one leaf there.
P13 Archaeological cultures: An archaeological culture known for one dominant medium (Nok terracottas, Jōmon pottery) is a leaf in that medium; its other finds are kinds or see-also, not separate leaves, unless the field names them.
P14 Culture rows: A gathered culture row (Ewe, Mende, Asante) is a kind of the tradition that holds most of its objects when one medium tradition of that people has a leaf; otherwise it is a place tag (P12). A named carving school, provincial painting school or centre (Te Arawa, Murshidabad, Swat) is a kind of its leaf.
P15 Period leaves for artists: With no attested movement, an artist goes to the period-style leaf of their medium by birth year: academic and salon art for those trained in the academies before the modern movements (born before c. 1850), Modern Art (born c. 1850–1944), Contemporary Art (born 1945 or later). The artist's own medium decides, not the medium recorded in the source when it is plainly wrong (a photographer recorded as a painter is a photographer).
P16 Period leaves for architects, designers and photographers: P15 applies to every medium: an architect with no attested style goes to the period architecture leaf of their birth (Neoclassical, Modern, Contemporary Architecture); a designer to the decorative-arts style of their working years (I3: Art Nouveau to Contemporary Design); a photographer to the photographic school of their working years (Pictorialism, Straight, Modernist, Documentary, Contemporary Photography). A designer or architect known first for one medium is placed by that medium.

MODIFIERS (tags and filters, never nodes)
Period and dynasty — a filter on every kind and leaf (Umayyad, Abbasid, Fatimid, Seljuq, Ilkhanid, Mamluk, Timurid, Safavid, Ottoman, Mughal, Qajar ...). Never a node (P6).
Region and place — the 20-region grid of the gathering, plus production centre and country as tags (P7).
Ornament and motif — girih (geometric strapwork), arabesque (islimi), muqarnas, saz leaves, cintamani: a tag across media, never a leaf, since one motif runs through tiles, carpets, metalwork and buildings (open question Q6).
Holding institution and collection — evidence, never a node.
Patron and reign — a tag (Akbar, Shah Tahmasp, Süleyman); a royal atelier is a kind only when it is a recorded workshop (Imperial Mughal atelier, the Ottoman nakkaşhane).
Object type — bowl, ewer, tile, folio, page: an attribute of representative works, not a node. A leaf is a tradition, not an object type, except where the field treats the type as a tradition (astrolabes).
Inscription and script on objects — an attribute; calligraphy as an art (C1) is placed by script.
Subject and genre — portrait, landscape, still life, history painting, genre scene, religious image: a tag on works and kinds, never a node.
Technique and process — oil, tempera, fresco, watercolour, gouache, acrylic; woodcut, engraving, etching, lithography, screen print; daguerreotype, albumen, gelatin silver, colour and black-and-white photography: tags, never nodes (a fresco cycle is Art in Place by its site, an etching is a print by its school).
Abstraction and figuration — abstract art is a tag across movements (Abstract Expressionism, Suprematism, Dansaekhwa …), not a leaf of its own.
Building type — skyscraper, mosque, temple, castle, palace, house, garden pavilion: a tag on architecture kinds; styles are the leaves.
Folk and popular art — a filter that gathers the folk leaves of every medium (Mexican folk art = the Mexican folk leaves in A6, D5, E1, C4, H …).
